# Computational and Statistical Guarantees of the \textit{c}-Rectified flow

**arXiv ID**: 2608.02487v1
**Authors**: Leda Wang, Zhehao Xu, Qiang Liu, Harrison H. Zhou
**Published**: 2026-08-03
**Categories**: stat.ML, cs.LG, math.OC, math.PR, math.ST
**HTML URL**: https://arxiv.org/html/2608.02487v1

## Abstract

Recently, rectified flow has emerged as a fundamental framework for large-scale image generation, powering state-of-the-art systems such as FLUX.1 and Stable Diffusion 3. Despite its remarkable empirical success, the computational and statistical guarantees of iterative rectified flow have remained largely unexplored. We address this problem by studying \textit{c}-rectified flow, a cost-aware class of rectified flow that projects velocity fields onto a gradient class while preserving endpoint marginals. The ordinary rectified flow can fail to recover the optimal transport coupling: in a Gaussian case study, the iteration converges to the optimal coupling if and only if the source and target covariance matrices commute. In contrast, under suitable compactness and uniform-integrability assumptions, iterative \textit{c}-rectified flow always converges to the optimal transport coupling. We further establish quantitative one-step contraction and exponential convergence guarantees under projection-stability assumptions for both quadratic and strongly convex displacement costs. Finally, under a Hölder ball assumption, we develop new minimax-optimal score estimation rates and show that, when combined with iterative \textit{c}-rectified flow, they yield a rate-optimal estimator of the optimal transport for the dimension \(d \ge 3\) and a nearly parametric rate for \(d=1,2\).

## Full Text

Computational and Statistical Guarantees of the c-Rectified flow

## Abstract

Recently, rectified flow has emerged as a fundamental framework for large-scale image generation, powering state-of-the-art systems such as FLUX.1 and Stable Diffusion 3. Despite its remarkable empirical success, the computational and statistical guarantees of iterative rectified flow have remained largely unexplored. We address this problem by studyingc-rectified flow, a cost-aware class of rectified flow that projects velocity fields onto a gradient class while preserving endpoint marginals. The ordinary rectified flow can fail to recover the optimal transport coupling: in a Gaussian case study, the iteration converges to the optimal coupling if and only if the source and target covariance matrices commute. In contrast, under suitable compactness and uniform-integrability assumptions, iterativec-rectified flow always converges to the optimal transport coupling. We further establish quantitative one-step contraction and exponential convergence guarantees under projection-stability assumptions for both quadratic and strongly convex displacement costs. Finally, under a Hölder ball assumption, we develop new minimax-optimal score estimation rates and show that, when combined with iterativec-rectified flow, they yield a rate-optimal estimator of the optimal transport for the dimensiond≥3d\geq 3and a nearly parametric rate ford=1,2d=1,2.convergence rates,

## 1Introduction

Rectified flow, introduced byLiuet al.(2022), is a simple and effective
framework for learning continuous-time transports between distributions. Its
least-squares training objective is readily implemented with neural networks,
while iterative reflow straightens transport trajectories and enables accurate
generation with few discretization steps. Closely related formulations such as
flow matchingLipmanet al.(2023)and stochastic interpolantsAlbergoet al.(2023)have further established regression-based
probability flows as a general framework for generative modeling. The original
rectified-flow experiments demonstrated strong performance in image generation,
image-to-image translation, and domain adaptationLiuet al.(2022); InstaFlow
subsequently used reflow to construct a one-step text-to-image generatorLiuet al.(2024). At a much larger scale, rectified-flow transformers
have become a practical foundation for high-resolution image synthesis: Stable
Diffusion 3 scales this architecture with a multimodal transformerEsseret al.(2024), while the FLUX.1 family places flow matching at the
core of large text-to-image and in-context image-generation systemsBlack Forest Labs (2025). These developments make rectification more than
a device for simplifying simulation and motivate a deeper understanding of the transport
coupling from the procedure. Yet the computational and statistical guarantees of iterative rectified flow have remained largely unexplored.

We study this question in the following statistical setting. Suppose that we observe two
independent samplesX1,…,Xn​∼i.i.d.​P,Y1,…,Yn​∼i.i.d.​Q,X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\overset{\mathrm{i.i.d.}}{\sim}P,\qquad Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n}\overset{\mathrm{i.i.d.}}{\sim}Q,

where the endpoint distributionsPPandQQare unknown. Our goal is to
estimate the optimal transport mapT∗T^{*}fromPPtoQQ, namely the
cost-minimizing measurable map satisfyingT#​P=QT_{\#}P=Q. The
statistical objective is to construct an estimatorT^n\widehat{T}_{n}whoseL2​(P)L^{2}(P)-risk relative toT∗T^{*}attains the optimal rate over the
distribution classes specified later.

In the one-dimensional setting, the connection between rectified flow and optimal
transport is particularly interesting. Under rectifiability assumptions that guarantee
uniqueness of the defining ODE, trajectories issued from two ordered initial
points cannot cross at a common time: otherwise two ODE solutions would pass
through the same space–time point, contradicting uniqueness. The endpoint flow
map is therefore monotone, which immediately implies that it is the one-dimensional optimal
transport map. This noncrossing argument underlies one-step recovery results for
one-dimensional rectified flowBansalet al.(2025); Hertrichet al.(2025); in particular,Hertrichet al.(2025)shows that every rectifiable one-dimensional
coupling is mapped to the optimal coupling by a single rectification step. The
conclusion is intrinsically one-dimensional: in higher dimensions, the
nonintersection of ODE trajectories does not by itself impose the cyclic
monotonicity, or equivalently the cost-dependent gradient structure, that
characterizes optimal transport.

The statistical analysis of rectified flow can be challenging.
The analysis inMenaet al.(2025)studies empirical rectified-flow
estimators and, under some regularity and Hölder smoothness assumptions, establishes a convergence rate that is slower than the minimax-optimal rate for optimal transport estimation but faster than the standard nonparametric rate. This naturally raises the question of whether rectified flow is statistically optimal for estimate the optimal transport.

These computational and statistical results leave three closely related questions. Is
a single step of rectified flow, or its cost-aware analogue, sufficient beyond the
one-dimensional setting? If repeated rectification is necessary, does the
iteration converge to optimal transport, and how fast? Finally, can an
iterative construction attain the
minimax rate for optimal transport map estimation? We answer these questions through
thec-rectified flow ofLiu (2022), which projects each velocity onto the appropriate
cost-dependent gradient class. Under suitable conditions, iterativec-rectified flow converges to optimal transport. Moreover, under additional structural assumptions, we establish quantitative one-step contraction and exponential convergence guarantees. Finally, when combined with score-based estimation of the endpoint
marginals, it can yield a rate-optimal estimator of the optimal transport map.

## 1.1Our Contributions

The main contributions of the paper are as follows.
- •

Vanilla rectified flow can fail to select optimal transport.In a centered Gaussian example, ordinary rectified flow initialized from the
independent coupling produces after one step a deterministic linear
coupling that is fixed by all subsequent reflows. This fixed point is the
Gaussian optimal transport map if and only if the source and target covariance matrices
commute. Thus cost decrease and marginal preservation alone do not guarantee
convergence to optimal transport.
- •

c-rectified flow converges qualitatively to optimal transport.The cost-aware gradient structure inc-rectified flow rules out the
non-optimal fixed points above under compactness and uniform-integrability
assumptions. For quadratic cost, the transport cost along the iterates
converges to the optimal value, and uniqueness of the optimal coupling gives
weak convergence of the couplings. We also prove the analogous qualitative
convergence statement for more general displacement costs.
- •

The convergence can be quantitative.We establish one-step decrease and exponential convergence guarantees. In
the Gaussian setting, the relevant projection mechanism can be computed
explicitly. In a general quadratic setting, Lipschitz regularity, strong
convexity of the Brenier map, and a projection-stability condition imply
geometric decay of the excess transport cost. The same contraction framework
extends beyond quadratic loss to strongly convex displacement costs.
- •

New minimax score rates yield a rate-optimal map estimator.For the multivariate Gaussian-ratio model, we construct an aggregate score
estimator and prove matching upper and lower bounds𝔯n,d​(t)\mathfrak{r}_{n,d}(t)across the
low-, intermediate-, and large-noise regimes. These new minimax-optimal
score-estimation rates lead to score-based marginal estimators with explicit
Wasserstein ratesϵn,d\epsilon_{n,d}. Combining these marginal estimators
with iterativec-rectified flow yields a rate-optimal estimator of
the optimal transport map.

Conceptually, the paper connects three viewpoints that are often studied
separately: classical optimal transport, iterative flow-based transport
algorithms, and statistical estimation from data. The main message is thatc-rectified flow is not merely a heuristic cost-reduction procedure:
with the right cost-aware gradient structure, it can serve as a principled
algorithmic bridge from estimated marginals to optimal transport maps.

## 1.2Related Works

Recent work on rectified flow and flow matching provides the conceptual
starting point for our analysis. The original rectified flow formulationLiuet al.(2022), together with the marginal-preservingc-rectified flow variant inLiu (2022), shows that
iterative rectification can decrease transport costs while preserving endpoint
marginals. Closely related frameworks, including flow matchingLipmanet al.(2023)and stochastic interpolantsAlbergoet al.(2023), place these methods in a broader
regression-based framework for learning transport dynamics between
distributions. Our work focuses instead on the limiting coupling generated by
rectification and on conditions under which this coupling converges to
classical optimal transport.

The closest strand of prior work examines when vanilla rectified flow recovers optimal
transport. The analysis inBansalet al.(2025)gives Wasserstein error
bounds for the sampling distribution of rectified flow and relates
discretization error to a stronger notion of straightness; in one dimension
with a Gaussian source, it also identifies regimes in which one-step rectified
flow recovers the Monge map. A complementary statistical analysis is given inMenaet al.(2025), which studies existence, uniqueness, regularity,
and limit distributions for empirical rectified-flow estimators. In the
Gaussian setting, that work shows that the rectified map agrees with the
optimal transport map in one step precisely when the source and target
covariance matrices commute. These results identify special cases in which
rectified flow is compatible with optimal transport. Our focus is on iterative
convergence guarantees and contraction mechanisms forc-rectified
flow.

A sharper structural characterization of the relation between rectified flow
and optimal transport is developed inHertrichet al.(2025). That work
derives explicit formulas in Gaussian and Gaussian-mixture settings and proves
that every rectifiable one-dimensional coupling is mapped to the optimal
transport coupling after a single rectification step. It also shows that
several previously claimed rectifiability statements require stronger
regularity assumptions than had been emphasized. Rather than assuming
rectifiability of the initial coupling, we study the behavior of the iterative
procedure and identify conditions under which its cost decreases toward the
optimal value.

Another neighboring line improves the coupling or path geometry without
directly proving convergence to the globally optimal transport plan.
Minibatch optimal transport and multisample flow-matching methodsTonget al.(2024); Pooladianet al.(2023a)use nontrivial couplings to
obtain straighter flows, lower transport cost, and lower training variance.
These methods are useful for constructing improved training couplings, but
they typically rely on batch-level approximations and do not by themselves
identify the population limit as the optimal coupling. Direct neural optimal transport solvers
take a different route by parameterizing Brenier potentialsMakkuvaet al.(2020), while plug-in methods recover the map from estimated
marginalsManoleet al.(2024). Our focus is complementary: we analyze
whether rectification itself is a convergent algorithm for classical optimal
transport.

The statistical estimation of optimal transport has been studied from several
complementary perspectives. A foundational line of work analyzes convergence
of empirical measures in Wasserstein distance. Nonasymptotic rates under
moment conditions were established byFournier and Guillin (2015), and sharp
asymptotic and finite-sample rates were further developed byWeed and Bach (2019). These results clarify the dimension dependence of
empirical optimal transport estimation and show that the empirical plug-in estimator can
exhibit the curse of dimensionality. In high-dimensional regimes, the
empirical Wasserstein distance typically converges much more slowly than
parametric rates unless additional structure or smoothing is
imposed. A related inferential literature studies optimal transport costs as statistical
functionals, including discrete settingsSommerfeld and Munk (2018)and
continuous settingsdel Barrio and Loubes (2019). These works show that transport
costs can be used for statistical inference, but their limiting behavior is
more delicate than that of smooth integral functionals. Our statistical
estimation results use Wasserstein control of the marginals as an input to
transport-map estimation, rather than studying inference for the optimal transport cost
itself.

Minimax and plug-in estimation form another important part of the statistical
optimal transport literature. For density estimation under Wasserstein loss,Niles-Weed and Berthet (2022)shows that minimax rates over smooth density
classes depend crucially on whether the density is bounded away from zero. For
optimal transport maps,Manoleet al.(2024)studies plug-in estimators
under smoothness and regularity assumptions on the Brenier map and establishes
minimax optimal rates. Those results also give central limit theorems for
plug-in estimators ofW22​(μ,ν)W_{2}^{2}(\mu,\nu)in sufficiently smooth regimes.
The workDivolet al.(2025a)develops rates for optimal transport map estimation in
general function spaces under assumptions expressed through metric entropy,
thereby moving beyond classical Hölder-type smoothness classes. These
results show that optimal transport maps can be estimated at nontrivial rates under suitable
structural assumptions on the underlying measures and maps. Our plug-in
analysis is complementary: it combines marginal estimation with thec-rectified-flow convergence theory developed in this paper.

Additional related work on flow-based distribution estimation,
Schrödinger bridges, score-based sampling, and entropic regularization is
collected in AppendixA.

## 1.3Organization

The remainder of the paper is organized as follows. Section1.2reviews related work on rectified flow and statistical optimal transport, with
additional adjacent literature collected in
AppendixA. Section2introduces the
background on optimal transport, rectified flow, andc-rectified flow.
Section3.1gives the Gaussian counterexample showing that
ordinary rectified flow can stabilize at a non-optimal coupling.
Section2.5proves qualitative convergence
of iterativec-rectified flow. Section3.2studies convergence speed for Gaussian couplings, and
Section3.3develops a general contraction
criterion for the quadratic cost. Section5.2extends the contraction analysis beyond quadratic loss.
Section4.1constructs the score-based marginal estimator and
derives its Wasserstein risk. Finally, Section4.2combines marginal estimation withc-rectified flow to obtain a
rate-optimal estimator of the optimal transport map.

## 2Background

## 2.2Statistical Property for Optimal Transport

The statistical problem considered later is to estimate the marginals and then
transport between the estimated distributions. Given samples from unknown
marginalsPPandQQ, we will construct estimatorsP^n\widehat{P}_{n}andQ^n\widehat{Q}_{n}and measure their quality in Wasserstein distance. These
marginal errors are then propagated to optimal-transport map error through
stability estimates. Detailed background on empirical OT rates and minimax OT
map estimation is deferred to AppendixB.

## 2.3Rectified Flow

Rectified flow starts from a coupling(X0,X1)(X_{0},X_{1})ofPPandQQand the linear
interpolationXt=(1−t)​X0+t​X1X_{t}=(1-t)X_{0}+tX_{1}. It fits the conditional velocity field byminv​∫01𝔼​‖X1−X0−vt​(Xt)‖2​dt,\min_{v}\int_{0}^{1}\mathbb{E}\|X_{1}-X_{0}-v_{t}(X_{t})\|^{2}\mathrm{d}t,(4)

whose minimizer isvt𝑿​(z)=𝔼​[X1−X0∣Xt=z]v_{t}^{{\bm{X}}}(z)=\mathbb{E}[X_{1}-X_{0}\mid X_{t}=z]

forPXtP_{X_{t}}-a.e.zz. The rectified flow associated with𝑿{\bm{X}}is the ODEd​Zt=vt𝑿​(Zt)​d​t,Z0∼P.\mathrm{d}Z_{t}=v_{t}^{{\bm{X}}}(Z_{t})\mathrm{d}t,\qquad Z_{0}\sim P.(5)

A process is rectifiable when this velocity exists, is locally bounded, and the
ODE is well posed. We write(Z0,Z1)=𝚁𝚎𝚌𝚝𝚒𝚏𝚢​((X0,X1))(Z_{0},Z_{1})=\mathtt{Rectify}((X_{0},X_{1}))for the endpoint
coupling produced by one rectification step.

## 2.4c-Rectified Flow

Rectified flow is not tied to a prescribed transport cost. The cost-dependent
variant studied in this paper replaces arbitrary velocity fields by the
cost-compatible gradient field generated by the convex conjugate ofcc.
Throughout,c:ℝd→ℝc:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}is differentiable and strictly convex,c∗c^{*}denotes
its convex conjugate, andmc​(x,y)=c​(x)+c∗​(y)−⟨x,y⟩=bc​(x;∇c∗​(y)),m_{c}(x,y)=c(x)+c^{*}(y)-\langle x,y\rangle=b_{c}(x;\nabla c^{*}(y)),

wherebcb_{c}is the Bregman divergence ofcc.

For a time-differentiable process𝑿{\bm{X}}, defineL𝑿,c(f):=∫01𝔼[mc(X˙s,∇fs(Xs))]ds.L_{{\bm{X}},c}(f):=\int_{0}^{1}\mathbb{E}\left[m_{c}(\dot{X}_{s},\nabla f_{s}(X_{s}))\right]\mathrm{d}s.

Thecc-rectified flow is the process𝒁=c-​𝚁𝚎𝚌𝚝𝚒𝚏𝚢​(𝑿){\bm{Z}}=\text{c-}\mathtt{Rectify}({\bm{X}})solvingd​Zs=∇c∗​(∇fs𝑿,c​(Zs))​d​s,s∈[0,1],Z0=X0,\mathrm{d}Z_{s}=\nabla c^{*}(\nabla f^{{\bm{X}},c}_{s}(Z_{s}))\mathrm{d}s,\qquad s\in[0,1],\qquad Z_{0}=X_{0},(6)

wheref𝑿,cf^{{\bm{X}},c}minimizesL𝑿,cL_{{\bm{X}},c}. A process iscc-rectifiable if the

bounded, and (6) admits a unique solution.

Given an endpoint coupling(X0,X1)(X_{0},X_{1}), we apply this construction to its
linear interpolation and write(Z0,Z1)=c-​𝚁𝚎𝚌𝚝𝚒𝚏𝚢​(X0,X1)(Z_{0},Z_{1})=\text{c-}\mathtt{Rectify}(X_{0},X_{1}). Iterating this map
gives the coupling sequence used throughout the paper.Algorithm 1cc-rectified Coupling1:Initialize(Z0(0),Z1(0))(Z_{0}^{(0)},Z_{1}^{(0)})such thatℒ​(Z0(0))=P\mathcal{L}(Z_{0}^{(0)})=P,ℒ​(Z1(0))=Q\mathcal{L}(Z_{1}^{(0)})=Q.2:fork=1,2,…,Kk=1,2,\ldots,Kdo3:(Z0(k),Z1(k))=c-​𝚁𝚎𝚌𝚝𝚒𝚏𝚢​(Z0(k−1),Z1(k−1))(Z^{(k)}_{0},Z^{(k)}_{1})=\text{c-}\mathtt{Rectify}(Z^{(k-1)}_{0},Z^{(k-1)}_{1}).4:endfor5:return(Z0(K),Z1(K))(Z^{(K)}_{0},Z^{(K)}_{1}).

Additional background facts and earlier results used in this section are
collected in AppendixB.

With the basic objects now fixed, we turn to the population behavior of the
iteration. The first question is qualitative: if the marginals are known and
thecc-rectified update is applied repeatedly, does the resulting coupling
approach the optimal transport coupling?

## 2.5The Convergence ofc-Rectified Flow

This subsection records the basic convergence guarantee forc-rectified flow. The main point is that the additional gradient structure imposed by thec-rectified objective rules out non-optimal limiting couplings under suitable compactness and integrability assumptions. We first present the quadratic cost, where the connection with classical optimal transport is most transparent.

## 2.5.1Quadratic Loss

We first specialize to the quadratic costc​(x)=‖x‖22c(x)=\tfrac{\|x\|^{2}}{2}. Below we always assumeP,Q∈𝒫2​(ℝd)P,Q\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}). In this case,
for allx,y∈ℝdx,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d},c∗​(x)=c​(x)=‖x‖22,∇c∗​(x)=∇c​(x)=id,mc​(x,y)=‖x−y‖22.\displaystyle c^{*}(x)=c(x)=\frac{\|x\|^{2}}{2},\qquad\nabla c^{*}(x)=\nabla c(x)=\mathrm{id},\qquad m_{c}(x,y)=\frac{\|x-y\|^{2}}{2}.

Thus, in the quadratic case, thec-rectified objective measures the squared deviation between the displacementZ1−Z0Z_{1}-Z_{0}and a gradient velocity field along the straight interpolation. This should be compared with ordinary rectified flow, where the minimizing velocity is an arbitrary conditional expectation. The restriction to gradient fields is precisely the structure compatible with Brenier’s theorem, and it is this restriction that allows the iteration to select the optimal coupling.

The following theorem makes this intuition precise.

## Theorem 1(Convergence ofc-rectified flow).

Let(Z0(0),Z1(0))=(X0,X1)(Z_{0}^{(0)},Z_{1}^{(0)})=(X_{0},X_{1})be an initial coupling ofPPandQQ, and{𝐙(k)=(Z0(k),Z1(k))}k=1∞\{{\bm{Z}}^{(k)}=(Z^{(k)}_{0},Z^{(k)}_{1})\}_{k=1}^{\infty}be a sequence of couplings of(P,Q)(P,Q)defined recursively by(Z0(k),Z1(k))=c-​𝚁𝚎𝚌𝚝𝚒𝚏𝚢​(Z0(k−1),Z1(k−1))(Z^{(k)}_{0},Z^{(k)}_{1})=\text{c-}\mathtt{Rectify}(Z^{(k-1)}_{0},Z^{(k-1)}_{1}).
For eachk≥1k\geq 1,
defineZt(k)=t​Z1(k)+(1−t)​Z0(k)Z_{t}^{(k)}=tZ_{1}^{(k)}+(1-t)Z_{0}^{(k)},t∈[0,1]t\in[0,1],
and letf⋅𝐙(k)​(⋅):(x,t)↦ft𝐙(k)​(x)f_{\cdot}^{{\bm{Z}}^{(k)}}(\cdot):(x,t)\mapsto f_{t}^{{\bm{Z}}^{(k)}}(x)be a minimizer ofL𝒁(k)​(f)=∫01𝔼​[‖Z1(k)−Z0(k)−∇fs​(Zs(k))‖2]​ds.L_{{\bm{Z}}^{(k)}}(f)=\int_{0}^{1}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|Z_{1}^{(k)}-Z_{0}^{(k)}-\nabla f_{s}\bigl(Z_{s}^{(k)}\bigr)\right\|^{2}\right]\mathrm{d}s.

Suppose moreover that the following hold.
- •

There exists anα>0\alpha>0, such that for everyR>0R>0, there exists a constantCR>0C_{R}>0,
for allk≥1k\geq 1,‖f𝒁(k)‖C2+α,1+α​(B​(0,R)¯×[0,1])≤CR;\|f^{{\bm{Z}}^{(k)}}\|_{C^{2+\alpha,1+\alpha}(\overline{B(0,R)}\times[0,1])}\leq C_{R};
- •

There exist a constantC>0C>0and a nonnegative measurable functiong:ℝd→[0,∞)g:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to[0,\infty)such that‖∇ft𝒁(k)​(x)‖≤C​g​(x)\|\nabla f_{t}^{{\bm{Z}}^{(k)}}(x)\|\leq Cg(x)for all(x,t)∈ℝd×[0,1](x,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,1]and allk≥1k\geq 1, and the family{∫01g​(Zt(k))2​dt}k=1∞\left\{\int_{0}^{1}g\bigl(Z_{t}^{(k)}\bigr)^{2}\mathrm{d}t\right\}_{k=1}^{\infty}is uniformly integrable.

Denote(Z0∗,Z1∗)(Z_{0}^{*},Z_{1}^{*})as the unique optimal coupling of(P,Q)(P,Q), thenlimk→∞𝔼​‖Z1(k)−Z0(k)‖2=𝔼​‖Z0∗−Z1∗‖2=W22​(P,Q),(Z0(k),Z1(k))⇒(Z0∗,Z1∗).\displaystyle\lim_{k\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\|Z^{(k)}_{1}-Z^{(k)}_{0}\|^{2}=\mathbb{E}\|Z_{0}^{*}-Z_{1}^{*}\|^{2}=W_{2}^{2}(P,Q),\qquad(Z^{(k)}_{0},Z^{(k)}_{1})\Rightarrow(Z_{0}^{*},Z_{1}^{*}).

The proof of Theorem1is given in
AppendixD.
The theorem identifies the population target of the iteration: under the stated
regularity assumptions, repeatedc-rectification drives the quadratic
transport cost down toW22​(P,Q)W_{2}^{2}(P,Q)and the couplings converge weakly to the
Brenier coupling. This qualitative result does not yet give a rate, nor does
it explain why the gradient restriction is essential. The next section
addresses both points by contrasting ordinary rectified flow with thec-rectified update.

## 3Computational Guarantee ofc-Rectified Flow

The convergence theorem above describes the limiting population behavior ofc-rectified flow. We now study finite-iteration behavior. We first
show that ordinary rectified flow can fail even in a centered Gaussian model,
and then prove quantitative contraction estimates for thec-rectified
update.

## 3.1The Failure of Rectified Flow: Gaussian Case Study

For centered Gaussian marginals, ordinary rectified flow recovers the optimal transport coupling if and only if the two covariance matrices commute. Indeed, commuting covariances yield the optimal map after one rectification step, as shown inHertrichet al.(2025); when the covariances do not commute, the first step produces a non-optimal deterministic coupling that is fixed by all subsequent reflow steps.
The following statements make this obstruction explicit: the first two record the Gaussian reflow dynamics, while the proposition gives the resulting non-convergence conclusion.

## Proposition 1(Jointly Gaussian Coupling Velocity).

Let(X0,X1)(X_{0},X_{1})be a centered jointly Gaussian coupling inℝd×ℝd\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}, withX0∼𝒩​(0,Σ1)X_{0}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma_{1}),X1∼𝒩​(0,Σ2)X_{1}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma_{2}),Σ01:=Cov⁡(X0,X1)\Sigma_{01}:=\operatorname{Cov}(X_{0},X_{1}).
For eacht∈[0,1]t\in[0,1], define the straight couplingXt=(1−t)​X0+t​X1X_{t}=(1-t)X_{0}+tX_{1}withΣt:=Var​(Xt)=(1−t)2​Σ1+t2​Σ2+t​(1−t)​(Σ01+Σ01⊤)\Sigma_{t}:=\mathrm{Var}(X_{t})=(1-t)^{2}\Sigma_{1}+t^{2}\Sigma_{2}+t(1-t)(\Sigma_{01}+\Sigma_{01}^{\top}).
Then the velocity fieldvt​(x):=𝔼​[X1−X0∣Xt=x]v_{t}(x):=\mathbb{E}[X_{1}-X_{0}\mid X_{t}=x]is linear inxxand is given byvt​(x)=[t​(Σ2−Σ01)+(1−t)​(Σ01⊤−Σ1)]​Σt−1​x.v_{t}(x)=\Bigl[t(\Sigma_{2}-\Sigma_{01})+(1-t)(\Sigma_{01}^{\top}-\Sigma_{1})\Bigr]\Sigma_{t}^{-1}x.(7)

## Lemma 1(Endpoint of one-step rectified flow).

LetΣ1,Σ2∈ℝd×d\Sigma_{1},\Sigma_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}be symmetric positive definite, and consider the linear ODE∂tZt=(t​Σ2−(1−t)​Σ1)​Σt−1​Zt,Z0=z∈ℝd,\partial_{t}Z_{t}=\bigl(t\Sigma_{2}-(1-t)\Sigma_{1}\bigr)\Sigma_{t}^{-1}Z_{t},\qquad Z_{0}=z\in\mathbb{R}^{d},

whereΣt=(1−t)2​Σ1+t2​Σ2\Sigma_{t}=(1-t)^{2}\Sigma_{1}+t^{2}\Sigma_{2}.
ThenZt=Σ11/2​((1−t)2​I+t2​Σ1−1/2​Σ2​Σ1−1/2)1/2​Σ1−1/2​z.Z_{t}=\Sigma_{1}^{1/2}\Bigl((1-t)^{2}I+t^{2}\Sigma_{1}^{-1/2}\Sigma_{2}\Sigma_{1}^{-1/2}\Bigr)^{1/2}\Sigma_{1}^{-1/2}z.

In particular,Z1=T​zZ_{1}=Tz,
whereT:=Σ11/2​(Σ1−1/2​Σ2​Σ1−1/2)1/2​Σ1−1/2.T:=\Sigma_{1}^{1/2}\Bigl(\Sigma_{1}^{-1/2}\Sigma_{2}\Sigma_{1}^{-1/2}\Bigr)^{1/2}\Sigma_{1}^{-1/2}.

## Lemma 2(Reflow of a deterministic linear coupling).

LetX0∼𝒩​(0,Σ1)X_{0}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma_{1})and defineX1=L​X0X_{1}=LX_{0}for an arbitrary invertible matrixL∈ℝd×dL\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}such thatX1∼𝒩​(0,Σ2)X_{1}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma_{2}).
Then the velocity
is given byvt​(x)=(L−I)​((1−t)​I+t​L)−1​xv_{t}(x)=(L-I)\bigl((1-t)I+tL\bigr)^{-1}x,
and the corresponding ODE∂tZt=(L−I)​((1−t)​I+t​L)−1​Zt\partial_{t}Z_{t}=(L-I)\bigl((1-t)I+tL\bigr)^{-1}Z_{t}

has the explicit solutionZt=((1−t)​I+t​L)​Z0Z_{t}=\bigl((1-t)I+tL\bigr)Z_{0}.
In particular, one reflow step preserves the coupling mapLL.

## Proposition 2(Non-convergence to optimal transport).

For rectified flow on the Gaussian measures𝒩​(0,Σ1)\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma_{1})and𝒩​(0,Σ2)\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma_{2}), initialized with the independent coupling, the first step produces the deterministic linear couplingX1=T​X0,T=Σ11/2​(Σ1−1/2​Σ2​Σ1−1/2)1/2​Σ1−1/2.X_{1}=TX_{0},\qquad T=\Sigma_{1}^{1/2}\Bigl(\Sigma_{1}^{-1/2}\Sigma_{2}\Sigma_{1}^{-1/2}\Bigr)^{1/2}\Sigma_{1}^{-1/2}.

Every subsequent step preserves this coupling.

Moreover, letTOT:=Σ1−1/2​(Σ11/2​Σ2​Σ11/2)1/2​Σ1−1/2T_{\mathrm{OT}}:=\Sigma_{1}^{-1/2}\Bigl(\Sigma_{1}^{1/2}\Sigma_{2}\Sigma_{1}^{1/2}\Bigr)^{1/2}\Sigma_{1}^{-1/2}

be the Gaussian optimal transport map. ThenT=TOT⟺Σ1​Σ2=Σ2​Σ1.T=T_{\mathrm{OT}}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\Sigma_{1}\Sigma_{2}=\Sigma_{2}\Sigma_{1}.

Consequently, ifΣ1\Sigma_{1}andΣ2\Sigma_{2}do not commute, rectified flow stabilizes after one step at a suboptimal coupling and does not recover the optimal transport map.

Proofs of Proposition1,
Lemma1, Lemma2, and
Proposition2are given in
AppendixC.
This failure is a population-level obstruction rather than a statistical or
numerical artifact. It motivates the quantitative analysis below: in the same
Gaussian setting, thec-rectified update contracts the excess
quadratic transport cost.

## 3.2Convergence Rates forc-Rectified Flow with Gaussian Marginals

In this section, we quantify the convergence rate of thec-rectified flow in the Gaussian transport setting. The previous convergence theorem shows that the iterates approach the optimal coupling under compactness and integrability assumptions, but it does not provide an explicit rate. For Gaussian marginals, the dynamics admit a more concrete description: the velocity fields remain linear, the gradient projection can be computed through a Gaussian Helmholtz decomposition, and the decrease in transport cost can be related to the non-gradient component of the initial velocity.

The main result is a one-step contraction estimate for the excess quadratic transport cost. The contraction is driven by the rotational part of the transport map. More precisely, writing a linear transport asT=T∗​UT=T^{*}U, whereT∗T^{*}is the Gaussian optimal transport map andUUisΣ1\Sigma_{1}-orthogonal, the deviation from optimality is encoded byU−IU-I. A spectral gap excluding eigenvalues ofUUnear−1-1ensures that the divergence-free component of the velocity controls this deviation, and hence that one step ofc-rectified flow removes a fixed fraction of the excess cost.

## 3.2.1One Step Contraction

LetΣ1,Σ2∈ℝd×d\Sigma_{1},\Sigma_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}be symmetric positive definite andP=𝒩​(0,Σ1)P=\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma_{1}),Q=𝒩​(0,Σ2)Q=\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma_{2}).

## Theorem 2.

LetX0∼PX_{0}\sim P,X1=T​X0X_{1}=TX_{0}, whereTTis a (linear) transport map fromPPtoQQ. Then there exists aΣ1\Sigma_{1}-orthogonal matrixUUsuch thatT=T∗​UT=T^{*}U. Supposedist​(Spec​(U),−1)≥γ>0\mathrm{dist}(\mathrm{Spec}(U),-1)\geq\gamma>0, then this coupling(X0,X1)(X_{0},X_{1})is c-rectifiable. Denote(Z0,Z1)=c-​𝚁𝚎𝚌𝚝𝚒𝚏𝚢​((X0,X1))(Z_{0},Z_{1})=\text{c-}\mathtt{Rectify}((X_{0},X_{1})). Then𝔼​‖Z1−Z0‖2−W22​(P,Q)≤(1−γ2​c)​(𝔼​‖X1−X0‖2−W22​(P,Q)).\mathbb{E}\|Z_{1}-Z_{0}\|^{2}-W_{2}^{2}(P,Q)\leq(1-\gamma^{2}c)\left(\mathbb{E}\|X_{1}-X_{0}\|^{2}-W_{2}^{2}(P,Q)\right).

The constantc∈(0,1/4)c\in(0,1/4)depends only on the spectra ofΣ1\Sigma_{1}andΣ2\Sigma_{2}.

## Corollary 1(One-stepc-rectified flow cannot recover OT unlessT=T∗T=T^{*}).

LetL2​(P;ℝd)=𝒢0⊕𝒮0L^{2}(P;\mathbb{R}^{d})={\mathcal{G}}_{0}\oplus{\mathcal{S}}_{0}be the orthogonal decomposition into gradient fields andpp-divergence-free fields at timet=0t=0.
Ifproj𝒢0⁡(T​x)=T∗​x\operatorname{proj}_{{\mathcal{G}}_{0}}(Tx)=T^{*}x,
thenT=T∗T=T^{*}.
Equivalently, ifT≠T∗T\neq T^{*}, thenproj𝒢0⁡(T​x)≠T∗​x\operatorname{proj}_{{\mathcal{G}}_{0}}(Tx)\neq T^{*}x.
In particular, one-step c-rectified flow cannot already recover the optimal transport map unless the original transport is itself optimal.

The proof of Theorem2and Corollary1are given in
AppendixE.
The theorem shows that, once the rotational component of the current linear
transport is separated from the Gaussian optimal transport map, thec-rectified projection removes a fixed fraction of the excess cost.
The spectral gap condition excludes the degenerate case in which this
rotational component is nearly invisible to the gradient projection.

## 3.2.2Iterating the One-step Contraction

The one-step contraction can now be iterated as long as the spectral gap condition remains uniform along the sequence of linear transport maps generated byc-rectified flow. This yields an exponential decay rate for the excess transport cost.

## Theorem 3(Exponential convergence of iterativec-rectified flow).

Let(Z0(0),Z1(0))=(X0,X1)(Z_{0}^{(0)},Z_{1}^{(0)})=(X_{0},X_{1})be any centered jointly Gaussian initial coupling ofP=𝒩​(0,Σ1)P=\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma_{1}),Q=𝒩​(0,Σ2)Q=\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma_{2}),
and for eachk≥0k\geq 0, let(Z0(k+1),Z1(k+1)):=c-​𝚁𝚎𝚌𝚝𝚒𝚏𝚢​(Z0(k),Z1(k)).(Z_{0}^{(k+1)},Z_{1}^{(k+1)}):=\text{c-}\mathtt{Rectify}(Z_{0}^{(k)},Z_{1}^{(k)}).

Then there exists a constantc>0c>0that depends only on(Z0(0),Z1(0))(Z_{0}^{(0)},Z_{1}^{(0)}), such that the excess transport cost decays geometrically:𝔼​‖Z1(k)−Z0(k)‖2−W22​(P,Q)≤e−c​(k−1)​(𝔼​‖X1−X0‖2−W22​(P,Q)).\mathbb{E}\left\|Z_{1}^{(k)}-Z_{0}^{(k)}\right\|^{2}-W_{2}^{2}(P,Q)\leq e^{-c(k-1)}\Big(\mathbb{E}\|X_{1}-X_{0}\|^{2}-W_{2}^{2}(P,Q)\Big).

The proof of Theorem3is also given in
AppendixE.
The Gaussian calculation gives a fully explicit contraction mechanism. We next
abstract the part of the argument that does not rely on Gaussian algebra: a
one-step decrease follows when the non-optimal component of the current map has
a stable projection onto divergence-free directions.

## 3.3A General Contraction Criterion forc-Rectified Flow

LetPPandQQbe probability measures onℝd\mathbb{R}^{d}, and assume thatPPis absolutely continuous with densitypp. LetT:ℝd→ℝdT:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}be a transport map such thatT#​P=QT_{\#}P=Q, and
letT∗T^{*}denote the unique optimal transport map fromPPtoQQwith
respect to the quadratic cost.

LetX0∼PX_{0}\sim Pand setX1=T​(X0)X_{1}=T(X_{0}). We study the one-stepc-rectified coupling given by(Z0,Z1)=c-​𝚁𝚎𝚌𝚝𝚒𝚏𝚢​((X0,T​(X0)))(Z_{0},Z_{1})=\text{c-}\mathtt{Rectify}((X_{0},T(X_{0}))).
The goal of this section is to give a general criterion under which this step
contracts the excess quadratic transport cost. The key structural condition is
a projection stability assumption: the deviationT−T∗T-T^{*}must have a
nontrivial component in thepp-divergence-free subspace.

Consider the linear interpolationTt=t​T+(1−t)​idT_{t}=tT+(1-t)\mathrm{id}for allt∈[0,1]t\in[0,1].
We first record a simple local-in-time invertibility estimate, which allows us
to define the Eulerian velocity field along this deterministic interpolation.

## Proposition 3(Invertibility ofTtT_{t}for smalltt).

Assume thatTTisLL-Lipschitz,
then for every0≤t<(1+L)−10\leq t<{(1+L)^{-1}},TtT_{t}is injective and bi-Lipschitz onto its image.
In particular,Tt−1T_{t}^{-1}is well-defined onTt​(ℝd)T_{t}(\mathbb{R}^{d})and(1−(1+L)​t)−1(1-(1+L)t)^{-1}-Lipschitz.

AppendixF.

By Proposition3, for everyt<(2+2​L)−1t<(2+2L)^{-1}the inverse mapTt−1T_{t}^{-1}is well-defined onTt​(ℝd)T_{t}(\mathbb{R}^{d}), and hence in particular on the support ofPt=(Tt)#​PP_{t}=(T_{t})_{\#}P. Therefore we may define the velocity fieldvt=(T−id)∘Tt−1on​Tt​(ℝd).v_{t}=(T-\mathrm{id})\circ T_{t}^{-1}\qquad\text{on }T_{t}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

This definition aligns with the marginal flow velocityvt​(x)=𝔼​[X1−X0|Xt=x]v_{t}(x)=\mathbb{E}[X_{1}-X_{0}|X_{t}=x]because the coupling is deterministic.

Letptp_{t}denote the probability density of the interpolantXt=Tt​(X)X_{t}=T_{t}(X), and letppdenote the density ofPP. We define the subspace of divergence-free vector fields with respect toptp_{t}as𝒮t:={h∈L2​(Pt;ℝd):∇⋅(pt​h)=0​in the weak sense}.\displaystyle{\mathcal{S}}_{t}:=\left\{h\in L^{2}(P_{t};\mathbb{R}^{d}):\nabla\cdot(p_{t}h)=0\text{ in the weak sense}\right\}.(8)

Equivalently,h∈𝒮th\in{\mathcal{S}}_{t}if and only if−∫ℝdφ​∇⋅(pt​h)​dx=∫ℝd⟨h,∇φ⟩​pt​dx=0∀φ∈Cc∞​(ℝd).-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varphi\nabla\cdot(p_{t}h)\mathrm{d}x=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\langle h,\nabla\varphi\rangle p_{t}\mathrm{d}x=0\qquad\forall\varphi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

We also let𝒢t:={∇φ:φ∈Cc∞​(ℝd)}¯L2​(Pt;ℝd),\displaystyle{\mathcal{G}}_{t}:=\overline{\{\nabla\varphi:\varphi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\}}^{\,L^{2}(P_{t};\mathbb{R}^{d})},(9)

denote the closure of gradient fields inL2​(Pt;ℝd)L^{2}(P_{t};\mathbb{R}^{d}).
By this weak formulation,𝒮t=𝒢t⟂{\mathcal{S}}_{t}={\mathcal{G}}_{t}^{\perp},
henceL2​(Pt;ℝd)=𝒢t⊕𝒮tL^{2}(P_{t};\mathbb{R}^{d})={\mathcal{G}}_{t}\oplus{\mathcal{S}}_{t}.
Our analysis proceeds in the weighted Hilbert spaceL2​(P;ℝd)L^{2}(P;\mathbb{R}^{d})equipped with the inner product⟨a,b⟩P=∫ℝda​(x)⊤​b​(x)​p​(x)​dx\langle a,b\rangle_{P}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}a(x)^{\top}b(x)p(x)\mathrm{d}x.

## 3.3.1Assumptions and Main Results

We impose three assumptions. The first controls the interpolation and the
transported test fields. The second converts control of‖T−T∗‖P2\|T-T^{*}\|_{P}^{2}into
control of the excess quadratic transport cost. The third is the structural
projection stability condition that drives the contraction estimate.

## Assumption 1(Lipschitz regularity).

The mapT∈C1T\in C^{1}isLL-Lipschitz.

## Assumption 2(Strong convexity of the Brenier potential).

The optimal transport mapT∗T^{*}is generated by aλ\lambda-strongly convex Brenier potentialφ\varphi, namelyT∗=∇φ0T^{*}=\nabla\varphi_{0}and∇2φ0⪰λ​Id\nabla^{2}\varphi_{0}\succeq\lambda I_{d}.

## Assumption 3(Projection stability).

There exists a constantc>0c>0such that‖proj𝒮0⁡(T−T∗)‖P2≥h​‖T−T∗‖P2,\|\operatorname{proj}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{0}}(T-T^{*})\|_{P}^{2}\geq h\|T-T^{*}\|_{P}^{2},

where𝒮0={u∈L2​(P;ℝd):∇⋅(p​u)=0​weakly}{\mathcal{S}}_{0}=\{u\in L^{2}(P;\mathbb{R}^{d}):\nabla\cdot(pu)=0\text{ weakly}\}.

Assumption3requires a fixed fraction of the deviationT−T∗T-T^{*}to lie in thePP-divergence-free subspace. Under these
assumptions, one step ofc-rectified flow removes a definite fraction
of this deviation.

## Theorem 4(One-step decrease of quadratic cost).

LetX0∼PX_{0}\sim P,X1=T​(X0)X_{1}=T(X_{0}), and suppose that the coupling(X0,X1)(X_{0},X_{1})is c-rectifiable. Let(Z0,Z1)=c-​𝚁𝚎𝚌𝚝𝚒𝚏𝚢​(X0,X1)(Z_{0},Z_{1})=\text{c-}\mathtt{Rectify}(X_{0},X_{1}).
Then under Assumptions1–3,𝔼​‖T​(X0)−X0‖2−𝔼​‖Z1−Z0‖2≥h8​(L+1)​‖T−T∗‖P2.\mathbb{E}\left\|T(X_{0})-X_{0}\right\|^{2}-\mathbb{E}\left\|Z_{1}-Z_{0}\right\|^{2}\geq\frac{h}{8(L+1)}\|T-T^{*}\|_{P}^{2}.

The proof of Theorem4is given in
AppendixF.
The corresponding excess-cost contraction follows by combining the theorem with
a standard cost-difference estimate.

## Corollary 2(One-step contraction of excess cost).

LetX0∼PX_{0}\sim P,X1=T​(X0)X_{1}=T(X_{0}), and suppose that the coupling(X0,X1)(X_{0},X_{1})is c-rectifiable. Let(Z0,Z1)=c-​𝚁𝚎𝚌𝚝𝚒𝚏𝚢​(X0,X1)(Z_{0},Z_{1})=\text{c-}\mathtt{Rectify}(X_{0},X_{1}).
Assume Assumptions1–3. Then𝔼​‖Z1−Z0‖2−W22​(P,Q)≤(1−λ​h8​(L+1))​(𝔼​‖T​(X0)−X0‖2−W22​(P,Q)).\mathbb{E}\left\|Z_{1}-Z_{0}\right\|^{2}-W_{2}^{2}(P,Q)\leq\left(1-\frac{\lambda h}{8(L+1)}\right)\left(\mathbb{E}\left\|T(X_{0})-X_{0}\right\|^{2}-W_{2}^{2}(P,Q)\right).

The proof of Corollary2is given in
AppendixF.
Thus, under local regularity and projection stability, a singlec-rectified step decreases the excess quadratic transport cost by a
fixed multiplicative factor. The remaining issue is whether the same
hypotheses persist along the iterates.

## 3.3.2Local Validity of the Projection Bound

We finally explain why Assumption3is natural near
the Brenier map. The following result proves the bound along smoothPP-measure-preserving perturbations of the identity. Thus, although the main
criterion is stated abstractly, it is locally valid aroundT∗T^{*}in directions
generated by incompressible perturbations ofPP.

## Proposition 4(Local projection stability nearT∗T^{*}).

Assume thatPPhas densitypp, and that

## 3.3.3Iterating the One-step Contraction

The one-step contraction can be iterated provided that the hypotheses of the
one-step result hold uniformly along the sequence ofc-rectified
couplings. We make this uniformity explicit in the following statement.

## Theorem 5(Exponential convergence of iterativec-rectified flow).

Let(Z0(0),Z1(0))(Z_{0}^{(0)},Z_{1}^{(0)})be a initial coupling ofPPandQQ.
For eachk≥0k\geq 0, define the iterates by(Z0(k+1),Z1(k+1)):=c-​𝚁𝚎𝚌𝚝𝚒𝚏𝚢​(Z0(k),Z1(k)).(Z_{0}^{(k+1)},Z_{1}^{(k+1)}):=\text{c-}\mathtt{Rectify}(Z_{0}^{(k)},Z_{1}^{(k)}).

Assume that for everyk≥1k\geq 1, the coupling(Z0(k),Z1(k))(Z_{0}^{(k)},Z_{1}^{(k)})is c-rectifiable and is induced by a transport mapTkT_{k}, namelyZ0(k)∼P,Z1(k)=Tk​(Z0(k)),(Tk)#​P=Q.Z_{0}^{(k)}\sim P,\qquad Z_{1}^{(k)}=T_{k}(Z_{0}^{(k)}),\qquad(T_{k})_{\#}P=Q.

Assume further that Assumptions1–3hold for everyTkT_{k}with the same constantsL,hL,h. Then,
the excess quadratic transport cost satisfies𝔼​‖Z1(k)−Z0(k)‖2−W22​(P,Q)≤exp⁡(−λ​h8​(L+1)​(k−1))​(𝔼​‖T0​(X0)−X0‖2−W22​(P,Q)).\mathbb{E}\left\|Z_{1}^{(k)}-Z_{0}^{(k)}\right\|^{2}-W_{2}^{2}(P,Q)\leq\exp{\left(-\frac{\lambda h}{8(L+1)}(k-1)\right)}\left(\mathbb{E}\left\|T_{0}(X_{0})-X_{0}\right\|^{2}-W_{2}^{2}(P,Q)\right).

The proof of Theorem5is given in
AppendixF.

## Remark 1.

If the initial coupling is not induced by a transport map satisfying the uniform
assumptions, the same argument applies from the first indexk0k_{0}at which the
iterate(Z0(k0),Z1(k0))(Z_{0}^{(k_{0})},Z_{1}^{(k_{0})})enters this admissible class. This is
expected to occur after sufficiently many iterations, since the preceding
convergence result implies that the iterates approach the optimal transport
coupling, for which the required structure is satisfied.
In that case, for everyk≥k0k\geq k_{0},ℰk≤(1−λ​h8​(L+1))k−k0​ℰk0.\mathcal{E}_{k}\leq\left(1-\frac{\lambda h}{8(L+1)}\right)^{k-k_{0}}\mathcal{E}_{k_{0}}.

Thus, the only additional issue for arbitrary initial couplings is to justify
that the iterates enter, and remain in, the class of map-induced couplings for
which the one-step contraction criterion applies.

The deterministic results in this section assume access to the population
marginals and the associated population update. We next supply the statistical
input needed to estimate the marginals from samples, and then combine this
input with stability of optimal transport maps.

## 4Statistical Guarantee ofc-Rectified Flow

## 4.1Score Estimation

This subsection develops the statistical input used in the optimal-transport
map estimation result of Section4.2. We study score
estimation for add-dimensional Gaussian-ratio model and derive matching
upper and lower bounds across the low-, intermediate-, and large-noise
regimes. Projecting the resulting estimator onto a class with suitable
matrix-valued one-sided slope bounds gives a stable reverse-time diffusion and
an explicitW2W_{2}risk bound in every fixed dimension.

For all sufficiently largenn,supf∈ℱα,d{𝔼f​W22​(μ^n,f)}1/2≲∫0n𝔯n,d​(t)1+t​dt+n−1≲ϵn,d:={n−12​log⁡log⁡n,d=1,n−12​(log⁡n)32,d=2,n−α+12​α+d,d≥3.\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{\alpha,d}}\left\{\mathbb{E}_{f}W_{2}^{2}(\widehat{\mu}_{n},f)\right\}^{1/2}\lesssim\int_{0}^{n}\frac{\sqrt{\mathfrak{r}_{n,d}(t)}}{1+t}\mathrm{d}t+n^{-1}\lesssim\epsilon_{n,d}:=\begin{cases}n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\log\log n,&d=1,\\[2.84526pt]

Here𝔯n,d​(t)\mathfrak{r}_{n,d}(t)denotes the local score-risk envelope at timett, whereasϵn,d\epsilon_{n,d}denotes the resulting global Wasserstein
rate after integration along the Gaussian interpolation.

The proof of Theorem7is given in
AppendixH.
These Wasserstein bounds are the marginal-estimation input for the transport
map problem. The next subsection plugs the estimated source and target
marginals into the optimal transport problem and uses map stability to transfer
the marginal rate to the estimated optimal transport map.

## 4.2Application: Optimal Estimation of Optimal Transport

This section combines marginal estimation with stability of optimal transport
maps under perturbations of the source and target measures. We treat the
marginalW2W_{2}error as the statistical input and first derive a plug-in
risk bound for the quadratic-cost Brenier map. The convergence results forcc-rectified flow established earlier provide a constructive procedure for
approximating the corresponding plug-in target. We then formulate an
analogous stability bound for general displacement costs.

We work with the class ofλ\lambda-log-concave andΛ\Lambda-log-smooth probability
measures𝒞λΛ:={P∈𝒫2​(ℝd):d​P​(x)=f​(x)​d​x,λ​Id⪯−∇2log⁡f⪯Λ​Id},{\cal C}_{\lambda}^{\Lambda}:=\left\{P\in{\cal P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}):\mathrm{d}P(x)=f(x)\mathrm{d}x,\quad\lambda I_{d}\preceq-\nabla^{2}\log f\preceq\Lambda I_{d}\right\},

and letT∗T^{*}denote the optimal transport map fromPPtoQQ, whereP,Q∈𝒞λΛP,Q\in{\cal C}_{\lambda}^{\Lambda}. Assume that, from the samplesX1,…,Xn​∼i.i.d.​PX_{1},\dots,X_{n}\overset{\mathrm{i.i.d.}}{\sim}P,Y1,…,Yn​∼i.i.d.​QY_{1},\dots,Y_{n}\overset{\mathrm{i.i.d.}}{\sim}Q,
we construct estimatorsP^n\widehat{P}_{n}andQ^n\widehat{Q}_{n}as in
Section4.1, satisfying𝔼​W22​(P,P^n)≤ρn2,𝔼​W22​(Q,Q^n)≤ρn2.\mathbb{E}W_{2}^{2}(P,\widehat{P}_{n})\leq\rho_{n}^{2},\qquad\mathbb{E}W_{2}^{2}(Q,\widehat{Q}_{n})\leq\rho_{n}^{2}.

The stability argument below only uses these two marginal risk bounds. To
instantiate them with Algorithm2and the
explicit rateϵn,d\epsilon_{n,d}, we additionally assume that the densities ofPPandQQbelong toℱα,d\mathcal{F}_{\alpha,d}, with common model
constants.

The projection step below only enforces membership in𝒞λΛ{\cal C}_{\lambda}^{\Lambda}, the class on which the regularity theory for
optimal maps is available. It does not itself construct the optimal map; the
map is obtained afterward as the exact transport between the projected
marginals.

The estimator is summarized in Algorithm3.Algorithm 3Plug-in estimator of the optimal transport map1:Input:samplesX1,…,Xn​∼i.i.d.​PX_{1},\dots,X_{n}\overset{\mathrm{i.i.d.}}{\sim}PandY1,…,Yn​∼i.i.d.​QY_{1},\dots,Y_{n}\overset{\mathrm{i.i.d.}}{\sim}Q.2:Construct marginal estimatorsP^n,Q^n\widehat{P}_{n},\widehat{Q}_{n}as in Algorithm2.3:Project the marginal estimators onto the structured class:P~n∈arg⁡minμ∈𝒞λΛ⁡W2​(μ,P^n),Q~n∈arg⁡minν∈𝒞λΛ⁡W2​(ν,Q^n).\widetilde{P}_{n}\in\arg\min_{\mu\in{\cal C}_{\lambda}^{\Lambda}}W_{2}(\mu,\widehat{P}_{n}),\qquad\widetilde{Q}_{n}\in\arg\min_{\nu\in{\cal C}_{\lambda}^{\Lambda}}W_{2}(\nu,\widehat{Q}_{n}).4:Compute the exact optimal transport map fromP~n\widetilde{P}_{n}toQ~n\widetilde{Q}_{n}, equivalently the limiting map obtained by Algorithm1.5:Output:the plug-in mapT~n∗\widetilde{T}^{*}_{n}fromP~n\widetilde{P}_{n}toQ~n\widetilde{Q}_{n}.

The outputT~n∗\widetilde{T}^{*}_{n}is therefore an exact population plug-in
target. The deterministic contraction estimates above describe howc-rectified flow approximates this target by iteration.

We use the following stability estimate for optimal transport maps under
squared Euclidean cost, which has been studied inBalakrishnan and Manole (2025).

## Theorem 8(Stability of smooth Brenier maps; Theorem 3 ofBalakrishnan and Manole (2025)).

LetT∗=∇φ0T^{*}=\nabla\varphi_{0}be the optimal transport map
fromPPtoQQ. LetΩ⊂ℝd\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}be a convex set containing the

Letπ~\widetilde{\pi}be an optimal
coupling ofP~\widetilde{P}andQ~\widetilde{Q}. Then1Mφ​𝔼(X,Y)∼π~​‖Y−T∗​(X)‖22≤1mφ​W22​(Q,Q~)+Mφ​W22​(P,P~)+2​W2​(P,P~)​W2​(Q,Q~).\frac{1}{M_{\varphi}}\mathbb{E}_{(X,Y)\sim\widetilde{\pi}}\|Y-T^{*}(X)\|_{2}^{2}\leq\frac{1}{m_{\varphi}}W_{2}^{2}(Q,\widetilde{Q})+M_{\varphi}W_{2}^{2}(P,\widetilde{P})+2W_{2}(P,\widetilde{P})W_{2}(Q,\widetilde{Q}).

## Proposition 5(Plug-in estimation of the Brenier map).

Assume thatP,Q∈𝒞λΛP,Q\in{\cal C}_{\lambda}^{\Lambda}, and that there exist marginal
estimators satisfying{𝔼​W22​(P,P^n)}1/2≤ρn,{𝔼​W22​(Q,Q^n)}1/2≤ρn.\left\{\mathbb{E}W_{2}^{2}(P,\widehat{P}_{n})\right\}^{1/2}\leq\rho_{n},\qquad\left\{\mathbb{E}W_{2}^{2}(Q,\widehat{Q}_{n})\right\}^{1/2}\leq\rho_{n}.

Together with the marginal rates from Section4.1, this gives a

estimated marginals.

## 5General Cost

The preceding sections focused on the quadratic cost. We now extend the same
chain of results to displacement costsc​(y−x)c(y-x): qualitative convergence,
local contraction, and stability of the induced cost-optimal map under marginal
perturbations. The quadratic squared distance is replaced by the dual slack
and thecc-transform geometry.

## 5.1Convergence ofc-Rectified Flow under General Cost

The following theorem is the general-cost analogue of
Theorem1. The squared error is replaced by the
Bregman-type discrepancybcb_{c}, and the velocity field is expressed through
the nonlinear map∇c∗∘∇fs\nabla c^{*}\circ\nabla f_{s}. The additional assumptions
below ensure that this nonlinear loss is stable under weak convergence of the
couplings and local convergence of the potentials.

## Theorem 9(Convergence ofc-rectified flow).

Let(Z0(0),Z1(0))=(X0,X1)(Z_{0}^{(0)},Z_{1}^{(0)})=(X_{0},X_{1})be an initial coupling ofPPandQQ, and let{𝐙(k)=(Z0(k),Z1(k))}k=1∞\{{\bm{Z}}^{(k)}=(Z_{0}^{(k)},Z_{1}^{(k)})\}_{k=1}^{\infty}be a sequence of couplings of(P,Q)(P,Q), where(Z0(k+1),Z1(k+1))=c-​𝚁𝚎𝚌𝚝𝚒𝚏𝚢​(Z0(k),Z1(k))(Z_{0}^{(k+1)},Z_{1}^{(k+1)})=\text{c-}\mathtt{Rectify}(Z_{0}^{(k)},Z_{1}^{(k)}).
For brevity, for eachk≥1k\geq 1,
defineZt(k)=t​Z1(k)+(1−t)​Z0(k)Z_{t}^{(k)}=tZ_{1}^{(k)}+(1-t)Z_{0}^{(k)},t∈[0,1]t\in[0,1],
andgk​(x,s):=(∇c∗∘∇fs𝒁(k))​(x).g_{k}(x,s):=(\nabla c^{*}\circ\nabla f_{s}^{{\bm{Z}}^{(k)}})(x).

Assume that for eachkk, the functionf⋅𝐙(k)f_{\cdot}^{{\bm{Z}}^{(k)}}minimizesL𝒁(k)​(f):=∫01𝔼​[bc​(Z1(k)−Z0(k);(∇c∗∘∇fs)​(Zs(k)))]​ds.L_{{\bm{Z}}^{(k)}}(f):=\int_{0}^{1}\mathbb{E}\Bigl[b_{c}\bigl(Z_{1}^{(k)}-Z_{0}^{(k)};(\nabla c^{*}\circ\nabla f_{s})(Z_{s}^{(k)})\bigr)\Bigr]\mathrm{d}s.

Suppose also there exists anα>0\alpha>0, such that for everyR>0R>0, there exists a constantCR>0C_{R}>0,
for allk≥1k\geq 1,‖f𝒁(k)‖C2+α,1+α​(B​(0,R)¯×[0,1])≤CR;\|f^{{\bm{Z}}^{(k)}}\|_{C^{2+\alpha,1+\alpha}(\overline{B(0,R)}\times[0,1])}\leq C_{R};

Assume moreover that there exist a constantC>0C>0and nonnegative measurable functionsΦ,Ψ:ℝd→[0,∞)\Phi,\Psi:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to[0,\infty)such thatbc​(x;y)≤C​(1+Φ​(x)+Ψ​(y))for all​x,y∈ℝd,b_{c}(x;y)\leq C\bigl(1+\Phi(x)+\Psi(y)\bigr)\qquad\text{for all }x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d},

and that the three families{Φ​(Z1(k)−Z0(k))}k=1∞,{∫01Ψ​(gk​(Zs(k),s))​ds}k=1∞,{c​(Z1(k)−Z0(k))}k=1∞\{\Phi(Z_{1}^{(k)}-Z_{0}^{(k)})\}_{k=1}^{\infty},\qquad\left\{\int_{0}^{1}\Psi\bigl(g_{k}(Z_{s}^{(k)},s)\bigr)\mathrm{d}s\right\}_{k=1}^{\infty},\qquad\{c(Z_{1}^{(k)}-Z_{0}^{(k)})\}_{k=1}^{\infty}

are uniformly integrable.

Thenlimk→∞𝔼​[c​(Z1(k)−Z0(k))]=infX∼P,Y∼Q𝔼​[c​(X−Y)].\lim_{k\to\infty}\mathbb{E}[c(Z_{1}^{(k)}-Z_{0}^{(k)})]=\inf_{X\sim P,\;Y\sim Q}\mathbb{E}[c(X-Y)].

Moreover,(Z0(k),Z1(k))⇒(Z0∗,Z1∗)(Z^{(k)}_{0},Z^{(k)}_{1})\Rightarrow(Z_{0}^{*},Z_{1}^{*}), where(Z0∗,Z1∗)(Z_{0}^{*},Z_{1}^{*})is the unique optimal coupling of(P,Q)(P,Q)under the cost functionc​(x−y)c(x-y).

The proof of Theorem9is given in
AppendixD.1.
The conclusion says thatc-rectified flow still selects the
cost-optimal coupling once the quadratic geometry is replaced by the
correspondingcc-geometry. In this setting, convergence of the objective is
expressed through the cost slack rather than through the squared displacement
alone.

## Remark 2.

A convenient sufficient condition for the uniform integrability assumptions in thecc-loss theorem is the following. Suppose there existp≥1p\geq 1,ϵ>0\epsilon>0, and a constantC>0C>0such that
for allx,y∈ℝdx,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d},max⁡{c​(x),Φ​(x),Ψ​(x)}≤C​(1+|x|p),\max\{c(x),\Phi(x),\Psi(x)\}\leq C(1+|x|^{p}),

and|gk​(x,s)|≤C​(1+|x|q)|g_{k}(x,s)|\leq C(1+|x|^{q})for all(x,s)∈ℝd×[0,1],k≥1(x,s)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,1],\ k\geq 1.
IfP,QP,Qhave finitep​max⁡{q,1}+ϵp\max\{q,1\}+\epsilonmoments, then the three families{Φ​(Z1(k)−Z0(k))}k≥1,{∫01Ψ​(gk​(Zs(k),s))​ds}k≥1,{c​(Z1(k)−Z0(k))}k≥1\{\Phi(Z_{1}^{(k)}-Z_{0}^{(k)})\}_{k\geq 1},\qquad\left\{\int_{0}^{1}\Psi\bigl(g_{k}(Z_{s}^{(k)},s)\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}s\right\}_{k\geq 1},\qquad\{c(Z_{1}^{(k)}-Z_{0}^{(k)})\}_{k\geq 1}

are uniformly integrable.

Indeed,
we havec​(Z1(k)−Z0(k))+Φ​(Z1(k)−Z0(k))≤C​(1+|Z1(k)−Z0(k)|p)≤C​(1+|Z0(k)|p+|Z1(k)|p).c(Z_{1}^{(k)}-Z_{0}^{(k)})+\Phi(Z_{1}^{(k)}-Z_{0}^{(k)})\leq C\bigl(1+|Z_{1}^{(k)}-Z_{0}^{(k)}|^{p}\bigr)\leq C\bigl(1+|Z_{0}^{(k)}|^{p}+|Z_{1}^{(k)}|^{p}\bigr).

Next,
since|Zs(k)|≤|Z0(k)|+|Z1(k)||Z_{s}^{(k)}|\leq|Z_{0}^{(k)}|+|Z_{1}^{(k)}|,
and by the growth assumption ongkg_{k}and Hölder’s inequality,|gk​(Zs(k),s)|p≤C​(1+|Zs(k)|q)p≤C​(1+|Z0(k)|p​q+|Z1(k)|p​q).|g_{k}(Z_{s}^{(k)},s)|^{p}\leq C\bigl(1+|Z_{s}^{(k)}|^{q}\bigr)^{p}\leq C\bigl(1+|Z_{0}^{(k)}|^{pq}+|Z_{1}^{(k)}|^{pq}\bigr).

Using the growth assumption onΨ\Psi, we obtainΨ​(gk​(Zs(k),s))≤C​(1+|gk​(Zs(k),s)|p)⟹∫01Ψ​(gk​(Zs(k),s))​ds≤C​(1+|Z0(k)|p​q+|Z1(k)|p​q).\Psi\bigl(g_{k}(Z_{s}^{(k)},s)\bigr)\leq C\bigl(1+|g_{k}(Z_{s}^{(k)},s)|^{p}\bigr)\implies\int_{0}^{1}\Psi\bigl(g_{k}(Z_{s}^{(k)},s)\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}s\leq C\bigl(1+|Z_{0}^{(k)}|^{pq}+|Z_{1}^{(k)}|^{pq}\bigr).

SinceZ0(k)∼PZ_{0}^{(k)}\sim PandZ1(k)∼QZ_{1}^{(k)}\sim Qfor allkk, andP,QP,Qhave finitep​max⁡{q,1}+ϵ{p\max\{q,1\}}+\epsilonmoments, the two families{1+|Z0(k)|p​q+|Z1(k)|p​q}k≥1,{1+|Z0(k)|p+|Z1(k)|p}k≥1\{1+|Z_{0}^{(k)}|^{pq}+|Z_{1}^{(k)}|^{pq}\}_{k\geq 1},\qquad\{1+|Z_{0}^{(k)}|^{p}+|Z_{1}^{(k)}|^{p}\}_{k\geq 1}

are uniformly integrable. Hence each of the three families above is uniformly integrable.

## 5.2Contraction Beyond Quadratic Loss

Qualitative convergence does not by itself give a rate. We therefore state a
local contraction criterion for a broader class of strictly convex cost
functions. Letc:ℝd→ℝc:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}be a strictly convex cost function. LetT:ℝd→ℝdT:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}be a transport map fromPPtoQQ, and letT∗T^{*}denote the unique optimal transport map for the costc​(y−x)c(y-x). SuchT∗T^{*}exists (see e.g.Villani (2021)).
As before, setX0∼PX_{0}\sim PandX1=T​(X0)X_{1}=T(X_{0}), then we consider the straight interpolationTt=(1−t)​id+t​T,Xt=Tt​(X0),Pt=(Tt)#​P.T_{t}=(1-t)\mathrm{id}+tT,\qquad X_{t}=T_{t}(X_{0}),\qquad P_{t}=(T_{t})_{\#}P.

WheneverTtT_{t}is invertible on its image, the velocity along this
deterministic interpolation isvt=(T−id)∘Tt−1v_{t}=(T-\mathrm{id})\circ T_{t}^{-1}.
We use the same notation for thePtP_{t}-divergence-free space and gradient space as defined before at (8).

We impose the following assumption on the cost function mainly by constraining its Hessian.

## Assumption 4.

The cost functionc∈C2​(ℝd)c\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}), and there exist constants0<μc≤Lc<∞0<\mu_{c}\leq L_{c}<\inftysuch that for allz∈ℝdz\in\mathbb{R}^{d},μc​Id⪯∇2c​(z)⪯Lc​Id.\mu_{c}I_{d}\preceq\nabla^{2}c(z)\preceq L_{c}I_{d}.

Under this assumption, we generalize the projection assumption. In the quadratic case, the relevant vector field wasT−idT-\mathrm{id}. For a general cost, the driving force is the gradient of the cost applied to the displacement.

## Assumption 5.

There exists a constantγ>0\gamma>0such that:‖proj𝒮0⁡(∇c​(T−id))‖P2≥h​‖∇c​(T−id)−∇c​(T∗−id)‖P2.\|\operatorname{proj}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{0}}(\nabla c(T-\mathrm{id}))\|_{P}^{2}\geq h\|\nabla c(T-\mathrm{id})-\nabla c(T^{*}-\mathrm{id})\|_{P}^{2}.

The next lemma compares the excesscc-transport cost with the
force discrepancy∇c​(T−id)−∇c​(T∗−id)\nabla c(T-\mathrm{id})-\nabla c(T^{*}-\mathrm{id}). This comparison is the
general-cost replacement for the quadratic cost-difference estimate used
earlier.

## Lemma 3(Cost deficit controlled by force discrepancy).

Letc:ℝd→ℝc:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}satisfy Assumption4.
LetT∗T^{*}be an optimal transport map fromPPtoQQfor the
costc​(y−x)c(y-x), and letTTbe any transport map fromPPtoQQ. Assume
that there exist optimal Kantorovich potentials(φ,ψ)(\varphi,\psi)given by (3), such thatψ∈C2​(ℝd)\psi\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})and∇2c​(y−x)−∇2ψ​(y)⪯Ly​Id,∀(x,y)∈ℝd×ℝd,\nabla^{2}c(y-x)-\nabla^{2}\psi(y)\preceq L_{y}I_{d},\qquad\forall\,(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d},

Then𝔼​[c​(T​(X)−X)]−Wc​(P,Q)≤Ly2​μc2​‖∇c​(T−id)−∇c​(T∗−id)‖P2.\mathbb{E}[c(T(X)-X)]-W_{c}(P,Q)\leq\frac{L_{y}}{2\mu_{c}^{2}}\|\nabla c(T-\mathrm{id})-\nabla c(T^{*}-\mathrm{id})\|_{P}^{2}.

The proof of Lemma3is given in
AppendixG.

## Theorem 10(One-step Contraction for General Cost).

LetX0∼PX_{0}\sim P,X1=T​(X0)∼QX_{1}=T(X_{0})\sim Q. Suppose(X0,T​(X0))(X_{0},T(X_{0}))is c-rectifiable, and let(Z0,Z1)=c-​𝚁𝚎𝚌𝚝𝚒𝚏𝚢​(X0,T​(X0))(Z_{0},Z_{1})=\text{c-}\mathtt{Rectify}(X_{0},T(X_{0})).
Assume Assumptions1,4,5, together with the hypotheses of Lemma3, we have𝔼​[c​(T​(X0)−X0)]−𝔼​[c​(Z1−Z0)]≥h​μc28​Lc​Ly​(L+1)​(𝔼​[c​(T​(X0)−X0)]−Wc​(P,Q)).\mathbb{E}[c(T(X_{0})-X_{0})]-\mathbb{E}[c(Z_{1}-Z_{0})]\geq\frac{h\mu_{c}^{2}}{8L_{c}L_{y}(L+1)}\left(\mathbb{E}[c(T(X_{0})-X_{0})]-W_{c}(P,Q)\right).

Equivalently,𝔼​[c​(Z1−Z0)]−Wc​(P,Q)≤(1−h​μc28​Lc​Ly​(L+1))​(𝔼​[c​(T​(X0)−X0)]−Wc​(P,Q)).\mathbb{E}[c(Z_{1}-Z_{0})]-W_{c}(P,Q)\leq\left(1-\frac{h\mu_{c}^{2}}{8L_{c}L_{y}(L+1)}\right)\left(\mathbb{E}[c(T(X_{0})-X_{0})]-W_{c}(P,Q)\right).

The proof of Theorem10is given in
AppendixG.
Thus, under the general-cost projection stability condition, onec-rectified step removes a fixed fraction of the excesscc-transport
cost. The constants now depend on the curvature ofccand on the local
curvature of the dual slack.

The one-step estimate can be iterated once the assumptions hold uniformly along
the sequence ofcc-rectified couplings.

## Theorem 11(Exponential convergence under uniform general-cost stability).

Let(Z0(0),Z1(0))(Z_{0}^{(0)},Z_{1}^{(0)})be an initial coupling ofPPandQQ, and
define(Z0(k+1),Z1(k+1))=c-​𝚁𝚎𝚌𝚝𝚒𝚏𝚢​(Z0(k),Z1(k))(Z_{0}^{(k+1)},Z_{1}^{(k+1)})=\text{c-}\mathtt{Rectify}(Z_{0}^{(k)},Z_{1}^{(k)}),k≥0k\geq 0.
Assume that for everyk≥0k\geq 0, the coupling(Z0(k),Z1(k))(Z_{0}^{(k)},Z_{1}^{(k)})iscc-rectifiable and induced by a transport mapTkT_{k}, so thatZ0(k)∼PZ_{0}^{(k)}\sim P,Z1(k)=Tk​(Z0(k))Z_{1}^{(k)}=T_{k}(Z_{0}^{(k)}),(Tk)#​P=Q(T_{k})_{\#}P=Q.
Assume further that
Assumptions1,4,5, and in Lemma3hold uniformly alongTkT_{k}, with the same constantsL,hL,h. Then𝔼​[c​(Z1(k)−Z0(k))]−Wc​(P,Q)≤exp⁡(−h​μc2​(k−1)8​Lc​Ly​(L+1))​(𝔼​[c​(Z1(0)−Z0(0))]−Wc​(P,Q)).\mathbb{E}[c(Z_{1}^{(k)}-Z_{0}^{(k)})]-W_{c}(P,Q)\leq\exp\left(-\frac{h\mu_{c}^{2}(k-1)}{8L_{c}L_{y}(L+1)}\right)\left(\mathbb{E}[c(Z_{1}^{(0)}-Z_{0}^{(0)})]-W_{c}(P,Q)\right).

The proof of Theorem11is given in
AppendixG.
This gives the algorithmic part of the general-cost theory. To obtain a
statistical statement, it remains to control how perturbing the source and
target marginals changes the inducedcc-optimal map.

## 5.3Estimation for General Cost

The final ingredient is a stability estimate for general displacement costs.
The assumptions below play the same role as strong convexity and smoothness of
the Brenier potential in the quadratic case.

## Theorem 12(Stability bound for displacement costs).

Suppose Assumption4holds, and let(φ,ψ)(\varphi,\psi)be an optimal Kantorovich dual pair for(P,Q)(P,Q)under
costcc, whereT∗T^{*}is thecc-OT map fromPPtoQQ.
Define the slack functionD​(x,y):=c​(y−x)−φ​(x)−ψ​(y)D(x,y):=c(y-x)-\varphi(x)-\psi(y).
Assume further that there exist constantsμy,Lx,Ly>0\mu_{y},L_{x},L_{y}>0such that the
following Hessian bounds hold for all(x,y)(x,y):μy​I⪯∇y​y2D​(x,y)=∇2c​(y−x)−∇2ψ​(y)⪯Ly​I,\mu_{y}I\preceq\nabla^{2}_{yy}D(x,y)=\nabla^{2}c(y-x)-\nabla^{2}\psi(y)\preceq L_{y}I,∇x​x2D​(x,y)=∇2c​(y−x)−∇2φ​(x)⪯Lx​I,\nabla^{2}_{xx}D(x,y)=\nabla^{2}c(y-x)-\nabla^{2}\varphi(x)\preceq L_{x}I,

Finally, letπ~\widetilde{\pi}be an optimal coupling betweenP~\widetilde{P}andQ~\widetilde{Q}for the costc​(y−x)c(y-x).
Thenμy​∫‖y−T∗​(x)‖22​dπ~​(x,y)≤Lx​W22​(P,P~)+Ly​W22​(Q,Q~)+2​Lc​W2​(P,P~)​W2​(Q,Q~).\mu_{y}\int\frac{\|y-T^{*}(x)\|^{2}}{2}\,\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\pi}(x,y)\leq L_{x}W_{2}^{2}(P,\widetilde{P})+L_{y}W_{2}^{2}(Q,\widetilde{Q})+2L_{c}W_{2}(P,\widetilde{P})W_{2}(Q,\widetilde{Q}).

The proof of Theorem12is given in
AppendixI.
This bound transfers marginal Wasserstein perturbations into anL2L^{2}-type
control of the induced cost-optimal map. It is the general-cost analogue of the
smooth Brenier-map stability used above.

Combining this stability estimate with the marginal estimators from
Section4.1gives the corresponding plug-in rate.

## Corollary 3(Plug-in stability for displacement costs).

LetT~nc\widetilde{T}_{n}^{c}be thecc-optimal map fromP~n\widetilde{P}_{n}toQ~n\widetilde{Q}_{n}, and letTcT^{c}be thecc-optimal map fromPPtoQQ. Suppose that the Kantorovich potentials associated with(P~n,Q~n)(\widetilde{P}_{n},\widetilde{Q}_{n})satisfy the assumptions of
Theorem12, with constants independent ofnn. Then𝔼P​‖Tc​(X)−T~nc​(X)‖2≲ρn2\mathbb{E}_{P}\|T^{c}(X)-\widetilde{T}_{n}^{c}(X)\|^{2}\lesssim\rho_{n}^{2}.

The proof of Corollary3is given in
AppendixI.
The main body has therefore established the full chain:c-rectified
flow targets the correct optimal coupling, contracts under local stability
conditions, and, when combined with score-based marginal estimation, yields
rate-optimal estimation of the optimal transport map and its general-cost
analogue.{acks}
