The authors would like to thank Aram-Alexandre Pooladian for helpful discussions.

## Appendix AAdditional Related Work

## Flow-based estimation and bridge methods.

There is a broader theory of convergence rates for flow-based transport
estimators that is adjacent to our objective. For general flow matching,
near-minimax convergence guarantees under Wasserstein metrics are established
inFukumizuet al.(2025); Kunkel and Trabs (2025). For rectified flow,Sahooet al.(2026)proves order-optimal sample complexity under smoothness
assumptions. These results concern statistical estimation of distributions or
transport dynamics from samples, whereas our main algorithmic results concern
the population rectification operator and its convergence to the optimal
transport coupling.

Schrödinger bridges and entropic optimal transport provide regularized
counterparts of the transport problem. Classical references interpret
Schrödinger bridges as entropy-regularized optimal transport on path spaceLéonard (2014); Di Marino and Gerolin (2020). In the small-noise limit,
Schrödinger potentials converge to Kantorovich potentialsNutz and Wiesel (2022), and the associated entropic optimizers admit
quantitative large-deviation asymptotics with local exponential ratesBerntonet al.(2022). Diffusion Schrödinger bridge matching introduces
iterative Markovian fitting as a bridge-matching procedureShiet al.(2023); related iterative methods are studied inPeluchetti (2023); Kholkinet al.(2024); andGentiloni Silveriet al.(2025)establishes non-asymptotic exponential
convergence guarantees for iterative Markovian fitting. Our results instead
concern unregularized rectified-flow iteration and convergence to classical
optimal transport.

## Score-based estimation and sampling.

Classical score matching estimates unnormalized models through the score of the
log densityHyvärinen (2005), and denoising score matching
provides a practical way to estimate scores after Gaussian smoothingVincent (2011). These ideas became central in score-based
generative modeling, where reverse-time diffusions use estimated scores to
sample from an unknown distributionSonget al.(2021).
The recent workDouet al.(2024)studies how optimal score-estimation
rates can be converted into optimal sampling guarantees, and its algorithmic
structure is closely related to the projected reverse sampler used in our
statistical section. In our setting, the resulting marginal estimator is an
input to optimal-transport-map estimation based onc-rectified flow.

## Entropic statistical optimal transport.

Sinkhorn-type estimators are computationally efficient and statistically more
stable than unregularized empirical optimal transport. The sample complexity
of Sinkhorn divergences was studied byGenevayet al.(2019), whileMena and Weed (2019)obtained statistical bounds and central limit theorems
for entropic optimal transport. More recently,Rigollet and Stromme (2025)proved dimension-free parametric rates for several entropic optimal transport
quantities. In the semi-discrete setting,Pooladianet al.(2023b)proved a
minimax-optimal dimension-independentn−1/2n^{-1/2}rate for an entropic
estimator of discontinuous optimal transport maps. These estimators target
regularized quantities, so their statistical benefits must be balanced against
regularization bias.

The barycentric projection of the empirical entropic plan was analyzed byPooladian and Niles-Weed (2021), which obtained finite-sample guarantees for a
computationally tractable Sinkhorn-based map estimator. The role of
regularization bias was further studied byPooladianet al.(2022), while
stability results for entropic Brenier maps inDivolet al.(2025b)quantify
how regularized maps vary with the target measure. This literature complements
the minimax theory for unregularized plug-in optimal transport map estimators
studied in the main body.

## Appendix BAdditional Details for Section2

## B.1Additional optimal-transport facts

The Kantorovich problem can be regarded as an infinite-dimensional linear program, hence admits a dual formulation:12​W22​(P,Q)=sup(f,g)∈ℱ{∫f​dP+∫g​dQ},\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}W_{2}^{2}(P,Q)=\sup_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{F}}\left\{\int f\mathrm{d}P+\int g\mathrm{d}Q\right\},(11)

whereℱ:={(f,g):f∈L1​(P),g∈L1​(Q),f​(x)+g​(y)≤12​‖x−y‖2}.\displaystyle\mathcal{F}:=\left\{(f,g):f\in L^{1}(P),\ g\in L^{1}(Q),\ f(x)+g(y)\leq\frac{1}{2}\|x-y\|^{2}\right\}.

The functions(f,g)(f,g)are called Kantorovich potentials. Under the finite second moment assumption, there exists a maximizing pair of Kantorovich potentials(f0,g0)(f_{0},g_{0})solving (11)Villani (2009).

The following fundamental theorem shows that, under absolute continuity of the source measure, the Kantorovich and Monge formulations are unified by a unique optimal map.

## Theorem 13(Brenier’s theoremKnott and Smith (1984); Villani (2009)).

LetP∈𝒫2​(ℝd)P\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})be absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure, and letQ∈𝒫2​(ℝd)Q\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}). Then there exists aPP-a.e. unique optimal transport mapT∗T^{*}fromPPtoQQ. Moreover, there exists a convex functionφ0:ℝd→ℝ\varphi_{0}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}such thatT∗=∇φ0P​-a.e.\displaystyle T^{*}=\nabla\varphi_{0}\quad P\text{-a.e.}

The unique optimal coupling in (1) is induced by the mapd​π0​(x,y)=d​P​(x)⋅δT∗​(x)​(y)\mathrm{d}\pi_{0}(x,y)=\mathrm{d}P(x)\cdot\delta_{T^{*}(x)}(y).

Thus, when the source distribution has a density, the optimal coupling is concentrated on the graph of a deterministic map. In particular, the solution of the Kantorovich problem (1) induces the solution of the Monge problem (2).

The Brenier potential can also be obtained from the Kantorovich potentials. Let(f0,g0)(f_{0},g_{0})be optimal potentials for (11), and defineφ0​(x):=12​‖x‖2−f0​(x),ψ0​(y):=12​‖y‖2−g0​(y).\displaystyle\varphi_{0}(x):=\frac{1}{2}\|x\|^{2}-f_{0}(x),\qquad\psi_{0}(y):=\frac{1}{2}\|y\|^{2}-g_{0}(y).(12)

Thenφ0\varphi_{0}andψ0\psi_{0}are convex functions, andφ0\varphi_{0}is the Brenier potential whose gradient gives the optimal transport map fromPPtoQQ:T∗=∇φ0T^{*}=\nabla\varphi_{0},PP-a.e.
IfQQis also absolutely continuous, then the inverse optimal transport map fromQQtoPPis given by(T∗)−1=∇ψ0(T^{*})^{-1}=\nabla\psi_{0},QQ-a.e.
Moreover,φ0\varphi_{0}andψ0\psi_{0}are convex conjugates of one another, that is,ψ0=φ0∗\psi_{0}=\varphi_{0}^{*}.

The Kantorovich dual can be rewritten in a more concise way in terms of the Brenier potentials. Specifically, ifφ∗\varphi^{*}denotes the Legendre–Fenchel conjugate ofφ\varphi,
then we have the semi-dual formulation12​W22​(P,Q)=12​∫‖x‖2​dP​(x)+12​∫‖y‖2​dQ​(y)−infφ{∫φ​dP+∫φ∗​dQ},\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}W_{2}^{2}(P,Q)=\frac{1}{2}\int\|x\|^{2}\mathrm{d}P(x)+\frac{1}{2}\int\|y\|^{2}\mathrm{d}Q(y)-\inf_{\varphi}\left\{\int\varphi\mathrm{d}P+\int\varphi^{*}\mathrm{d}Q\right\},(13)

where the infimum is taken over suitable functionsφ\varphifor which the two integrals are well-defined. Under the assumptions of Brenier’s theorem, the minimizer of (13) is precisely the Brenier potentialφ0\varphi_{0}, and the optimal transport map is recovered asT∗=∇φ0T^{*}=\nabla\varphi_{0}. This semi-dual perspective is frequently used for statistical estimation of optimal transport maps; for example,Hütter and Rigollet (2021)studies (13) over smooth function classes such as functions with bounded Hölder norms.

We also recall the dynamic formulation of optimal transport. Let(ρt)t∈[0,1](\rho_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}be a curve of probability measures onℝd\mathbb{R}^{d}such thatρ0=P\rho_{0}=Pandρ1=Q\rho_{1}=Q. If particles evolve according to the velocity fieldvt:ℝd→ℝdv_{t}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}, equivalently, ifXt∼ρtX_{t}\sim\rho_{t}follows the ODEd​Xt=vt​(Xt)​d​t,\displaystyle\mathrm{d}X_{t}=v_{t}(X_{t})\mathrm{d}t,

then the evolution of their law is described by the continuity equation∂tρt+∇⋅(ρt​vt)=0,\displaystyle\partial_{t}\rho_{t}+\nabla\cdot(\rho_{t}v_{t})=0,

understood in the weak sense. The Benamou–Brenier formula gives a dynamic characterization of the22-Wasserstein distance, which states that the optimal coupling is related to the minimizer of the total kinetic energy along the path space:12​W22​(P,Q)=infρt,vt∫01∫ℝd12​‖vt​(x)‖2​dρt​(x)​dt=infρt,vt∫0112​𝔼​‖vt​(Xt)‖2​dt,\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}W_{2}^{2}(P,Q)=\inf_{\rho_{t},v_{t}}\int_{0}^{1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{1}{2}\|v_{t}(x)\|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\rho_{t}(x)\mathrm{d}t=\inf_{\rho_{t},v_{t}}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}\|v_{t}(X_{t})\|^{2}\mathrm{d}t,(14)

where the infimum is taken over all pairs(ρt,vt)(\rho_{t},v_{t})satisfying the continuity equation and the boundary conditionsρ0=P\rho_{0}=P,ρ1=Q\rho_{1}=QBenamou and Brenier (2000); Villani (2009).

Under the assumptions of Brenier’s theorem, letTTbe the optimal transport map fromPPtoQQ. Then the minimizing path in (14) is the constant-speed displacement interpolationXt=(1−t)​X0+t​T​(X0),t∈[0,1],X0∼P.\displaystyle X_{t}=(1-t)X_{0}+tT(X_{0}),\qquad t\in[0,1],\qquad X_{0}\sim P.(15)

Equivalently, the intermediate law isρt=((1−t)​Id+t​T)#​P\rho_{t}=\big((1-t)\mathrm{Id}+tT\big)_{\#}Pfor allt∈[0,1]t\in[0,1].
The curve(ρt)t∈[0,1](\rho_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}is a constant-speed geodesic in the Wasserstein space(𝒫2​(ℝd),W2)(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),W_{2}).

## B.2Statistical OT background

## B.3Statistical Property for Optimal Transport

We briefly recall the statistical estimation problem for optimal transport.
In statistical estimation problems,PPandQQare typically unknown and are observed through
finite given samples. For instance, given i.i.d. samplesX1,…,Xn∼PX_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\sim PandY1,…,Ym∼QY_{1},\ldots,Y_{m}\sim Q, one forms the empirical measuresP^n=n−1​∑i=1nδXi\widehat{P}_{n}=n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{X_{i}}andQ^m=m−1​∑j=1mδYj\widehat{Q}_{m}=m^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\delta_{Y_{j}}. Natural plug-in estimators of
transport costs are thenWp​(P^n,Q)W_{p}(\widehat{P}_{n},Q), in the one-sample case, orWp​(P^n,Q^m)W_{p}(\widehat{P}_{n},\widehat{Q}_{m}), in the two-sample case. A central question
is to quantify the error between these empirical quantities and their
population counterparts, for example|Wp​(P^n,Q^m)−Wp​(P,Q)||W_{p}(\widehat{P}_{n},\widehat{Q}_{m})-W_{p}(P,Q)|or|Wpp​(P^n,Q^m)−Wpp​(P,Q)||W_{p}^{p}(\widehat{P}_{n},\widehat{Q}_{m})-W_{p}^{p}(P,Q)|.

The first major phenomenon in this literature is the curse of dimensionality.
For general distributions onℝd\mathbb{R}^{d}, the empirical measure converges in
Wasserstein distance at dimension-dependent rates. Nonasymptotic bounds of this
type were obtained byFournier and Guillin (2015), while sharp finite-sample and
asymptotic rates were further developed byWeed and Bach (2019). In a
representative compactly supported setting, the empirical Wasserstein error satisfies
dimension-dependent rates of the form𝔼​Wpp​(μ^n,μ)≲{n−1/2,d<2​p,n−1/2​log⁡n,d=2​p,n−p/d,d>2​p,\mathbb{E}W_{p}^{p}(\widehat{\mu}_{n},\mu)\lesssim\begin{cases}n^{-1/2},&d<2p,\\
n^{-1/2}\log n,&d=2p,\\
n^{-p/d},&d>2p,\end{cases}

up to assumptions and constants depending on the support and moment conditions;
seeFournier and Guillin (2015); Weed and Bach (2019).Niles-Weed and Rigollet (2022)construct a case in which the minimax rate (of the estimation ofW2​(P,Q)W_{2}(P,Q)) is at leastC​n−1dCn^{-\frac{1}{d}}for largeddand a constantCC.
Thus, whend>2​pd>2p,Wp​(μ^n,μ)W_{p}(\widehat{\mu}_{n},\mu)typically behaves liken−1/dn^{-1/d}, displaying the
classical curse of dimensionality for empirical optimal transport.
There is also a line of work that studies statistical estimation of optimal
transport maps under additional structural assumptions on the Brenier map. For
example,Hütter and Rigollet (2021)considers optimal transport
problem with quadratic loss,
and studies the minimax rate for estimatingT∗T^{*}. In contrast to the
distribution-free empirical Wasserstein theory, this setting imposes structural
regularity assumptions on the transport map and on the underlying measures.
The smoothness of the
transport map is measured through‖T∗‖Cα≤R\|T^{*}\|_{C^{\alpha}}\leq Rfor a fixed
constantα\alphaandRR. The estimation error is measured in the loss‖T^−T∗‖P2=𝔼P​‖T^​(X)−T∗​(X)‖2\|\hat{T}-T^{*}\|_{P}^{2}=\mathbb{E}_{P}\|\hat{T}(X)-T^{*}(X)\|^{2},
whereT^\hat{T}is an estimator constructed from the samples. Under these
assumptions,Hütter and Rigollet (2021)proves the minimax lower boundinfT^supP,T∗‖T^−T∗‖P2≳n−2​α2​α−2+d∨n−1,\displaystyle\inf_{\hat{T}}\sup_{P,T^{*}}\|\hat{T}-T^{*}\|_{P}^{2}\gtrsim n^{-\frac{2\alpha}{2\alpha-2+d}}\vee{n}^{-1},

where the supremum is taken over the admissible pairs(P,T∗)(P,T^{*})satisfying the
above regularity conditions. They also construct an estimatorT^\hat{T}using an estimator constructed on a wavelet basis such
thatsupP,T∗‖T^−T∗‖P2≲n−2​α2​α−2+d​(log⁡n)2∨n−1.\displaystyle\sup_{P,T^{*}}\|\hat{T}-T^{*}\|_{P}^{2}\lesssim n^{-\frac{2\alpha}{2\alpha-2+d}}(\log n)^{2}\vee{n}^{-1}.

Thus, up to the logarithmic factor in the upper bound, the minimax rate isn−2​α2​α−2+d∨n−1n^{-\frac{2\alpha}{2\alpha-2+d}}\vee n^{-1}. The remaining logarithmic gap is
conjectured inHütter and Rigollet (2021)to be removable. These results show how
smoothness of the Brenier map can partially offset the statistical curse of
dimensionality: whenT∗T^{*}is very smooth, namely whenα\alphais large, the
rate approaches the parametric ordern−1n^{-1}; however, whenα\alphais small
andddis large, the rate remains slow and is close to the high-dimensional
empirical-OT behavior.

Another line of work studies the estimation of entropic optimal transport, where the objective isinfπ∈Π​(P,Q){∫ℝd×ℝd‖x−y‖2​dπ​(x,y)+η⋅KL​(π∥P⊗Q)}.\inf_{\pi\in\Pi(P,Q)}\left\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\|x-y\|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\pi(x,y)+\eta\cdot\mathrm{KL}\left(\pi\,\|\,P\otimes Q\right)\right\}.

We discuss this line of work in the related work
section and omit further details here.

## B.4Additional rectified-flow facts

The basic properties of rectification are the following. First, since𝑿{\bm{X}}and𝒁{\bm{Z}}have the same expected velocity field, they have the same time marginals; in particular,ℒ​(Z1)=ℒ​(X1)\mathcal{L}(Z_{1})=\mathcal{L}(X_{1}), andℒ​(Zt)=ℒ​(Xt)\mathcal{L}(Z_{t})=\mathcal{L}(X_{t})for allt∈[0,1]t\in[0,1].
Second, for any convex functionc:ℝd→ℝc:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R},𝔼​[c​(Z1−Z0)]≤𝔼​[c​(X1−X0)]\mathbb{E}[c(Z_{1}-Z_{0})]\leq\mathbb{E}[c(X_{1}-X_{0})].
For the detailed proof of the above facts, we refer to(Liuet al.,2022, Theorem 3.3, 3.5).

Therefore, the rectified flow can be iterated. Starting from an initial coupling(Z0(0),Z1(0))=(X0,X1)(Z_{0}^{(0)},Z_{1}^{(0)})=(X_{0},X_{1}), define recursively(Z0(k),Z1(k))=𝚁𝚎𝚌𝚝𝚒𝚏𝚢​((Z0(k−1),Z1(k−1))),\displaystyle(Z_{0}^{(k)},Z_{1}^{(k)})=\mathtt{Rectify}((Z_{0}^{(k-1)},Z_{1}^{(k-1)})),

Thenℒ​(Z0(k))=ℒ​(X0)=P\mathcal{L}(Z_{0}^{(k)})=\mathcal{L}(X_{0})=P,ℒ​(Z1(k))=ℒ​(X1)=Q\mathcal{L}(Z_{1}^{(k)})=\mathcal{L}(X_{1})=Q, and for every convexcc,𝔼​[c​(Z1(k)−Z0(k))]\mathbb{E}[c(Z_{1}^{(k)}-Z_{0}^{(k)})]is non-increasing inkk.
In practice, one estimatesv𝑿v^{{\bm{X}}}from samples by solving (4) over a parameterized class, solves the ODE (5) with the fitted field, and then uses the resulting pair(Z0(k),Z1(k))(Z_{0}^{(k)},Z_{1}^{(k)})to fit a transport map. This gives a learned transport fromPPtoQQ, although the limiting coupling is not necessarily optimal for a prescribed transport cost.

If we view the rectified flow as an optimization algorithm on the path space of time-differentiable stochastic processes with path-wisecc-transport cost, then one-step rectified flow will be a one-step steepest descent.
For a time-differentiable stochastic process𝒀{\bm{Y}},
defineFc​(𝒀)=∫01𝔼​[c​(Y˙s)]​ds.\displaystyle F_{c}({\bm{Y}})=\int_{0}^{1}\mathbb{E}\left[c(\dot{Y}_{s})\right]\mathrm{d}s.(16)

## Lemma 4(Lemma 3.3 inLiu (2022)).

For every convex functionsc:ℝd→ℝc\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R},𝒁=𝚁𝚎𝚌𝚝𝚒𝚏𝚢​(𝑿)⟺𝒁=arg⁡min𝒀⁡{Fc​(𝒀)s.t.v𝒀=v𝑿}.\displaystyle{\bm{Z}}=\mathtt{Rectify}({\bm{X}})\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad{\bm{Z}}=\arg\min_{{\bm{Y}}}\left\{F_{c}({\bm{Y}})\quad\text{s.t.}\quad v^{{\bm{Y}}}=v^{{\bm{X}}}\right\}.(17)

## B.5Additionalc-rectified-flow facts

## Marginal-preserving vector fields.

We first define the notion of marginal-preserving vector fields. Let𝑿={Xt:t∈[0,1]}{\bm{X}}=\{X_{t}:t\in[0,1]\}be a stochastic process.

## Definition 1(Marginal-preserving vector field).

A vector fieldr:ℝd×[0,1]→ℝdr:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}^{d}is called𝑿{\bm{X}}-marginal-preserving if, for everyt∈[0,1]t\in[0,1]and everyh∈Cc1​(ℝd)h\in C_{c}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),∫0t𝔼​[∇h​(Xs)⊤​rs​(Xs)]​ds=0.\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}[\nabla h(X_{s})^{\top}r_{s}(X_{s})]\mathrm{d}s=0.

This condition means thatrrcontributes zero to the weak continuity equation along the marginal curveℒ​(Xt)\mathcal{L}(X_{t}). The following characterization is the basic tool used incc-rectified flow.

## Theorem 14(Lemma 4.2 inLiu (2022)).

Let𝐗{\bm{X}}and𝐘{\bm{Y}}be time-differentiable stochastic processes such thatℒ​(X0)=ℒ​(Y0)\mathcal{L}(X_{0})=\mathcal{L}(Y_{0}). Assume that𝐗{\bm{X}}is rectifiable and thatv𝐘v^{{\bm{Y}}}exists and is locally bounded. Thenℒ​(Xt)=ℒ​(Yt)\mathcal{L}(X_{t})=\mathcal{L}(Y_{t}), for allt∈[0,1]t\in[0,1],
if and only ifv𝐗−v𝐘v^{{\bm{X}}}-v^{{\bm{Y}}}is𝐘{\bm{Y}}-marginal-preserving.

## Theorem 15(Theorem 4.2 inLiu (2022)).

For acc-rectifiable𝐗{\bm{X}}andc∗∈C1​(ℝd)c^{*}\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d}), let𝐙=c-​𝚁𝚎𝚌𝚝𝚒𝚏𝚢​(𝐗){\bm{Z}}=\text{c-}\mathtt{Rectify}(\bm{X}), we haveℒ​(Zt)=ℒ​(Xt)\mathcal{L}(Z_{t})=\mathcal{L}(X_{t})fort∈[0,1]t\in[0,1].

## c-Rectified duality formulation.

Back to the viewpoint of the rectified flow algorithm as an optimization on path space, A natural relaxation of (17) would be𝒁=arg⁡min𝒀⁡{Fc​(𝒀),s.t.ℒ​(Yt)=ℒ​(Xt),∀t∈[0,1]},\displaystyle{\bm{Z}}=\arg\min_{{\bm{Y}}}\left\{F_{c}({\bm{Y}}),\quad\text{s.t.}\quad\mathcal{L}(Y_{t})=\mathcal{L}(X_{t}),\,\,\forall t\in[0,1]\right\},(18)

For each continuously differentiablef:ℝd×[0,1]→ℝdf:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}^{d}, Fenchel’s inequality and Theorem14yieldFc​(𝑿)−Fc​(𝒀)\displaystyle F_{c}({\bm{X}})-F_{c}({\bm{Y}})=∫01𝔼​[c​(X˙s)−c​(Y˙s)]​ds≤∫01𝔼​[c​(X˙s)+c∗​(∇fs​(Ys))−⟨∇fs​(Ys),Y˙s⟩]​ds\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\mathbb{E}[c(\dot{X}_{s})-c(\dot{Y}_{s})]\mathrm{d}s\leq\int_{0}^{1}\mathbb{E}[c(\dot{X}_{s})+c^{*}(\nabla f_{s}(Y_{s}))-\langle\nabla f_{s}(Y_{s}),\dot{Y}_{s}\rangle]\mathrm{d}s(19)=∫01𝔼​[c​(X˙s)+c∗​(∇fs​(Ys))−⟨∇fs​(Ys),vs𝒀​(Ys)⟩]​ds\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\mathbb{E}[c(\dot{X}_{s})+c^{*}(\nabla f_{s}(Y_{s}))-\langle\nabla f_{s}(Y_{s}),v^{{\bm{Y}}}_{s}(Y_{s})\rangle]\mathrm{d}s=∫01𝔼​[c​(X˙s)+c∗​(∇fs​(Xs))−⟨∇fs​(Ys),vs𝑿​(Ys)⟩]​ds=∫01𝔼​[mc​(X˙s,∇fs​(Xs))]​ds,\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\mathbb{E}[c(\dot{X}_{s})+c^{*}(\nabla f_{s}(X_{s}))-\langle\nabla f_{s}(Y_{s}),v^{{\bm{X}}}_{s}(Y_{s})\rangle]\mathrm{d}s=\int_{0}^{1}\mathbb{E}[m_{c}(\dot{X}_{s},\nabla f_{s}(X_{s}))]\mathrm{d}s,(20)

where (19) is becausec​(x)+c∗​(y)≥⟨x,y⟩c(x)+c^{*}(y)\geq\langle x,y\rangleand (20) is becausev𝑿−v𝒀v^{{\bm{X}}}-v^{{\bm{Y}}}isYY-marginal-preserving as stated in Theorem14. Therefore, (19) takes equality if and only ifY˙s=∇c∗​(∇fs​(Ys))\dot{Y}_{s}=\nabla c^{*}(\nabla f_{s}(Y_{s}))for almost everyss.
This suggests the strong duality, as presented in(Liu,2022, Theorem 5.3), thatminf⁡{L𝑿,c​(f):=∫01𝔼​[mc​(X˙s,∇fs​(Xs))]​ds}=sup𝒀{Fc​(𝑿)−Fc​(𝒀):ℒ​(Xt)=ℒ​(Yt),∀t∈[0,1]},\displaystyle\min_{f}\left\{L_{{\bm{X}},c}(f):=\int_{0}^{1}\mathbb{E}[m_{c}(\dot{X}_{s},\nabla f_{s}(X_{s}))]\mathrm{d}s\right\}=\sup_{{\bm{Y}}}\Big\{F_{c}({\bm{X}})-F_{c}({\bm{Y}}):\mathcal{L}(X_{t})=\mathcal{L}(Y_{t}),\ \forall t\in[0,1]\Big\},(21)

## Remark 3.

For the rectified flow, the analogous dual identity isinfv∫01𝔼​[bc​(X˙s;vs​(Xs))]​ds=sup𝒀{Fc​(𝑿)−Fc​(𝒀):v𝑿=v𝒀}.\displaystyle\inf_{v}\int_{0}^{1}\mathbb{E}[b_{c}(\dot{X}_{s};v_{s}(X_{s}))]\mathrm{d}s=\sup_{{\bm{Y}}}\Big\{F_{c}({\bm{X}})-F_{c}({\bm{Y}}):v^{{\bm{X}}}=v^{{\bm{Y}}}\Big\}.

## Stationary points ofc-rectified flow.

The next result identifies whether a stationary coupling of thec-rectified flow
must be optimal.

## Theorem 16(Theorem 5.6 inLiu (2022)).

Letccbe convex withc,c∗∈C1​(ℝd)c,c^{*}\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d}), and let𝐗={Xt=t​X1+(1−t)​X0:t∈[0,1]}{\bm{X}}=\{X_{t}=tX_{1}+(1-t)X_{0}:\ t\in[0,1]\}for a coupling(X0,X1)(X_{0},X_{1}). Assume that𝐗{\bm{X}}iscc-rectifiable and thatf𝐗,c∈C2,1​(ℝd×[0,1])f^{{\bm{X}},c}\in C^{2,1}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,1])minimizesL𝐗,cL_{{\bm{X}},c}. Then the following are equivalent:(X0,X1)=c-​𝚁𝚎𝚌𝚝𝚒𝚏𝚢​((X0,X1))⟺L𝑿,c​(f𝑿,c)=0⟺(X0,X1)​is a c-optimal coupling.(X_{0},X_{1})=\text{c-}\mathtt{Rectify}((X_{0},X_{1}))\,\,\Longleftrightarrow\,\,L_{{\bm{X}},c}(f^{{\bm{X}},c})=0\,\,\Longleftrightarrow\,\,(X_{0},X_{1})\text{ is a {c}-optimal coupling}.

## Decrease of transport cost.

DefineSc​(𝑿)=Fc​(𝑿)−𝔼​[c​(X1−X0)]S_{c}({\bm{X}})=F_{c}({\bm{X}})-\mathbb{E}[c(X_{1}-X_{0})], where recallFcF_{c}is defined at (16). By the convexity ofccand Jensen’s inequality, for every time differentiable process𝑿{\bm{X}}, we always haveSc​(𝑿)≥0S_{c}({\bm{X}})\geq 0.
Also,
If𝑿{\bm{X}}is the linear interpolation process betweenX0X_{0}andX1X_{1}, thenX˙t=X1−X0\dot{X}_{t}=X_{1}-X_{0}for allt∈[0,1]t\in[0,1], we haveSc​(𝑿)=0S_{c}({\bm{X}})=0.

Now let𝒀=c-​𝚁𝚎𝚌𝚝𝚒𝚏𝚢​(𝑿){\bm{Y}}=\text{c-}\mathtt{Rectify}(\bm{X}), so that(Y0,Y1)=c-​𝚁𝚎𝚌𝚝𝚒𝚏𝚢​(X0,X1)(Y_{0},Y_{1})=\text{c-}\mathtt{Rectify}(X_{0},X_{1}). By the dual identity (21),Fc​(𝑿)−Fc​(𝒀)=L𝑿,c​(f𝑿,c).\displaystyle F_{c}({\bm{X}})-F_{c}({\bm{Y}})=L_{{\bm{X}},c}(f^{{\bm{X}},c}).(22)

Therefore, we have the following inequality𝔼​[c​(X1−X0)]−𝔼​[c​(Y1−Y0)]\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[c(X_{1}-X_{0})]-\mathbb{E}[c(Y_{1}-Y_{0})]=Fc​(𝑿)−Fc​(𝒀)+Sc​(𝒀)=L𝑿,c​(f𝑿,c)+Sc​(𝒀)≥0.\displaystyle=F_{c}({\bm{X}})-F_{c}({\bm{Y}})+S_{c}({\bm{Y}})=L_{{\bm{X}},c}(f^{{\bm{X}},c})+S_{c}({\bm{Y}})\geq 0.(23)

Hencecc-rectification does not increase the transport cost. In particular, the sequence of couplings generated by Algorithm1has non-increasing transport cost.

Finally, by (23), for everyK≥1K\geq 1,∑k=1KL𝒁(k),c​(f𝒁(k),c)\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{K}L_{{\bm{Z}}^{(k)},c}(f^{{\bm{Z}}^{(k)},c})≤∑k=1K(𝔼​[c​(Z0(k−1)−Z1(k−1))]−𝔼​[c​(Z0(k)−Z1(k))])\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=1}^{K}\left(\mathbb{E}[c(Z^{(k-1)}_{0}-Z^{(k-1)}_{1})]-\mathbb{E}[c(Z^{(k)}_{0}-Z^{(k)}_{1})]\right)=𝔼​[c​(Z1(0)−Z0(0))]−𝔼​[c​(Z0(K)−Z1(K))]≤𝔼​[c​(Z1(0)−Z0(0))]<+∞.\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[c(Z_{1}^{(0)}-Z_{0}^{(0)})]-\mathbb{E}[c(Z^{(K)}_{0}-Z^{(K)}_{1})]\leq\mathbb{E}[c(Z_{1}^{(0)}-Z_{0}^{(0)})]<+\infty.

Therefore, as stated in(Liu,2022, Corollary 5.7),mink≤K⁡L𝒁(k),c​(f𝒁(k),c)=O​(1/K).\displaystyle\min_{k\leq K}L_{{\bm{Z}}^{(k)},c}(f^{{\bm{Z}}^{(k)},c})=O(1/K).(24)

## Proof of Proposition1.

Since(X0,X1)(X_{0},X_{1})is jointly Gaussian, the pair(X1−X0,Xt)(X_{1}-X_{0},X_{t})is jointly Gaussian and centered. Therefore,𝔼​[X1−X0∣Xt=x]=Cov⁡(X1−X0,Xt)​Σt−1​x.\mathbb{E}[X_{1}-X_{0}\mid X_{t}=x]=\operatorname{Cov}(X_{1}-X_{0},X_{t})\Sigma_{t}^{-1}x.

Moreover,Cov⁡(X1−X0,Xt)\displaystyle\operatorname{Cov}(X_{1}-X_{0},X_{t})=Cov⁡(X1−X0,(1−t)​X0+t​X1)\displaystyle=\operatorname{Cov}\bigl(X_{1}-X_{0},(1-t)X_{0}+tX_{1}\bigr)=(1−t)​(Cov⁡(X1,X0)−Cov⁡(X0,X0))+t​(Cov⁡(X1,X1)−Cov⁡(X0,X1))\displaystyle=(1-t)\bigl(\operatorname{Cov}(X_{1},X_{0})-\operatorname{Cov}(X_{0},X_{0})\bigr)+t\bigl(\operatorname{Cov}(X_{1},X_{1})-\operatorname{Cov}(X_{0},X_{1})\bigr)=(1−t)​(Σ01⊤−Σ1)+t​(Σ2−Σ01).\displaystyle=(1-t)(\Sigma_{01}^{\top}-\Sigma_{1})+t(\Sigma_{2}-\Sigma_{01}).

Substituting this identity into the Gaussian conditional expectation formula
gives (7).
∎

we haveX0=((1−t)​I+t​L)−1​XtX_{0}=\bigl((1-t)I+tL\bigr)^{-1}X_{t}almost surely. Thereforevt​(x)=𝔼​[(L−I)​X0∣Xt=x]=(L−I)​((1−t)​I+t​L)−1​xv_{t}(x)=\mathbb{E}[(L-I)X_{0}\mid X_{t}=x]=(L-I)\bigl((1-t)I+tL\bigr)^{-1}x.

## Proof of Proposition2.

By Lemma1, the first run of rectified flow produces the deterministic couplingX1=T​X0X_{1}=TX_{0}.
Applying Lemma2withL=TL=Tshows that every subsequent run preserves this coupling.
Thus the algorithm stabilizes at the mapTTafter one step.

Then, we compareTTwith the Gaussian optimal transport mapTOTT_{\mathrm{OT}}.

First assume thatΣ1\Sigma_{1}andΣ2\Sigma_{2}commute.
ThenΣ1−1/2\Sigma_{1}^{-1/2}andΣ2\Sigma_{2}also commute, and henceΣ1−1/2​Σ2​Σ1−1/2=Σ1−1​Σ2\Sigma_{1}^{-1/2}\Sigma_{2}\Sigma_{1}^{-1/2}=\Sigma_{1}^{-1}\Sigma_{2}.
ThereforeT=Σ11/2​(Σ1−1​Σ2)1/2​Σ1−1/2=Σ21/2​Σ1−1/2.T=\Sigma_{1}^{1/2}\bigl(\Sigma_{1}^{-1}\Sigma_{2}\bigr)^{1/2}\Sigma_{1}^{-1/2}=\Sigma_{2}^{1/2}\Sigma_{1}^{-1/2}.

Similarly, sinceΣ11/2\Sigma_{1}^{1/2}andΣ2\Sigma_{2}commute,TOT=Σ1−1/2​(Σ1​Σ2)1/2​Σ1−1/2=Σ21/2​Σ1−1/2T_{\mathrm{OT}}=\Sigma_{1}^{-1/2}\bigl(\Sigma_{1}\Sigma_{2}\bigr)^{1/2}\Sigma_{1}^{-1/2}=\Sigma_{2}^{1/2}\Sigma_{1}^{-1/2}.
HenceT=TOTT=T_{\mathrm{OT}}.

Conversely, assume thatT=TOTT=T_{\mathrm{OT}}.
SinceTOTT_{\mathrm{OT}}is symmetric positive definite,TTmust be symmetric. WriteM:=Σ1−1/2​Σ2​Σ1−1/2.M:=\Sigma_{1}^{-1/2}\Sigma_{2}\Sigma_{1}^{-1/2}.

ThenT=Σ11/2​M1/2​Σ1−1/2T=\Sigma_{1}^{1/2}M^{1/2}\Sigma_{1}^{-1/2}.
The symmetry ofTTgivesT=T⊤T=T^{\top}, which is,Σ11/2​M1/2​Σ1−1/2=Σ1−1/2​M1/2​Σ11/2.\Sigma_{1}^{1/2}M^{1/2}\Sigma_{1}^{-1/2}=\Sigma_{1}^{-1/2}M^{1/2}\Sigma_{1}^{1/2}.

Multiplying on the left and right byΣ11/2\Sigma_{1}^{1/2}yieldsΣ1​M1/2=M1/2​Σ1\Sigma_{1}M^{1/2}=M^{1/2}\Sigma_{1}.
Therefore,Σ1\Sigma_{1}commutes withM1/2M^{1/2}, and hence also withM=M1/2​M1/2M=M^{1/2}M^{1/2}.

SinceMMcommutes withΣ1\Sigma_{1},MMalso commutes with every spectral function ofΣ1\Sigma_{1}, in particular withΣ11/2\Sigma_{1}^{1/2}.
UsingΣ2=Σ11/2​M​Σ11/2\Sigma_{2}=\Sigma_{1}^{1/2}M\Sigma_{1}^{1/2},
we computeΣ1​Σ2=Σ1​Σ11/2​M​Σ11/2=Σ13/2​M​Σ11/2=Σ11/2​M​Σ13/2=Σ11/2​M​Σ11/2​Σ1=Σ2​Σ1,\Sigma_{1}\Sigma_{2}=\Sigma_{1}\Sigma_{1}^{1/2}M\Sigma_{1}^{1/2}=\Sigma_{1}^{3/2}M\Sigma_{1}^{1/2}=\Sigma_{1}^{1/2}M\Sigma_{1}^{3/2}=\Sigma_{1}^{1/2}M\Sigma_{1}^{1/2}\Sigma_{1}=\Sigma_{2}\Sigma_{1},

where we used the commutation ofΣ1\Sigma_{1}andMM.
ThusΣ1​Σ2=Σ2​Σ1\Sigma_{1}\Sigma_{2}=\Sigma_{2}\Sigma_{1}.
We have thus shown thatT=TOTT=T_{\mathrm{OT}}impliesΣ1​Σ2=Σ2​Σ1\Sigma_{1}\Sigma_{2}=\Sigma_{2}\Sigma_{1}. Together with the first implication, this proves the equivalence. Hence noncommuting covariances lead after one step to a non-optimal fixed point of rectified flow.
∎

## Lemma 5(Continuity of integrated losses).

Let𝐙(k)=(Z0(k),Z1(k))⇒𝐙∗=(Z0∗,Z1∗){\bm{Z}}^{(k)}=(Z_{0}^{(k)},Z_{1}^{(k)})\Rightarrow{\bm{Z}}^{*}=(Z_{0}^{*},Z_{1}^{*})inℝd×ℝd\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}, and defineZt(k)=(1−t)​Z0(k)+t​Z1(k)Z_{t}^{(k)}=(1-t)Z_{0}^{(k)}+tZ_{1}^{(k)},Zt∗=(1−t)​Z0∗+t​Z1∗Z_{t}^{*}=(1-t)Z_{0}^{*}+tZ_{1}^{*}.
Lethk,h∗:ℝd×[0,1]→ℝmh_{k},h_{*}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}^{m}be continuous functions such thathk→h∗h_{k}\to h_{*}locally uniformly onℝd×[0,1]\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,1], and letℓ:ℝd×ℝm→[0,∞)\ell:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}\to[0,\infty)be continuous. DefineVk:=∫01ℓ​(Z1(k)−Z0(k);hk​(Zt(k),t))​dt,V∗:=∫01ℓ​(Z1∗−Z0∗;h∗​(Zt∗,t))​dt.V_{k}:=\int_{0}^{1}\ell\bigl(Z_{1}^{(k)}-Z_{0}^{(k)};h_{k}(Z_{t}^{(k)},t)\bigr)\mathrm{d}t,\qquad V_{*}:=\int_{0}^{1}\ell\bigl(Z_{1}^{*}-Z_{0}^{*};h_{*}(Z_{t}^{*},t)\bigr)\mathrm{d}t.

If{Vk}k≥1\{V_{k}\}_{k\geq 1}is uniformly integrable, then𝔼​[Vk]⟶𝔼​[V∗]\mathbb{E}[V_{k}]\longrightarrow\mathbb{E}[V_{*}].

## Proof of Lemma5.

By the Skorokhod representation theorem, after replacing the random
variables by copies with the same laws on a common probability space, we
may assume that(Z0(k),Z1(k))⟶(Z0∗,Z1∗)almost surely.(Z_{0}^{(k)},Z_{1}^{(k)})\longrightarrow(Z_{0}^{*},Z_{1}^{*})\qquad\text{almost surely}.

Fix an eventω\omegafor which this convergence holds. Thensupt∈[0,1]‖Zt(k)​(ω)−Zt∗​(ω)‖≤‖Z0(k)​(ω)−Z0∗​(ω)‖+‖Z1(k)​(ω)−Z1∗​(ω)‖⟶0.\sup_{t\in[0,1]}\bigl\|Z_{t}^{(k)}(\omega)-Z_{t}^{*}(\omega)\bigr\|\leq\|Z_{0}^{(k)}(\omega)-Z_{0}^{*}(\omega)\|+\|Z_{1}^{(k)}(\omega)-Z_{1}^{*}(\omega)\|\longrightarrow 0.

In particular, there existsR=R​(ω)<∞R=R(\omega)<\inftysuch thatZt(k)​(ω),Zt∗​(ω)∈B​(0,R)¯Z_{t}^{(k)}(\omega),Z_{t}^{*}(\omega)\in\overline{B(0,R)}for everyt∈[0,1]t\in[0,1]and everykk.

Using the local uniform convergence ofhkh_{k}and the uniform continuity
ofh∗h_{*}on the compact setB​(0,R)¯×[0,1]\overline{B(0,R)}\times[0,1], we obtainsupt∈[0,1]‖hk​(Zt(k)​(ω),t)−h∗​(Zt∗​(ω),t)‖\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,1]}\bigl\|h_{k}(Z_{t}^{(k)}(\omega),t)-h_{*}(Z_{t}^{*}(\omega),t)\bigr\|≤sup(x,t)∈B​(0,R)¯×[0,1]‖hk​(x,t)−h∗​(x,t)‖+supt∈[0,1]‖h∗​(Zt(k)​(ω),t)−h∗​(Zt∗​(ω),t)‖⟶0.\displaystyle\quad\leq\sup_{(x,t)\in\overline{B(0,R)}\times[0,1]}\|h_{k}(x,t)-h_{*}(x,t)\|+\sup_{t\in[0,1]}\bigl\|h_{*}(Z_{t}^{(k)}(\omega),t)-h_{*}(Z_{t}^{*}(\omega),t)\bigr\|\longrightarrow 0.

Moreover,Z1(k)​(ω)−Z0(k)​(ω)⟶Z1∗​(ω)−Z0∗​(ω)Z_{1}^{(k)}(\omega)-Z_{0}^{(k)}(\omega)\longrightarrow Z_{1}^{*}(\omega)-Z_{0}^{*}(\omega).
By the continuity ofℓ\ell, which implies uniform
continuity on this compact set,supt∈[0,1]|ℓ​(Z1(k)−Z0(k);hk​(Zt(k),t))−ℓ​(Z1∗−Z0∗;h∗​(Zt∗,t))|⟶0\sup_{t\in[0,1]}\left|\ell\bigl(Z_{1}^{(k)}-Z_{0}^{(k)};h_{k}(Z_{t}^{(k)},t)\bigr)-\ell\bigl(Z_{1}^{*}-Z_{0}^{*};h_{*}(Z_{t}^{*},t)\bigr)\right|\longrightarrow 0

almost surely. Consequently, we have almost surely thatVk⟶V∗V_{k}\longrightarrow V_{*}.
Since{Vk}k≥1\{V_{k}\}_{k\geq 1}is uniformly integrable, Vitali’s theorem yields𝔼​[Vk]⟶𝔼​[V∗]\mathbb{E}[V_{k}]\longrightarrow\mathbb{E}[V_{*}].
Because the expectations involved depend only on the laws of the random
variables, the conclusion also holds on the original probability spaces.
∎

This implies that the full sequence satisfies(Z0(k),Z1(k))⇒(Z0∗,Z1∗)(Z_{0}^{(k)},Z_{1}^{(k)})\Rightarrow(Z_{0}^{*},Z_{1}^{*}).

## Remark 4.

The uniform integrability assumption in the theorem is automatically satisfied ifgghas polynomial growth. More precisely, suppose there exist constantsC>0C>0andp≥0p\geq 0such thatg​(x)≤C​(1+|x|p)g(x)\leq C(1+|x|^{p})for allx∈ℝdx\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.
IfPPandQQhave finite2​p+ϵ2p+\epsilon-moments, then the family{∫01g​(Zs(k))2​ds}k≥1\left\{\int_{0}^{1}g\bigl(Z^{(k)}_{s}\bigr)^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s\right\}_{k\geq 1}

is uniformly integrable.

Indeed, since|Zt(k)|≤|Z0(k)|+|Z1(k)||Z_{t}^{(k)}|\leq|Z_{0}^{(k)}|+|Z_{1}^{(k)}|, using Hölder’s inequality,g​(Zt(k))2≤C​(1+|Zt(k)|2​p)≤C​(1+|Z0(k)|2​p+|Z1(k)|2​p)⟹∫01g​(Zt(k))2​dt≤C​(1+|Z0(k)|2​p+|Z1(k)|2​p).g(Z_{t}^{(k)})^{2}\leq C(1+|Z_{t}^{(k)}|^{2p})\leq C\bigl(1+|Z_{0}^{(k)}|^{2p}+|Z_{1}^{(k)}|^{2p}\bigr)\implies\int_{0}^{1}g(Z_{t}^{(k)})^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq C\bigl(1+|Z_{0}^{(k)}|^{2p}+|Z_{1}^{(k)}|^{2p}\bigr).

SinceZ0(k)∼PZ_{0}^{(k)}\sim PandZ1(k)∼QZ_{1}^{(k)}\sim Qfor allkk, andP,QP,Qhave finite2​p+ϵ2p+\epsilon-moments, the families{|Z0(k)|2​p}k≥1\{|Z_{0}^{(k)}|^{2p}\}_{k\geq 1},{|Z1(k)|2​p}k≥1\{|Z_{1}^{(k)}|^{2p}\}_{k\geq 1}are uniformly integrable. Therefore{∫01g​(Zt(k))2​dt}k≥1\left\{\int_{0}^{1}g(Z_{t}^{(k)})^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\right\}_{k\geq 1}is uniformly integrable.

Especially, ifPPandQQfollow a Gaussian distribution, thenggwill be a linear function, satisfying all the above.

The tightness argument for{(Z0(k),Z1(k))}k≥1\{(Z_{0}^{(k)},Z_{1}^{(k)})\}_{k\geq 1}is exactly the same as in the proof of Theorem1. Hence, after passing to a further subsequence,(Z0(k),Z1(k))⇒(Z0∗,Z1∗)(Z_{0}^{(k)},Z_{1}^{(k)})\Rightarrow(Z_{0}^{*},Z_{1}^{*})for some coupling𝒁∗:=(Z0∗,Z1∗){\bm{Z}}^{*}:=(Z_{0}^{*},Z_{1}^{*})of(P,Q)(P,Q).

Since∇c∗\nabla c^{*}is continuous, it follows thatgk​(x,s)=(∇c∗∘∇fs𝒁(k))​(x)→g∗​(x,s):=(∇c∗∘∇fs∗)​(x)g_{k}(x,s)=(\nabla c^{*}\circ\nabla f_{s}^{{\bm{Z}}^{(k)}})(x)\to g_{*}(x,s):=(\nabla c^{*}\circ\nabla f_{s}^{*})(x)

locally uniformly onℝd×[0,1]\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,1].

Now, we apply Lemma5withhk​(x,t):=gk​(x,t)h_{k}(x,t):=g_{k}(x,t),h∗​(x,t):=g∗​(x,t)h_{*}(x,t):=g_{*}(x,t),ℓ​(a;b):=bc​(a;b)\ell(a;b):=b_{c}(a;b).
Herebcb_{c}is continuous by assumption, and the preceding argument gives
the required local uniform convergencegk→g∗g_{k}\to g_{*}.
DefineVk:=∫01bc​(Z1(k)−Z0(k);gk​(Zt(k),t))​dt.V_{k}:=\int_{0}^{1}b_{c}\bigl(Z_{1}^{(k)}-Z_{0}^{(k)};g_{k}(Z_{t}^{(k)},t)\bigr)\mathrm{d}t.

By the assumed growth bound onbcb_{c},Vk\displaystyle V_{k}≤C​∫01[1+Φ​(Z1(k)−Z0(k))+Ψ​(gk​(Zt(k),t))]​dt=C​[1+Φ​(Z1(k)−Z0(k))+∫01Ψ​(gk​(Zt(k),t))​dt].\displaystyle\leq C\int_{0}^{1}\left[1+\Phi(Z_{1}^{(k)}-Z_{0}^{(k)})+\Psi\bigl(g_{k}(Z_{t}^{(k)},t)\bigr)\right]\mathrm{d}t=C\left[1+\Phi(Z_{1}^{(k)}-Z_{0}^{(k)})+\int_{0}^{1}\Psi\bigl(g_{k}(Z_{t}^{(k)},t)\bigr)\mathrm{d}t\right].

By assumption, the two nonconstant terms on the right-hand side form
uniformly integrable families. Hence{Vk}k≥1\{V_{k}\}_{k\geq 1}is uniformly
integrable.
Lemma5therefore yieldsL𝒁(k)​(f𝒁(k))\displaystyle L_{{\bm{Z}}^{(k)}}(f^{{\bm{Z}}^{(k)}})=𝔼​[Vk]⟶𝔼​[∫01bc​(Z1∗−Z0∗;g∗​(Zt∗,t))​dt]=L𝒁∗​(f∗).\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[V_{k}]\longrightarrow\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{1}b_{c}\bigl(Z_{1}^{*}-Z_{0}^{*};g_{*}(Z_{t}^{*},t)\bigr)\mathrm{d}t\right]=L_{{\bm{Z}}^{*}}(f^{*}).

SinceL𝒁(k)​(f𝒁(k))⟶0L_{{\bm{Z}}^{(k)}}(f^{{\bm{Z}}^{(k)}})\longrightarrow 0,
we conclude thatL𝒁∗​(f∗)=0L_{{\bm{Z}}^{*}}(f^{*})=0.
Therefore,(Z0∗,Z1∗)(Z_{0}^{*},Z_{1}^{*})is an optimal coupling of(P,Q)(P,Q)under the costc​(y−x)c(y-x).

Finally, since(Z0(k),Z1(k))⇒(Z0∗,Z1∗)(Z_{0}^{(k)},Z_{1}^{(k)})\Rightarrow(Z_{0}^{*},Z_{1}^{*}), we haveZ1(k)−Z0(k)⇒Z1∗−Z0∗Z_{1}^{(k)}-Z_{0}^{(k)}\Rightarrow Z_{1}^{*}-Z_{0}^{*}. Thusc​(Z1(k)−Z0(k))⇒c​(Z1∗−Z0∗)c(Z_{1}^{(k)}-Z_{0}^{(k)})\Rightarrow c(Z_{1}^{*}-Z_{0}^{*}). Because{c​(Z1(k)−Z0(k))}k≥1\{c(Z_{1}^{(k)}-Z_{0}^{(k)})\}_{k\geq 1}is uniformly integrable, Vitali’s theorem yields𝔼​[c​(Z1(k)−Z0(k))]→𝔼​[c​(Z1∗−Z0∗)]\mathbb{E}[c(Z_{1}^{(k)}-Z_{0}^{(k)})]\to\mathbb{E}[c(Z_{1}^{*}-Z_{0}^{*})].
Since𝒁∗{\bm{Z}}^{*}iscc-optimal, we obtainlimk→∞𝔼​[c​(Z1(k)−Z0(k))]=infX∼P,Y∼Q𝔼​[c​(X−Y)].\lim_{k\to\infty}\mathbb{E}[c(Z_{1}^{(k)}-Z_{0}^{(k)})]=\inf_{X\sim P,\;Y\sim Q}\mathbb{E}[c(X-Y)].

This completes the proof.
∎

## Appendix EProof of Section3.2

## E.0.1Technical Preparation

We begin with two elementary structural facts that will be used throughout the proof.

## Factorization of linear transports.

Every linear transport can be regarded as a matrixT∈ℝd×dT\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}andT​(x)=T​xT(x)=Tx,T#​P=Q⇔T​Σ1​T⊤=Σ2.T_{\#}P=Q\iff T\Sigma_{1}T^{\top}=\Sigma_{2}.

The quadratic-cost optimal transport map is also linear:T∗​(x)=Σ1−1/2​(Σ11/2​Σ2​Σ11/2)1/2​Σ1−1/2​x:=T∗​x,T^{*}(x)=\Sigma_{1}^{-1/2}\big(\Sigma_{1}^{1/2}\Sigma_{2}\Sigma_{1}^{1/2}\big)^{1/2}\Sigma_{1}^{-1/2}x:=T^{*}x,(25)

so thatT∗T^{*}is symmetric positive definite andT∗​Σ1​T∗=Σ2T^{*}\Sigma_{1}T^{*}=\Sigma_{2}.

## Lemma 6(Factorization by aΣ1\Sigma_{1}-rotation).

LetTTsatisfyT​Σ1​T⊤=Σ2T\Sigma_{1}T^{\top}=\Sigma_{2}, and letT∗T^{*}be as in (25).
Then there exists a unique matrixUUsuch thatT=T∗​U,U​Σ1​U⊤=Σ1.T=T^{*}U,\qquad U\Sigma_{1}U^{\top}=\Sigma_{1}.

In particular,UUis orthogonal with respect to the inner product⟨x,y⟩Σ1−1:=x⊤​Σ1−1​y\langle x,y\rangle_{\Sigma_{1}^{-1}}:=x^{\top}\Sigma_{1}^{-1}y.
WhenΣ1=I\Sigma_{1}=I,UUis an ordinary orthogonal matrix.

## Proof of Lemma6.

DefineU:=(T∗)−1​TU:=(T^{*})^{-1}T. Using(T∗)⊤=T∗(T^{*})^{\top}=T^{*},T∗​Σ1​T∗=Σ2T^{*}\Sigma_{1}T^{*}=\Sigma_{2}andT​Σ1​T⊤=Σ2T\Sigma_{1}T^{\top}=\Sigma_{2},U​Σ1​U⊤=(T∗)−1​T​Σ1​T⊤​(T∗)−1=(T∗)−1​Σ2​(T∗)−1=Σ1.U\Sigma_{1}U^{\top}=(T^{*})^{-1}T\Sigma_{1}T^{\top}(T^{*})^{-1}=(T^{*})^{-1}\Sigma_{2}(T^{*})^{-1}=\Sigma_{1}.

ThusT=T∗​UT=T^{*}UwithU​Σ1​U⊤=Σ1U\Sigma_{1}U^{\top}=\Sigma_{1}. Uniqueness is immediate sinceT∗T^{*}is invertible.
∎

Henceforth we writeT​(x)=T​x=T∗​U​x,T∗​(x)=T∗​xT(x)=Tx=T^{*}Ux,T^{*}(x)=T^{*}x,
withU​Σ1​U⊤=Σ1U\Sigma_{1}U^{\top}=\Sigma_{1}.

## Projection of linear fields.

We work in the Hilbert spaceL2​(μ;ℝd)L^{2}(\mu;\mathbb{R}^{d})with inner product⟨f,g⟩μ:=𝔼​[f​(X)⊤​g​(X)],X∼𝒩​(0,Σ).\langle f,g\rangle_{\mu}:=\mathbb{E}[f(X)^{\top}g(X)],\qquad X\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma).

For matricesM,N∈ℝd×dM,N\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}, by a slight abuse of notation, we also write⟨M,N⟩μ:=⟨M​x,N​x⟩μ=𝔼​[X⊤​M⊤​N​X]=Tr​(M​Σ​N⊤).\langle M,N\rangle_{\mu}:=\langle Mx,Nx\rangle_{\mu}=\mathbb{E}[X^{\top}M^{\top}NX]=\mathrm{Tr}(M\Sigma N^{\top}).(26)

The next lemma is the finite-dimensional form of the weighted Helmholtz decomposition for Gaussian measures. It shows that, when the input vector field is linear, both its gradient and divergence-free components are again linear. This is the key reason that the Gaussian-linear class is preserved by one-stepc-rectified flow.

## Lemma 7(Projection of a linear field under a Gaussian measure).

Letμ=𝒩​(0,Σ)\mu=\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma)onℝd\mathbb{R}^{d}, whereΣ≻0\Sigma\succ 0, andppis the density ofμ\mu. Denote𝒢μ:={∇φ:φ∈Cc∞​(ℝd)}¯L2​(μ;ℝd){\mathcal{G}}_{\mu}:=\overline{\{\nabla\varphi:\varphi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\}}^{\,L^{2}(\mu;\mathbb{R}^{d})}, and𝒮μ:={u∈L2​(μ;ℝd):∇⋅(p​u)=0​in the weak sense, i.e.​∫ℝdu​(x)⊤​∇φ​(x)​p​(x)​dx=0​for every​φ∈Cc∞​(ℝd)}.{\mathcal{S}}_{\mu}:=\left\{u\in L^{2}(\mu;\mathbb{R}^{d}):\nabla\cdot(pu)=0\text{ in the weak sense, i.e.}~\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}u(x)^{\top}\nabla\varphi(x)\,p(x)\mathrm{d}x=0\text{ for every }\varphi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\right\}.

Then,
- (a)

𝒢μ=𝒮μ⟂{\mathcal{G}}_{\mu}={\mathcal{S}}_{\mu}^{\perp}inL2​(μ;ℝd)L^{2}(\mu;\mathbb{R}^{d}),
and for every linear mapx↦M​xx\mapsto Mx,M​x∈𝒮μMx\in{\mathcal{S}}_{\mu}is equivalent toM=Σ​KM=\Sigma Kfor some skew-symmetricKK,M​x∈𝒢μMx\in{\mathcal{G}}_{\mu}is equivalent toMMis symmetric;
- (b)

For every matrixM∈ℝd×dM\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}, the orthogonal projectionsproj𝒮μ⁡(M​x),proj𝒢μ⁡(M​x)\operatorname{proj}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{\mu}}(Mx),\operatorname{proj}_{{\mathcal{G}}_{\mu}}(Mx)are both linear functions;
- (c)

SupposeKKis the unique skew-symmetric matrix satisfying the continuous Lyapunov equationΣ​K+K​Σ=M−M⊤,\Sigma K+K\Sigma=M-M^{\top},

thenproj𝒢μ⁡(M​x)=(M−Σ​K)​x\operatorname{proj}_{{\mathcal{G}}_{\mu}}(Mx)=(M-\Sigma K)x,proj𝒮μ⁡(M​x)=Σ​K​x\operatorname{proj}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{\mu}}(Mx)=\Sigma Kx.

## Proof of Lemma7.

We argue in three parts.

(a): Characterization of linear fields in𝒮μ{\mathcal{S}}_{\mu}.First,𝒢μ=𝒮μ⟂{\mathcal{G}}_{\mu}={\mathcal{S}}_{\mu}^{\perp}comes from the definition, and for thosex↦M​xx\mapsto Mxin𝒢μ{\mathcal{G}}_{\mu},M​x=∇φMx=\nabla\varphifor some potentialφ\varphi. HenceM=∇(M​x)=∇2φM=\nabla(Mx)=\nabla^{2}\varphiis symmetric. On the other hand, for symmetricMM,M​x=∇(12​x⊤​M​x)∈𝒢μMx=\nabla(\frac{1}{2}x^{\top}Mx)\in{\mathcal{G}}_{\mu}.

We then characterize those linear fieldsx↦M​xx\mapsto Mxthat belong to𝒮μ{\mathcal{S}}_{\mu}. DefineK=Σ−1​MK=\Sigma^{-1}M.
The conditionM​x∈𝒮μMx\in{\mathcal{S}}_{\mu}means∇⋅(p​(x)​M​x)≡0\nabla\cdot(p(x)Mx)\equiv 0.
Using the product rule and the identity∇p​(x)=−p​(x)​Σ−1​x\nabla p(x)=-p(x)\Sigma^{-1}x,
we compute∇⋅(p​(x)​M​x)=p​(x)​Tr​(M)+∇p​(x)⋅M​x=p​(x)​[Tr​(M)−x⊤​Σ−1​M​x].\nabla\cdot(p(x)Mx)=p(x)\mathrm{Tr}(M)+\nabla p(x)\cdot Mx=p(x)\bigl[\mathrm{Tr}(M)-x^{\top}\Sigma^{-1}Mx\bigr].

Sincep​(x)>0p(x)>0, this impliesx⊤​Σ−1​M​x=Tr​(M)x^{\top}\Sigma^{-1}Mx=\mathrm{Tr}(M)for allx∈ℝdx\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.
The quadratic form can be a constant function only ifK=Σ−1​MK=\Sigma^{-1}MsatisfiesK=−K⊤K=-K^{\top}.

Conversely, ifKKis skew-symmetric, thenTr​(M)=Tr​(Σ​K)=0\mathrm{Tr}(M)=\mathrm{Tr}(\Sigma K)=0by the symmetry ofΣ\Sigma, andx⊤​Σ−1​M​x=x⊤​K​x=0x^{\top}\Sigma^{-1}Mx=x^{\top}Kx=0for allxx, satisfying the equation. So indeedM​x∈𝒮μMx\in{\mathcal{S}}_{\mu}.

(b): Reduction to linear fields via adapted Hermite polynomials.Let{Hek​(z)}k∈ℕd\{{\rm He}_{k}(z)\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}^{d}}be the standard multivariate Hermite polynomials, which form an orthogonal basis forL2​(γ;ℝd)L^{2}(\gamma;\mathbb{R}^{d})whereγ=𝒩​(0,Id)\gamma=\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d}). Under the affine transformationz=Σ−1/2​xz=\Sigma^{-1/2}x, the functionsΦk​(x):=Hek​(Σ−1/2​x)\Phi_{k}(x):={\rm He}_{k}(\Sigma^{-1/2}x)form an orthogonal basis for the scalar spaceL2​(μ;ℝd)L^{2}(\mu;\mathbb{R}^{d}). We can orthogonally decomposeL2​(μ;ℝd)=⨁n≥0ℋn​(μ)L^{2}(\mu;\mathbb{R}^{d})=\bigoplus_{n\geq 0}\mathcal{H}_{n}(\mu), where thenn-th chaosℋn​(μ)\mathcal{H}_{n}(\mu)is the span of{Φk​(x):|k|=n}\{\Phi_{k}(x):|k|=n\}.

By the property of the standard Hermite polynomial,∇zHek​(z)∈ℋ|k|−1​(μ)⊕d\nabla_{z}{\rm He}_{k}(z)\in\mathcal{H}_{|k|-1}(\mu)^{\oplus d}. By the chain rule,∇xΦk​(x)=Σ−1/2​∇zHek​(z)\nabla_{x}\Phi_{k}(x)=\Sigma^{-1/2}\nabla_{z}{\rm He}_{k}(z). Therefore,∇xΦk​(x)∈ℋ|k|−1​(μ)⊕d\nabla_{x}\Phi_{k}(x)\in\mathcal{H}_{|k|-1}(\mu)^{\oplus d}.
This implies that for allφ∈ℋn​(μ)\varphi\in\mathcal{H}_{n}(\mu),∇φ∈ℋn−1​(μ)⊕d\nabla\varphi\in\mathcal{H}_{n-1}(\mu)^{\oplus d}. Consequently, ifφ∈ℋn​(μ)\varphi\in\mathcal{H}_{n}(\mu)andψ∈ℋm​(μ)\psi\in\mathcal{H}_{m}(\mu)withn≠mn\neq m,∇φ∈ℋn−1​(μ)⊕d\nabla\varphi\in\mathcal{H}_{n-1}(\mu)^{\oplus d}and∇ψ∈ℋm−1​(μ)⊕d\nabla\psi\in\mathcal{H}_{m-1}(\mu)^{\oplus d}respectively. By the orthonogonal decomposition ofL2​(μ;ℝd)L^{2}(\mu;\mathbb{R}^{d}),⟨∇φ,∇ψ⟩μ=0\langle\nabla\varphi,\nabla\psi\rangle_{\mu}=0.
Thus, the gradient subspaces∇ℋn​(μ)\nabla\mathcal{H}_{n}(\mu)are mutually orthogonal inL2​(μ;ℝd)L^{2}(\mu;\mathbb{R}^{d}).

Now, consider the linear vector fieldf​(x)=M​xf(x)=Mx. Its coordinate functions are linear combinations of the degree-1 adapted Hermite polynomials, meaningM​x∈ℋ1​(μ)⊕dMx\in\mathcal{H}_{1}(\mu)^{\oplus d}. Because∇ℋn​(μ)⊂ℋn−1​(μ)⊕d\nabla\mathcal{H}_{n}(\mu)\subset\mathcal{H}_{n-1}(\mu)^{\oplus d}, for alln−1≠1n-1\neq 1,M​x⟂∇ℋn​(μ)Mx\perp\nabla\mathcal{H}_{n}(\mu).
Therefore,proj𝒢μ⁡(M​x)∈∇ℋ2​(μ)\operatorname{proj}_{{\mathcal{G}}_{\mu}}(Mx)\in\nabla\mathcal{H}_{2}(\mu). Becauseℋ2​(μ)\mathcal{H}_{2}(\mu)consists of scalar quadratic polynomials,∇ℋ2​(μ)\nabla\mathcal{H}_{2}(\mu)only have linear vector fields. Therefore there exist matricesMGM_{G}andMSM_{S}such thatproj𝒢μ⁡(M​x)=MG​x\operatorname{proj}_{{\mathcal{G}}_{\mu}}(Mx)=M_{G}x,proj𝒮μ⁡(M​x)=MS​x\operatorname{proj}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{\mu}}(Mx)=M_{S}x.
Sinceproj𝒢μ⁡(M​x)∈Gμ\operatorname{proj}_{{\mathcal{G}}_{\mu}}(Mx)\in G_{\mu},MGM_{G}must be symmetric.

(c):
Identification of the two projections.SinceM​x=proj𝒢μ⁡(M​x)+proj𝒮μ⁡(M​x)=MG​x+MS​xMx=\operatorname{proj}_{{\mathcal{G}}_{\mu}}(Mx)+\operatorname{proj}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{\mu}}(Mx)=M_{G}x+M_{S}x,
we haveMG=M−MS=M−Σ​KM_{G}=M-M_{S}=M-\Sigma K.
BecauseMGM_{G}is symmetric,M−Σ​K=(M−Σ​K)⊤=M⊤−K⊤​Σ⊤M-\Sigma K=(M-\Sigma K)^{\top}=M^{\top}-K^{\top}\Sigma^{\top}.
UsingΣ⊤=Σ\Sigma^{\top}=\SigmaandK⊤=−KK^{\top}=-K, we obtainM−Σ​K=M⊤+K​ΣM-\Sigma K=M^{\top}+K\Sigma,
henceΣ​K+K​Σ=M−M⊤.\Sigma K+K\Sigma=M-M^{\top}.

It remains to prove uniqueness. SinceΣ≻0\Sigma\succ 0, the linear mapK↦Σ​K+K​ΣK\mapsto\Sigma K+K\Sigmais invertible on the space of skew-symmetric matrices. Therefore the above Lyapunov equation has a unique skew-symmetric solutionKK. This uniquely determinesMS=Σ​K,MG=M−Σ​KM_{S}=\Sigma K,M_{G}=M-\Sigma K.
Thusproj𝒮μ⁡(M​x)=Σ​K​x\operatorname{proj}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{\mu}}(Mx)=\Sigma Kx,proj𝒢μ⁡(M​x)=(M−Σ​K)​x\operatorname{proj}_{{\mathcal{G}}_{\mu}}(Mx)=(M-\Sigma K)x,
as claimed.
∎

We finally recall a standard variational lower bound for projections in Hilbert spaces, which will be the key tool for proving the lower bound in our cases.

## Lemma 8.

Letℋ\mathcal{H}be a Hilbert space and𝒮⊆ℋ{\mathcal{S}}\subseteq\mathcal{H}a closed subspace. For anyv∈ℋv\in\mathcal{H}and nonzerow∈𝒮w\in{\mathcal{S}},‖proj𝒮⁡v‖ℋ≥|⟨v,w⟩ℋ|‖w‖ℋ.\|\operatorname{proj}_{{\mathcal{S}}}v\|_{\mathcal{H}}\geq\frac{|\langle v,w\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}|}{\|w\|_{\mathcal{H}}}.

## Proof of Lemma8.

Letv𝒮=proj𝒮⁡vv_{{\mathcal{S}}}=\operatorname{proj}_{{\mathcal{S}}}v. Sincev−v𝒮⟂𝒮v-v_{{\mathcal{S}}}\perp{\mathcal{S}}, we have⟨v,w⟩ℋ=⟨v𝒮,w⟩ℋ\langle v,w\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}=\langle v_{{\mathcal{S}}},w\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}. The result follows immediately from the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality:|⟨v𝒮,w⟩ℋ|≤‖v𝒮‖ℋ​‖w‖ℋ|\langle v_{\mathcal{S}},w\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}|\leq\|v_{\mathcal{S}}\|_{\mathcal{H}}\|w\|_{\mathcal{H}}.
∎

## E.0.2Explicitc-Rectified Flow Framework in the Gaussian Case

We apply the preceding projection formula to the velocity field generated by a linear coupling. This gives an explicit matrix-valued description of one-stepc-rectified flow. In particular, the projected velocity remains linear at every time, so the resulting flow map is again linear.

Since the joint Gaussian velocity field is always linear as suggested by Proposition1, independent of the initial coupling (as long as the joint distribution is jointly Gaussian), one-stepc-rectified flow will always produce a linear transport map.
Consider the initial linear transport coupling of the Gaussian marginalsX1=T​X0X_{1}=TX_{0},X0∼PX_{0}\sim P,T#​P=QT_{\#}P=Q. The straight interpolation associated with this coupling isXt=(1−t)​X0+t​X1=((1−t)​I+t​T)​X0:=Tt​X0.X_{t}=(1-t)X_{0}+tX_{1}=((1-t)I+tT)X_{0}:=T_{t}X_{0}.

The marginal distribution of the straight interpolation is given byXt∼Pt=(Tt)#​P=𝒩​(0,Σt)X_{t}\sim P_{t}=(T_{t})_{\#}P=\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma_{t}),Σt=Tt​Σ1​Tt⊤\Sigma_{t}=T_{t}\Sigma_{1}T_{t}^{\top}, for allt∈[0,1]t\in[0,1].

According to Lemma6,T=T∗​UT=T^{*}U, so‖T‖op≤‖T∗‖op​‖U‖op<+∞\|T\|_{\rm op}\leq\|T^{*}\|_{\rm op}\|U\|_{\rm op}<+\infty. Hence for everyt∈[0,‖T−I‖op−1)t\in[0,\|T-I\|_{\rm op}^{-1}), the matrixTt=(1−t)​I+t​TT_{t}=(1-t)I+tTis invertible. In what follows we work on the time interval[0,‖T−I‖op−1∧1)[0,\|T-I\|_{\rm op}^{-1}\wedge 1). On this interval,
the velocity field along the interpolation is the linear fieldvt​(y)=(T−I)​Tt−1​yv_{t}(y)=(T-I)T_{t}^{-1}y.

The one-step quadraticc-rectified flow is obtained by projecting this velocity onto the gradient subspace𝒢t=𝒮t⟂⊂L2​(Pt;ℝd){\mathcal{G}}_{t}={\mathcal{S}}_{t}^{\perp}\subset L^{2}(P_{t};\mathbb{R}^{d}), where𝒮t{\mathcal{S}}_{t}denotes theptp_{t}-divergence-free subspace. By Lemma7, projections of linear fields under Gaussian measures remain linear. Hence there exist matricesGtG_{t}andStS_{t}such thatvt​(z)=gt​(z)+st​(z),gt​(z):=proj𝒢t⁡vt​(z)=Gt​z,st​(z):=proj𝒮t⁡vt​(z)=St​z,v_{t}(z)=g_{t}(z)+s_{t}(z),\qquad g_{t}(z):=\operatorname{proj}_{{\mathcal{G}}_{t}}v_{t}(z)=G_{t}z,\qquad s_{t}(z):=\operatorname{proj}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{t}}v_{t}(z)=S_{t}z,

and define thec-rectified flow equation byZ0=X0Z_{0}=X_{0}, andZ˙t=gt​(Zt)=Gt​Zt\dot{Z}_{t}=g_{t}(Z_{t})=G_{t}Z_{t},t∈[0,1]t\in[0,1].
Consequently, the flow itself is linear: if a matrix flow𝒯t{\cal T}_{t}solves𝒯˙t=Gt​𝒯t\dot{\cal T}_{t}=G_{t}{\cal T}_{t},𝒯0=I{\cal T}_{0}=I,
thenZt=𝒯t​X0Z_{t}={\cal T}_{t}X_{0}. In particular, one step ofc-rectified flow again produces a linear Gaussian couplingc-​𝚁𝚎𝚌𝚝𝚒𝚏𝚢​((X0,X1))=(Z0,Z1)=(X0,𝒯1​X0).\text{c-}\mathtt{Rectify}((X_{0},X_{1}))=(Z_{0},Z_{1})=(X_{0},{\cal T}_{1}X_{0}).

Thus, in the Gaussian-linear setting, one step ofc-rectified flow can be implemented entirely at the matrix level. The construction is summarized below.Algorithm 4One-step Gaussianc-rectified flow1:Input:TTsuch thatT​Σ1​T⊤=Σ2T\Sigma_{1}T^{\top}=\Sigma_{2}.2:fort∈[0,1]t\in[0,1]do3:Tt=(1−t)​I+t​TT_{t}=(1-t)I+tT, computeΣt=Tt​Σ1​Tt⊤\Sigma_{t}=T_{t}\Sigma_{1}T_{t}^{\top}, andVt=(T−I)​Tt−1V_{t}=(T-I)T_{t}^{-1}.4:Vt=Gt+StV_{t}=G_{t}+S_{t}inL2​(𝒩​(0,Σt);ℝd)L^{2}(\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma_{t});\mathbb{R}^{d})according to Lemma7.5:Solve𝒯˙t=Gt​𝒯t\dot{\cal T}_{t}=G_{t}{\cal T}_{t}, with𝒯0=I{\cal T}_{0}=I.6:endforreturn𝒯1{\cal T}_{1}

This explicit framework also clarifies a useful limitation of one-stepc-rectification. Although the projection removes the divergence-free part of the instantaneous velocity, it does not generally recover the full optimal map in a single step. The following corollary makes this obstruction precise.

## Proof of Corollary1.

We claim thatproj𝒢0⁡(T​x)=T∗​x\operatorname{proj}_{{\mathcal{G}}_{0}}(Tx)=T^{*}xalready forcesT=T∗T=T^{*}.
Because of the orthogonal decompositionL2​(P;ℝd)=𝒢0⊕𝒮0L^{2}(P;\mathbb{R}^{d})={\mathcal{G}}_{0}\oplus{\mathcal{S}}_{0},T​x=T∗​x+proj𝒮0⁡(T​x)Tx=T^{*}x+\operatorname{proj}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{0}}(Tx),
and therefore‖T​x‖P2=‖T∗​x‖P2+‖proj𝒮0⁡(T​x)‖P2.\|Tx\|_{P}^{2}=\|T^{*}x\|_{P}^{2}+\|\operatorname{proj}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{0}}(Tx)\|_{P}^{2}.

On the other hand, ifX∼PX\sim P, then sinceT#∗​P=T#​P=QT^{*}_{\#}P=T_{\#}P=Q,‖T∗​x‖P2=𝔼P​‖T∗​X‖2=𝔼Q​‖Y‖2,‖T​x‖P2=𝔼P​‖T​X‖2=𝔼Q​‖Y‖2.\|T^{*}x\|_{P}^{2}=\mathbb{E}_{P}\|T^{*}X\|^{2}=\mathbb{E}_{Q}\|Y\|^{2},\qquad\|Tx\|_{P}^{2}=\mathbb{E}_{P}\|TX\|^{2}=\mathbb{E}_{Q}\|Y\|^{2}.

In particular, ifT≠T∗T\neq T^{*}, thenproj𝒢0⁡(T​x)≠T∗​x\operatorname{proj}_{{\mathcal{G}}_{0}}(Tx)\neq T^{*}x.
So one-stepc-rectified flow cannot already produce the optimal transport map.

## E.0.3Quantitative Variational Lower Bound for One-stepc-Rectified Flow

The goal of this subsection is to show that one step ofcc-rectified flow removes a definite amount of the non-gradient component of the initial velocity. ## E.0.4Spectral Criterion for the Projection Bound.

By Lemma6,T−T∗=T∗​(U−I)T-T^{*}=T^{*}(U-I).
Thus the excess over the optimal map is entirely encoded by the “rotational” partU−IU-I.

We formulate the projection hypothesis explicitly as follows.
The preceding estimate reduces the contraction problem to a purely algebraic question: whether the initial divergence-free componentΣ1​K​x\Sigma_{1}Kxcontrols the deviationT−T∗T-T^{*}. The next proposition records this projection bound as the finite-dimensional condition needed for contraction.

## Proposition 6(Explicit Gaussian projection bound).

There existsc>0c>0such that for all admissibleTTin Theorem2,
forKKdefined by (27),‖proj𝒮0⁡(T−I)‖P2=‖Σ1​K‖P2≥c0​‖T−T∗‖P2.\|\operatorname{proj}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{0}}({T-I})\|_{P}^{2}=\|\Sigma_{1}K\|_{P}^{2}\geq c_{0}\,\|T-T^{*}\|_{P}^{2}.

It remains to give a checkable condition under which this projection bound holds. The natural condition is spectral: after whitening byΣ1\Sigma_{1}, the rotational factor becomes an ordinary orthogonal matrix, and the relevant obstruction is the presence of eigenvalues close to−1-1. The following proposition shows that excluding this obstruction quantitatively gives the desired bound.

To derive this bound from the spectrum ofUU, introduce the whitened variablesT~∗:=Σ11/2​T∗​Σ11/2=(Σ11/2​Σ2​Σ11/2)1/2,U~:=Σ1−1/2​U​Σ11/2∈𝒪​(d).\widetilde{T}^{*}:=\Sigma_{1}^{1/2}T^{*}\Sigma_{1}^{1/2}=(\Sigma_{1}^{1/2}\Sigma_{2}\Sigma_{1}^{1/2})^{1/2},\qquad\widetilde{U}:=\Sigma_{1}^{-1/2}U\Sigma_{1}^{1/2}\in{\mathcal{O}}(d).

This proves (29).

## E.0.5Cost-difference Lemma and Contraction.

The final ingredient is to compare the matrix distance‖T−T∗‖P2\|T-T^{*}\|_{P}^{2}with the excess quadratic transport cost. For Gaussian linear transports, this comparison is exact up to multiplication by(T∗)−1(T^{*})^{-1}, and it allows us to convert the projection lower bound into the desired contraction estimate.

## Lemma 9.

ForP=𝒩​(0,Σ1)P=\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma_{1}),Q=𝒩​(0,Σ2)Q=\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma_{2}), and a transport mapTTfromPPtoQQ, we have𝔼​‖T​(X)−X‖2−W22​(P,Q)=Tr​((T∗)−1​(T−T∗)​Σ1​(T−T∗)⊤)≤1λmin​(T∗)​‖T−T∗‖P2.\mathbb{E}\|T(X)-X\|^{2}-W_{2}^{2}(P,Q)=\mathrm{Tr}\Big((T^{*})^{-1}(T-T^{*})\Sigma_{1}(T-T^{*})^{\top}\Big)\leq\frac{1}{\lambda_{\min}(T^{*})}\|T-T^{*}\|_{P}^{2}.

## Proof of Lemma9.

Since bothTTandT∗T^{*}pushPPforward toQQ, we haveT​Σ1​T⊤=T∗​Σ1​(T∗)⊤T\Sigma_{1}T^{\top}=T^{*}\Sigma_{1}(T^{*})^{\top}.
BecauseT∗T^{*}is symmetric, this becomes(T−T∗)​Σ1​(T−T∗)⊤+(T−T∗)​Σ1​T∗+T∗​Σ1​(T−T∗)⊤=0.(T-T^{*})\Sigma_{1}(T-T^{*})^{\top}+(T-T^{*})\Sigma_{1}T^{*}+T^{*}\Sigma_{1}(T-T^{*})^{\top}=0.(32)

On the other hand, sinceT​(X)T(X)andT∗​(X)T^{*}(X)have the same law,𝔼​‖T​(X)‖2=𝔼​‖T∗​(X)‖2\mathbb{E}\|T(X)\|^{2}=\mathbb{E}\|T^{*}(X)\|^{2}.
Therefore,𝔼​‖T​(X)−X‖2−W22​(P,Q)\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\|T(X)-X\|^{2}-W_{2}^{2}(P,Q)=𝔼​‖T​(X)−X‖2−𝔼​‖T∗​(X)−X‖2\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\|T(X)-X\|^{2}-\mathbb{E}\|T^{*}(X)-X\|^{2}=−2​𝔼​⟨(T−T∗)​X,X⟩=−2​T​r​((T−T∗)​Σ1).\displaystyle=-2\mathbb{E}\langle(T-T^{*})X,X\rangle=-2\mathrm{Tr}((T-T^{*})\Sigma_{1}).

Now multiply (32) on the left by(T∗)−1(T^{*})^{-1}and take traces. This gives0\displaystyle 0=Tr​((T∗)−1​(T−T∗)​Σ1​(T−T∗)⊤)+Tr​((T−T∗)​Σ1)+Tr​(Σ1​(T−T∗)⊤).\displaystyle=\mathrm{Tr}\big((T^{*})^{-1}(T-T^{*})\Sigma_{1}(T-T^{*})^{\top}\big)+\mathrm{Tr}((T-T^{*})\Sigma_{1})+\mathrm{Tr}(\Sigma_{1}(T-T^{*})^{\top}).

SinceTr​(Σ1​(T−T∗)⊤)=Tr​((T−T∗)​Σ1)\mathrm{Tr}(\Sigma_{1}(T-T^{*})^{\top})=\mathrm{Tr}((T-T^{*})\Sigma_{1}), we obtainTr​((T∗)−1​(T−T∗)​Σ1​(T−T∗)⊤)=−2​T​r​((T−T∗)​Σ1)\mathrm{Tr}\big((T^{*})^{-1}(T-T^{*})\Sigma_{1}(T-T^{*})^{\top}\big)=-2\mathrm{Tr}((T-T^{*})\Sigma_{1}).
Combining this with the previous identity yields the exact formula𝔼​‖T​(X)−X‖2−W22​(P,Q)=Tr​((T∗)−1​(T−T∗)​Σ1​(T−T∗)⊤).\mathbb{E}\|T(X)-X\|^{2}-W_{2}^{2}(P,Q)=\mathrm{Tr}\Big((T^{*})^{-1}(T-T^{*})\Sigma_{1}(T-T^{*})^{\top}\Big).

Finally, sinceT∗⪰λmin​(T∗)​IT^{*}\succeq\lambda_{\min}(T^{*})I, we have(T∗)−1⪯λmin​(T∗)−1​I(T^{*})^{-1}\preceq\lambda_{\min}(T^{*})^{-1}I, and thereforeTr​((T∗)−1​(T−T∗)​Σ1​(T−T∗)⊤)≤1λmin​(T∗)​Tr​((T−T∗)​Σ1​(T−T∗)⊤)=1λmin​(T∗)​‖T−T∗‖P2.\mathrm{Tr}\Big((T^{*})^{-1}(T-T^{*})\Sigma_{1}(T-T^{*})^{\top}\Big)\leq\frac{1}{\lambda_{\min}(T^{*})}\mathrm{Tr}\Big((T-T^{*})\Sigma_{1}(T-T^{*})^{\top}\Big)=\frac{1}{\lambda_{\min}(T^{*})}\|T-T^{*}\|_{P}^{2}.

This proves the lemma.

Finally,c~⋅λmin​(T∗)=λmin​(T∗)3​λmin​(Σ1)2⋅γ216​λmax​(T∗)2​λmax​(Σ1)2​(1+λmax​(T∗)):=γ2​c,\tilde{c}\cdot\lambda_{\min}(T^{*})=\frac{\lambda_{\min}(T^{*})^{3}\lambda_{\min}(\Sigma_{1})^{2}\cdot\gamma^{2}}{16{\lambda_{\max}(T^{*})^{2}\lambda_{\max}(\Sigma_{1})^{2}}(1+\lambda_{\max}(T^{*}))}:=\gamma^{2}c,

wherec=C​(Spec​(Σ1),Spec​(Σ2))c=C(\mathrm{Spec}(\Sigma_{1}),\mathrm{Spec}(\Sigma_{2}))is a constant that only depends on the spectrum ofΣ1\Sigma_{1}andΣ2\Sigma_{2}, i.e. the second moments ofPPandQQ.

for allk≥1k\geq 1, there exists aTkT_{k}such thatZ1(k)=Tk​Z0(k)Z_{1}^{(k)}=T_{k}Z_{0}^{(k)}andTk=T∗​UkT_{k}=T^{*}U_{k}whereUkU_{k}is aΣ1\Sigma_{1}-orthogonal matrix by Lemma6.

According to the proof of Theorem1,{(Z0(k),Z1(k))}k∈ℕ\{(Z_{0}^{(k)},Z_{1}^{(k)})\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}is tight, and so{(Z0(kn),Z1(kn))}n∈ℕ\{(Z_{0}^{(k_{n})},Z_{1}^{(k_{n})})\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}is tight, there is a further subsequence ofknk_{n}, still denoted by the indexkk, such that(Z0(k),Z1(k))⇒(Z0∗,Z1∗).(Z_{0}^{(k)},Z_{1}^{(k)})\Rightarrow(Z_{0}^{*},Z_{1}^{*}).

By the continuity property,Z1(k)−T∗​Z0(k)⇒Z1∗−T∗​Z0∗=0Z_{1}^{(k)}-T^{*}Z_{0}^{(k)}\Rightarrow Z_{1}^{*}-T^{*}Z_{0}^{*}=0. SinceZ1(k)=T∗​Uk​Z0(k)Z_{1}^{(k)}=T^{*}U_{k}Z_{0}^{(k)}, this further showsT∗​(Uk−I)​Z0(k)⇒0.T^{*}(U_{k}-I)Z_{0}^{(k)}\Rightarrow 0.

SinceT∗T^{*}is invertible, andZ0(k)∼PZ_{0}^{(k)}\sim Pis a nondegenerate Gaussian distribution on the full space for everykk, it follows thatUk−I→0U_{k}-I\to 0, i.e.Uk→IU_{k}\to I. Therefore,dist​(Spec​(Uk),−1)→2\mathrm{dist}(\mathrm{Spec}(U_{k}),-1)\to 2, contradiction!

## F.1Projection Estimates Along the Interpolation

The proof of Theorem4is based on testing the solenoidal
part ofvtv_{t}against a transported initial divergence-free field. The next
lemma shows that this test field remains divergence-free under the
interpolation and that its pairing withvtv_{t}is independent oftt.
To utilize the variational bound, we construct a specific test function in𝒮t{\mathcal{S}}_{t}.

## Lemma 10(Transported divergence-free fields).

Letu∈𝒮0u\in{\mathcal{S}}_{0}. Define the vector fieldhth_{t}onℝd\mathbb{R}^{d}byht​(x)=J​Tt​(Tt−1​(x))​u​(Tt−1​(x)).h_{t}(x)=JT_{t}(T_{t}^{-1}(x))\,u(T_{t}^{-1}(x)).

Thenht∈𝒮th_{t}\in{\mathcal{S}}_{t}.

## Proof of Lemma10.

Letg∈Cc∞​(ℝd)g\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})be an arbitrary smooth test function. Using the change of variablesy=Tt​(x)y=T_{t}(x)(noting thatptp_{t}is the pushforward ofppbyTtT_{t}), we compute:∫∇g​(y)⊤​ht​(y)​pt​(y)​dy\displaystyle\int\nabla g(y)^{\top}h_{t}(y)p_{t}(y)\mathrm{d}y=∫∇g(Tt(x))⊤JTt(x)u(x)p(x)dx=∫∇(g∘Tt)(x)⊤u(x)p(x)dx.\displaystyle=\int\nabla g(T_{t}(x))^{\top}JT_{t}(x)u(x)p(x)\mathrm{d}x=\int\nabla(g\circ T_{t})(x)^{\top}u(x)p(x)\mathrm{d}x.

Sinceu∈𝒮0u\in{\mathcal{S}}_{0}, it is orthogonal to gradients of scalar functions inL2​(P;ℝd)L^{2}(P;\mathbb{R}^{d}), so the integral vanishes. Thus,∇⋅(pt​ht)=0\nabla\cdot(p_{t}h_{t})=0in the weak sense.
∎

The following lemma identifies the𝒮0{\mathcal{S}}_{0}-projection of the field appearing in the
pairing withvtv_{t}.

## Lemma 11(Projection identity along the interpolation).

It holds thatproj𝒮0⁡(J​Tt⊤​(T−id))=proj𝒮0⁡(T−id)=proj𝒮0⁡(T).\operatorname{proj}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{0}}\left(JT_{t}^{\top}(T-\mathrm{id})\right)=\operatorname{proj}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{0}}(T-\mathrm{id})=\operatorname{proj}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{0}}(T).

## Proof of Lemma11.

Recall thatL2​(P;ℝd)L^{2}(P;\mathbb{R}^{d})admits the orthogonal decompositionL2​(P;ℝd)=𝒮0⊕GL^{2}(P;\mathbb{R}^{d})={\mathcal{S}}_{0}\oplus G, whereGGis the space of gradient fields. SinceTt=(1−t)​id+t​TT_{t}=(1-t)\mathrm{id}+tT, its Jacobian isJ​Tt=I+t​(J​T−I)JT_{t}=I+t(JT-I).

We observe thatJ​Tt​(x)⊤​(T​(x)−x)=(T​(x)−x)+t​(J​T​(x)−I)⊤​(T​(x)−x).JT_{t}(x)^{\top}(T(x)-x)=(T(x)-x)+t(JT(x)-I)^{\top}(T(x)-x).Since(J​T​(x)−I)⊤​(T​(x)−x)=12​∇‖T​(x)−x‖2(JT(x)-I)^{\top}(T(x)-x)=\frac{1}{2}\nabla\|T(x)-x\|^{2}, therefore,J​Tt⊤​(T−id)=(T−id)+∇(t2​‖T−id‖2).JT_{t}^{\top}(T-\mathrm{id})=(T-\mathrm{id})+\nabla\left(\frac{t}{2}\|T-\mathrm{id}\|^{2}\right).

The second term is a gradient field and thus lies in𝒮0⟂{\mathcal{S}}_{0}^{\perp}. Consequently,proj𝒮0⁡(J​Tt⊤​(T−id))=proj𝒮0⁡(T−id)=proj𝒮0⁡(T)−proj𝒮0⁡(id).\operatorname{proj}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{0}}(JT_{t}^{\top}(T-\mathrm{id}))=\operatorname{proj}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{0}}(T-\mathrm{id})=\operatorname{proj}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{0}}(T)-\operatorname{proj}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{0}}(\mathrm{id}).

Sinceid=∇(12​‖x‖2)\mathrm{id}=\nabla(\frac{1}{2}\|x\|^{2})is a gradient,proj𝒮0⁡(id)=0\operatorname{proj}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{0}}(\mathrm{id})=0, completing the proof.
∎

## Lemma 12(Transport Stability).

Under Assumption2, the difference in quadratic costs satisfies:𝔼​‖T​(X0)−X0‖2−W22​(P,Q)≤1λ​‖T−T∗‖P2.\mathbb{E}\left\|T(X_{0})-X_{0}\right\|^{2}-W_{2}^{2}(P,Q)\leq\frac{1}{\lambda}\|T-T^{*}\|_{P}^{2}.

## Proof of Lemma12.

We expand the squared norm‖T​(X0)−X0‖2=‖T​(X0)‖2+‖X0‖2−2​⟨T​(X0),X0⟩\|T(X_{0})-X_{0}\|^{2}=\|T(X_{0})\|^{2}+\|X_{0}\|^{2}-2\langle T(X_{0}),X_{0}\rangle. Noting that‖T​(X0)‖2\|T(X_{0})\|^{2}and‖T∗​(X0)‖2\|T^{*}(X_{0})\|^{2}have the same expectation (the second moment ofQQ), the difference in costs becomes:𝔼​‖T​(X0)−X0‖2−𝔼​‖T∗​(X0)−X0‖2=2​𝔼​⟨T∗​(X0)−T​(X0),X0⟩.\mathbb{E}\|T(X_{0})-X_{0}\|^{2}-\mathbb{E}\|T^{*}(X_{0})-X_{0}\|^{2}=2\mathbb{E}\langle T^{*}(X_{0})-T(X_{0}),X_{0}\rangle.

We substituteX0=∇φ0∗​(T∗​(X0))X_{0}=\nabla\varphi_{0}^{*}(T^{*}(X_{0}))using convex duality. Since𝔼​[φ0∗​(T​(X0))]=𝔼​[φ0∗​(T∗​(X0))]\mathbb{E}[\varphi_{0}^{*}(T(X_{0}))]=\mathbb{E}[\varphi_{0}^{*}(T^{*}(X_{0}))], we can rewrite the term on the right-hand side as a Bregman divergence:2​𝔼​⟨T∗​(X0)−T​(X0),∇φ0∗​(T∗​(X0))⟩=2​𝔼​[Dφ0∗​(T​(X0),T∗​(X0))].2\mathbb{E}\langle T^{*}(X_{0})-T(X_{0}),\nabla\varphi_{0}^{*}(T^{*}(X_{0}))\rangle=2\mathbb{E}\left[D_{\varphi_{0}^{*}}(T(X_{0}),T^{*}(X_{0}))\right].

By Assumption2,φ0\varphi_{0}isλ\lambda-strongly convex, which impliesφ0∗\varphi_{0}^{*}is(1/λ)(1/\lambda)-smooth. This smoothness yields the boundDφ0∗​(y,z)≤12​λ​‖y−z‖2D_{\varphi_{0}^{*}}(y,z)\leq\frac{1}{2\lambda}\|y-z\|^{2}. Therefore,𝔼​‖T​(X0)−X0‖2−W22​(P,Q)≤1λ​𝔼​‖T​(X0)−T∗​(X0)‖2,\mathbb{E}\|T(X_{0})-X_{0}\|^{2}-W_{2}^{2}(P,Q)\leq\frac{1}{\lambda}\mathbb{E}\left\|T(X_{0})-T^{*}(X_{0})\right\|^{2},

which concludes the proof.

## F.2Proof of the Contraction Criterion

Now we prove Theorem4and its contraction corollary.

## Proof of Theorem4.

Using thec-rectified flow variational identity (23) and Jensen’s inequality, the reduction in cost is bounded by the integrated projection of the velocity field:𝔼​‖T​(X0)−X0‖2−𝔼​‖Z1−Z0‖2\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\|T(X_{0})-X_{0}\|^{2}-\mathbb{E}\|Z_{1}-Z_{0}\|^{2}≥inff∫01𝔼∥X˙s−∇fs(Xs)∥2ds≥inff∫01𝔼∥𝔼[X˙s|Xs]−∇fs(Xs)∥2ds\displaystyle\geq\inf_{f}\int_{0}^{1}\mathbb{E}\|\dot{X}_{s}-\nabla f_{s}(X_{s})\|^{2}\mathrm{d}s\geq\inf_{f}\int_{0}^{1}\mathbb{E}\|\mathbb{E}[\dot{X}_{s}|X_{s}]-\nabla f_{s}(X_{s})\|^{2}\mathrm{d}s=inff∫01‖vs−∇fs‖Ps2​ds=∫01‖vs−projGs⁡vs‖Ps2​ds\displaystyle=\inf_{f}\int_{0}^{1}\|v_{s}-\nabla f_{s}\|_{P_{s}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s=\int_{0}^{1}\|v_{s}-\operatorname{proj}_{G_{s}}v_{s}\|_{P_{s}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s≥∫0(2+2​L)−1‖vs−projSs⟂⁡vs‖Ps2​ds.\displaystyle\geq\int_{0}^{(2+2L)^{-1}}\|v_{s}-\operatorname{proj}_{S_{s}^{\perp}}v_{s}\|_{P_{s}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s.

Letwt=proj𝒮t⁡vtw_{t}=\operatorname{proj}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{t}}v_{t}. By Lemma8, for any test functionht∈𝒮th_{t}\in{\mathcal{S}}_{t},‖wt‖Pt2≥⟨vt,ht⟩Pt2‖ht‖Pt2.\|w_{t}\|_{P_{t}}^{2}\geq\frac{\langle v_{t},h_{t}\rangle_{P_{t}}^{2}}{\|h_{t}\|_{P_{t}}^{2}}.

We choosehth_{t}as in Lemma10, settingu=proj𝒮0⁡(T)u=\operatorname{proj}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{0}}(T).

Using the change of variablesy=Tt​(x)y=T_{t}(x)and Lemma11, we compute the numerator:⟨vt,ht⟩Pt\displaystyle\langle v_{t},h_{t}\rangle_{P_{t}}=∫(vt∘Tt)⊤​(ht∘Tt)​p​(x)​dx=∫(T​(x)−x)⊤​J​Tt​(x)​u​(x)​p​(x)​dx\displaystyle=\int(v_{t}\circ T_{t})^{\top}(h_{t}\circ T_{t})\,p(x)\mathrm{d}x=\int(T(x)-x)^{\top}JT_{t}(x)u(x)\,p(x)\mathrm{d}x=⟨J​Tt⊤​(T−id),u⟩P=⟨proj𝒮0⁡(J​Tt⊤​(T−id)),u⟩P\displaystyle=\langle JT_{t}^{\top}(T-\mathrm{id}),u\rangle_{P}=\langle\operatorname{proj}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{0}}(JT_{t}^{\top}(T-\mathrm{id})),u\rangle_{P}=⟨proj𝒮0⁡(T),u⟩P=‖u‖P2.\displaystyle=\langle\operatorname{proj}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{0}}(T),u\rangle_{P}=\|u\|_{P}^{2}.

For the denominator, Assumption1implies‖J​T‖o​p≤L\|JT\|_{op}\leq L. The JacobianJ​Tt=(1−t)​I+t​J​TJT_{t}=(1-t)I+tJTtherefore satisfies‖J​Tt​(x)‖o​p≤2\|JT_{t}(x)\|_{op}\leq 2for allt≤(2​L+2)−1t\leq(2L+2)^{-1}. Consequently:‖ht‖Pt2=∫‖J​Tt​(x)​u​(x)‖2​p​(x)​dx≤4​‖u‖P2.\|h_{t}\|_{P_{t}}^{2}=\int\|JT_{t}(x)u(x)\|^{2}p(x)\mathrm{d}x\leq 4\|u\|_{P}^{2}.

Combining these estimates gives:‖wt‖Pt2≥(‖u‖P2)24​‖u‖P2=14​‖u‖P2.\|w_{t}\|_{P_{t}}^{2}\geq\frac{(\|u\|_{P}^{2})^{2}}{4\|u\|_{P}^{2}}=\frac{1}{4}\|u\|_{P}^{2}.

Recalling thatu=proj𝒮0⁡(T)=proj𝒮0⁡(T−T∗)u=\operatorname{proj}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{0}}(T)=\operatorname{proj}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{0}}(T-T^{*})(sinceT∗=∇φ⟂𝒮0T^{*}=\nabla\varphi\perp{\mathcal{S}}_{0}), and invoking Assumption3:‖wt‖Pt2≥h4​‖T−T∗‖P2.\|w_{t}\|_{P_{t}}^{2}\geq\frac{h}{4}\|T-T^{*}\|_{P}^{2}.

Integrating overt∈[0,(2+2​L)−1]t\in[0,(2+2L)^{-1}]yields𝔼​‖T​(X0)−X0‖2−𝔼​‖Z1−Z0‖2\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\|T(X_{0})-X_{0}\|^{2}-\mathbb{E}\|Z_{1}-Z_{0}\|^{2}≥∫0(2+2​L)−1‖wt‖Pt2​dt≥h8​(L+1)​‖T−T∗‖P2.\displaystyle\geq\int_{0}^{(2+2L)^{-1}}\|w_{t}\|_{P_{t}}^{2}\mathrm{d}t\geq\frac{h}{8(L+1)}\|T-T^{*}\|_{P}^{2}.

## Proof of Corollary2.

Theorem4provides the lower bound for the cost reduction:𝔼​‖T​(X0)−X0‖2−𝔼​‖Z1−Z0‖2≥h8​(L+1)​‖T−T∗‖P2.\mathbb{E}\left\|T(X_{0})-X_{0}\right\|^{2}-\mathbb{E}\left\|Z_{1}-Z_{0}\right\|^{2}\geq\frac{h}{8(L+1)}\|T-T^{*}\|_{P}^{2}.

By Lemma12, we have‖T−T∗‖P2≥λ​(𝔼​‖T​(X0)−X0‖2−W22​(P,Q))\|T-T^{*}\|_{P}^{2}\geq\lambda\left(\mathbb{E}\|T(X_{0})-X_{0}\|^{2}-W_{2}^{2}(P,Q)\right).
Substituting this into the inequality yields:𝔼​‖T​(X0)−X0‖2−𝔼​‖Z1−Z0‖2≥λ​h8​(L+1)​(𝔼​‖T​(X0)−X0‖2−W22​(P,Q)).\mathbb{E}\left\|T(X_{0})-X_{0}\right\|^{2}-\mathbb{E}\left\|Z_{1}-Z_{0}\right\|^{2}\geq\frac{\lambda h}{8(L+1)}\left(\mathbb{E}\left\|T(X_{0})-X_{0}\right\|^{2}-W_{2}^{2}(P,Q)\right).

Rearranging the terms completes the proof.
∎

## Proof of Proposition4.

SinceSεS_{\varepsilon}isPP-measure preserving andT#∗​P=QT^{*}_{\#}P=Q, we have(Tε)#​P=(T∗∘Sε)#​P=T#∗​((Sε)#​P)=T#∗​P=Q.(T_{\varepsilon})_{\#}P=(T^{*}\circ S_{\varepsilon})_{\#}P=T^{*}_{\#}((S_{\varepsilon})_{\#}P)=T^{*}_{\#}P=Q.

We first show that the infinitesimal perturbationuulies in𝒮0{\mathcal{S}}_{0}. Letg∈Cc∞​(ℝd)g\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d}). SinceSεS_{\varepsilon}preservesPP,∫g​(Sε​(x))​p​(x)​dx=∫g​(x)​p​(x)​dx\int g(S_{\varepsilon}(x))\,p(x)\mathrm{d}x=\int g(x)\,p(x)\mathrm{d}xfor every sufficiently smallε\varepsilon. Differentiating atε=0\varepsilon=0, using(Sε−id)/ε→u(S_{\varepsilon}-\mathrm{id})/\varepsilon\to uinL2​(P;ℝd)L^{2}(P;\mathbb{R}^{d}), gives∫∇g​(x)⊤​u​(x)​p​(x)​dx=0.\int\nabla g(x)^{\top}u(x)\,p(x)\mathrm{d}x=0.

Thusu∈𝒮0u\in{\mathcal{S}}_{0}.
By Taylor’s formula,Tε​(x)−T∗​(x)ε=∫01∇2φ​(x+s​(Sε​(x)−x))​ds⋅Sε​(x)−xε.\frac{T_{\varepsilon}(x)-T^{*}(x)}{\varepsilon}=\int_{0}^{1}\nabla^{2}\varphi\bigl(x+s(S_{\varepsilon}(x)-x)\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}s\cdot\frac{S_{\varepsilon}(x)-x}{\varepsilon}.

we also haveproj𝒮0⁡(Tε−T∗)/ε⟶proj𝒮0⁡(∇2φ0⋅u){\operatorname{proj}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{0}}(T_{\varepsilon}-T^{*})}/{\varepsilon}\longrightarrow\operatorname{proj}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{0}}(\nabla^{2}\varphi_{0}\cdot u)inL2​(P;ℝd)L^{2}(P;\mathbb{R}^{d}).

and‖∇2φ0⋅u‖P≤Λ​‖u‖P\|\nabla^{2}\varphi_{0}\cdot u\|_{P}\leq\Lambda\|u\|_{P}.
This implies‖proj𝒮0⁡(∇2φ0⋅u)‖P‖∇2φ0⋅u‖P≥λΛ.\frac{\|\operatorname{proj}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{0}}(\nabla^{2}\varphi_{0}\cdot u)\|_{P}}{\|\nabla^{2}\varphi_{0}\cdot u\|_{P}}\geq\frac{\lambda}{\Lambda}.

## Proof of Theorem5.

Define the excess cost at thekk-th step byℰk:=𝔼​‖Z1(k)−Z0(k)‖2−W22​(P,Q)\mathcal{E}_{k}:=\mathbb{E}\left\|Z_{1}^{(k)}-Z_{0}^{(k)}\right\|^{2}-W_{2}^{2}(P,Q).
SinceW22​(P,Q)W_{2}^{2}(P,Q)is the optimal quadratic transport cost betweenPPandQQ, we haveℰk≥0\mathcal{E}_{k}\geq 0for everyk≥0k\geq 0.
Sincec-rectified flow reduces the cost,ℰ1≤ℰ0\mathcal{E}_{1}\leq\mathcal{E}_{0}.

After the first round ofc-rectified flow, it induces a transport mapT1T_{1}, and in each following runs, thec-rectified flow always induces a new transport map. Therefore, we can use our one-step contraction results after the first step.
By the uniform assumptions, Corollary2applies to each
coupling(Z0(k),Z1(k))(Z_{0}^{(k)},Z_{1}^{(k)}), withTTreplaced byTkT_{k}. Therefore,
for everyk≥1k\geq 1,ℰk+1≤(1−λ​h8​(L+1))​ℰk\mathcal{E}_{k+1}\leq\left(1-\frac{\lambda h}{8(L+1)}\right)\mathcal{E}_{k}.
Iterating this inequality givesℰk≤(1−λ​h8​(L+1))k−1​ℰ1≤(1−λ​h8​(L+1))k−1​ℰ0≤exp⁡(−λ​h8​(L+1)​(k−1))​ℰ0.\mathcal{E}_{k}\leq\left(1-\frac{\lambda h}{8(L+1)}\right)^{k-1}\mathcal{E}_{1}\leq\left(1-\frac{\lambda h}{8(L+1)}\right)^{k-1}\mathcal{E}_{0}\leq\exp{\left(-\frac{\lambda h}{8(L+1)}(k-1)\right)}\mathcal{E}_{0}.

Substituting the definition ofℰ0\mathcal{E}_{0}yields the stated exponential
decay estimate.

## Proof of Lemma3.

Recall that forPP-a.s.xx, and for ally∈ℝdy\in\mathbb{R}^{d},c​(y−x)−ψ​(y)−φ​(x)≥0c(y-x)-\psi(y)-\varphi(x)\geq 0,

Now, becauseT#​P=QT_{\#}P=Qand(T∗)#​P=Q(T^{*})_{\#}P=Q,𝔼​[ψ​(T​(X))]=𝔼​[ψ​(T∗​(X))]\mathbb{E}[\psi(T(X))]=\mathbb{E}[\psi(T^{*}(X))].
Therefore,𝔼​[c​(T​(X)−X)−c​(T∗​(X)−X)]\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[c(T(X)-X)-c(T^{*}(X)-X)]=𝔼​[c​(T​(X)−X)−ψ​(T​(X))−c​(T∗​(X)−X)+ψ​(T∗​(X))]\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Big[c(T(X)-X)-\psi(T(X))-c(T^{*}(X)-X)+\psi(T^{*}(X))\Big]=𝔼​[D​(X,T​(X))−D​(X,T∗​(X))].\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[D(X,T(X))-D(X,T^{*}(X))].

We next bound the last expression. For each fixedxx, the Hessian ofDDwith respect toyyis∇y2D​(x,y)=∇2c​(y−x)−∇2ψ​(y)⪯Ly​Id\nabla_{y}^{2}D(x,y)=\nabla^{2}c(y-x)-\nabla^{2}\psi(y)\preceq L_{y}I_{d}.
HenceDDisLyL_{y}-smooth inyy. Therefore,D​(x,T​(x))−D​(x,T∗​(x))≤Ly2​‖T​(x)−T∗​(x)‖2.D(x,T(x))-D(x,T^{*}(x))\leq\frac{L_{y}}{2}\|T(x)-T^{*}(x)\|^{2}.

Taking expectation with respect toPP, we get𝔼​[c​(T​(X)−X)]−𝔼​[c​(T∗​(X)−X)]≤Ly2​‖T−T∗‖P2.\mathbb{E}[c(T(X)-X)]-\mathbb{E}[c(T^{*}(X)-X)]\leq\frac{L_{y}}{2}\|T-T^{*}\|_{P}^{2}.(33)

To relate the transport distance to the gradient difference, we invoke the strong convexity ofcc. Sinceccisμc\mu_{c}-strongly convex, its gradient∇c\nabla cis strictly monotone:⟨∇c​(u)−∇c​(v),u−v⟩≥μc​‖u−v‖2,∀u,v∈ℝd.\langle\nabla c(u)-\nabla c(v),u-v\rangle\geq\mu_{c}\|u-v\|^{2},\quad\forall u,v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

Applying the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, we have⟨∇c​(u)−∇c​(v),u−v⟩≤‖∇c​(u)−∇c​(v)‖​‖u−v‖\langle\nabla c(u)-\nabla c(v),u-v\rangle\leq\|\nabla c(u)-\nabla c(v)\|\|u-v\|. Combining these inequalities yieldsμc​‖u−v‖≤‖∇c​(u)−∇c​(v)‖\mu_{c}\|u-v\|\leq\|\nabla c(u)-\nabla c(v)\|.
Lettingu=T​(x)−xu=T(x)-xandv=T∗​(x)−xv=T^{*}(x)-x,‖T−T∗‖P2≤1μc2​‖∇c​(T−id)−∇c​(T∗−id)‖P2.\|T-T^{*}\|_{P}^{2}\leq\frac{1}{\mu_{c}^{2}}\|\nabla c(T-\mathrm{id})-\nabla c(T^{*}-\mathrm{id})\|_{P}^{2}.(34)

Substituting (34) into (33) completes the proof.

We begin with the multivariate low-noise estimator. For a multi-indexν=(ν1,…,νd)\nu=(\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{d}), write|ν|=∑jνj|\nu|=\sum_{j}\nu_{j},uν=∏jujνju^{\nu}=\prod_{j}u_{j}^{\nu_{j}}, andDν=∏j∂jνjD^{\nu}=\prod_{j}\partial_{j}^{\nu_{j}}.

## Proposition 8(Multivariate weighted ratio derivative estimation).

There existsb0>0b_{0}>0, depending only on(c,C,L,α,d)(c,C,L,\alpha,d), for which one can construct estimatorsr^b,0\hat{r}_{b,0}andr^b,1\hat{r}_{b,1}such that, for every0<b≤b00<b\leq b_{0},supf∈ℱα,d𝔼f​∫ℝd|r^b,0​(x)−r​(x)|2​φd​(x)​dx≲b2​α+1n​bd,\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{\alpha,d}}\mathbb{E}_{f}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\hat{r}_{b,0}(x)-r(x)|^{2}\varphi_{d}(x)\mathrm{d}x\lesssim b^{2\alpha}+\frac{1}{nb^{d}},

andsupf∈ℱα,d𝔼f​∫ℝd‖r^b,1​(x)−∇r​(x)‖2​φd​(x)​dx≲b2​(α−1)+1n​bd+2.\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{\alpha,d}}\mathbb{E}_{f}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\|\hat{r}_{b,1}(x)-\nabla r(x)\|^{2}\varphi_{d}(x)\mathrm{d}x\lesssim b^{2(\alpha-1)}+\frac{1}{nb^{d+2}}.

## Proof.

Letp=mαp=m_{\alpha}, and letℐp={ν∈ℕ0d:|ν|≤p}\mathcal{I}_{p}=\{\nu\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}:|\nu|\leq p\}. Choose a
nonnegativeC∞C^{\infty}kernelKK, supported on the unit ball and bounded
from below by a positive constant on the ball of radius1/21/2. PutEb={x:‖x‖≤b−1/3},ℓb​(x)=b​(1+‖x‖)2.E_{b}=\{x:\|x\|\leq b^{-1/3}\},\qquad\ell_{b}(x)=b(1+\|x\|)^{2}.

## Lemma 14(Multivariate Gaussian second moments).

Let0<t≤10<t\leq 1,σ=t\sigma=\sqrt{t}, andY∼φdY\sim\varphi_{d}. Thenγt​(x)​𝔼​{φt(d)​(x−Y)γt​(x)}2≲σ−d​e−c0​σ2​‖x‖2,\gamma_{t}(x)\mathbb{E}\left\{\frac{\varphi_{t}^{(d)}(x-Y)}{\gamma_{t}(x)}\right\}^{2}\lesssim\sigma^{-d}e^{-c_{0}\sigma^{2}\|x\|^{2}},

andγt​(x)​𝔼​‖∇x{φt(d)​(x−Y)γt​(x)}‖2≲σ−d​(σ−2+‖x‖2)​e−c0​σ2​‖x‖2,\gamma_{t}(x)\mathbb{E}\left\|\nabla_{x}\left\{\frac{\varphi_{t}^{(d)}(x-Y)}{\gamma_{t}(x)}\right\}\right\|^{2}\lesssim\sigma^{-d}(\sigma^{-2}+\|x\|^{2})e^{-c_{0}\sigma^{2}\|x\|^{2}},

wherec0>0c_{0}>0is universal.

## Proof.

Completing the square coordinatewise gives∫φt(d)​(x−y)2​φd​(y)​dy≲σ−d​γt​(x)​e−c0​σ2​‖x‖2.\int\varphi_{t}^{(d)}(x-y)^{2}\varphi_{d}(y)\mathrm{d}y\lesssim\sigma^{-d}\gamma_{t}(x)e^{-c_{0}\sigma^{2}\|x\|^{2}}.

Moreover,∇x{φt(d)​(x−y)γt​(x)}=1t​(y−x1+t)​φt(d)​(x−y)γt​(x).\nabla_{x}\left\{\frac{\varphi_{t}^{(d)}(x-y)}{\gamma_{t}(x)}\right\}=\frac{1}{t}\left(y-\frac{x}{1+t}\right)\frac{\varphi_{t}^{(d)}(x-y)}{\gamma_{t}(x)}.

A second coordinatewise Gaussian calculation gives the stated derivative
bound.
∎

The following local polynomial is fitted on an inward set so that the
Gaussian reference density is never smaller than at the point of estimation.

## Lemma 15(Inward multivariate local-polynomial ratio estimation).

LetY1,…,YnY_{1},\ldots,Y_{n}be i.i.d. with densityR​γtR\gamma_{t}, where0<t≤10<t\leq 1,c≤R≤Cc\leq R\leq C, and‖R‖Cα​(ℝd)≤L\|R\|_{C^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq L. There exist estimatorsR^t,0lp​(x)\hat{R}_{t,0}^{\rm lp}(x)andR^t,1lp​(x)\hat{R}_{t,1}^{\rm lp}(x), defined for‖x‖≥1\|x\|\geq 1, such that𝔼​|R^t,0lp​(x)−R​(x)|2≲{n​γt​(x)}−2​α2​α+d∧1,\mathbb{E}|\hat{R}_{t,0}^{\rm lp}(x)-R(x)|^{2}\lesssim\{n\gamma_{t}(x)\}^{-\frac{2\alpha}{2\alpha+d}}\wedge 1,

and𝔼​‖R^t,1lp​(x)−∇R​(x)‖2≲{n​γt​(x)}−2​(α−1)2​α+d∧1.\mathbb{E}\|\hat{R}_{t,1}^{\rm lp}(x)-\nabla R(x)\|^{2}\lesssim\{n\gamma_{t}(x)\}^{-\frac{2(\alpha-1)}{2\alpha+d}}\wedge 1.

## Proof.

Letp=mαp=m_{\alpha}, and fix the open ball𝒰=B​(e1/2,1/8)\mathcal{U}=B(e_{1}/2,1/8). Since restriction to𝒰\mathcal{U}defines a
positive-definite inner product on the finite-dimensional space of
polynomials of degree at mostpp, there exist bounded functionsL0,L1,…,LdL_{0},L_{1},\ldots,L_{d}, supported on𝒰\mathcal{U}, such that, for every
polynomialPPof degree at mostpp,∫𝒰L0​(u)​P​(u)​du=P​(0),∫𝒰Lj​(u)​P​(u)​du=∂jP​(0),1≤j≤d.\int_{\mathcal{U}}L_{0}(u)P(u)\mathrm{d}u=P(0),\qquad\int_{\mathcal{U}}L_{j}(u)P(u)\mathrm{d}u=\partial_{j}P(0),\quad 1\leq j\leq d.

WriteL∇=(L1,…,Ld)⊤L_{\nabla}=(L_{1},\ldots,L_{d})^{\top}. Fix a Borel measurable choice of
an orthogonal matrixQxQ_{x}satisfyingQx​e1=x/‖x‖Q_{x}e_{1}=x/\|x\|,x≠0x\neq 0.

Ifn​γt​(x)<1n\gamma_{t}(x)<1, setR^t,0lp​(x)=1\hat{R}_{t,0}^{\rm lp}(x)=1andR^t,1lp​(x)=0\hat{R}_{t,1}^{\rm lp}(x)=0. Otherwise putbx={n​γt​(x)}−1/(2​α+d)≤1,Ui=Qx⊤​(x−Yi)bx,b_{x}=\{n\gamma_{t}(x)\}^{-1/(2\alpha+d)}\leq 1,\qquad U_{i}=\frac{Q_{x}^{\top}(x-Y_{i})}{b_{x}},

and defineR^t,0lp​(x)=1n​bxd​∑i=1nL0​(Ui)​𝟏{Ui∈𝒰}γt​(Yi),\hat{R}_{t,0}^{\rm lp}(x)=\frac{1}{nb_{x}^{d}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{L_{0}(U_{i})\mathbf{1}_{\{U_{i}\in\mathcal{U}\}}}{\gamma_{t}(Y_{i})},R^t,1lp​(x)=−Qxn​bxd+1​∑i=1nL∇​(Ui)​𝟏{Ui∈𝒰}γt​(Yi).\hat{R}_{t,1}^{\rm lp}(x)=-\frac{Q_{x}}{nb_{x}^{d+1}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{L_{\nabla}(U_{i})\mathbf{1}_{\{U_{i}\in\mathcal{U}\}}}{\gamma_{t}(Y_{i})}.

Foru∈𝒰u\in\mathcal{U},‖x‖≥1\|x\|\geq 1, and0<bx≤10<b_{x}\leq 1,‖x−bx​Qx​u‖2=‖x‖2−2​bx​‖x‖​u1+bx2​‖u‖2≤‖x‖2.\|x-b_{x}Q_{x}u\|^{2}=\|x\|^{2}-2b_{x}\|x\|u_{1}+b_{x}^{2}\|u\|^{2}\leq\|x\|^{2}.

Henceγt​(x−bx​Qx​u)≥γt​(x)\gamma_{t}(x-b_{x}Q_{x}u)\geq\gamma_{t}(x).

The reproduction identities and the multivariate Taylor remainder give|𝔼​R^t,0lp​(x)−R​(x)|≲bxα,‖𝔼​R^t,1lp​(x)−∇R​(x)‖≲bxα−1.|\mathbb{E}\hat{R}_{t,0}^{\rm lp}(x)-R(x)|\lesssim b_{x}^{\alpha},\qquad\|\mathbb{E}\hat{R}_{t,1}^{\rm lp}(x)-\nabla R(x)\|\lesssim b_{x}^{\alpha-1}.

UsingR≤CR\leq C, the inward inequality above, and the change of variablesy=x−bx​Qx​uy=x-b_{x}Q_{x}u,Var⁡(R^t,0lp​(x))≲1n​γt​(x)​bxd,𝔼​‖R^t,1lp​(x)−𝔼​R^t,1lp​(x)‖2≲1n​γt​(x)​bxd+2.\operatorname{Var}(\hat{R}_{t,0}^{\rm lp}(x))\lesssim\frac{1}{n\gamma_{t}(x)b_{x}^{d}},\qquad\mathbb{E}\|\hat{R}_{t,1}^{\rm lp}(x)-\mathbb{E}\hat{R}_{t,1}^{\rm lp}(x)\|^{2}\lesssim\frac{1}{n\gamma_{t}(x)b_{x}^{d+2}}.

Consequently, for every measurableE⊂{x:‖x‖≥1}E\subset\{x:\|x\|\geq 1\}, withD=2​α+dD=2\alpha+d,𝔼​∫E‖R^t,1lp−∇R‖2​γt≲n−2​(α−1)D​∫Eγtd+2D,\mathbb{E}\int_{E}\|\hat{R}_{t,1}^{\rm lp}-\nabla R\|^{2}\gamma_{t}\lesssim n^{-\frac{2(\alpha-1)}{D}}\int_{E}\gamma_{t}^{\frac{d+2}{D}},(42)

and𝔼​∫E|R^t,0lp−R|2​γt≲n−2​αD​∫EγtdD.\mathbb{E}\int_{E}|\hat{R}_{t,0}^{\rm lp}-R|^{2}\gamma_{t}\lesssim n^{-\frac{2\alpha}{D}}\int_{E}\gamma_{t}^{\frac{d}{D}}.(43)

We finally record the exact global Gaussian calculation.

## Lemma 16(Multivariate global plug-in bound).

DefineR^t,0G​(x)=n−1​∑i=1nφt(d)​(x−Xi)γt​(x),R^t,1G=∇R^t,0G,\hat{R}_{t,0}^{G}(x)=\frac{n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varphi_{t}^{(d)}(x-X_{i})}{\gamma_{t}(x)},\qquad\hat{R}_{t,1}^{G}=\nabla\hat{R}_{t,0}^{G},

and lets^tG\hat{s}_{t}^{G}be the corresponding clipped ratio score estimator.
Then, uniformly overt>0t>0,supf∈ℱα,d𝔼f​∫ℝd‖s^tG−st‖2​pt≲(1+t)d−1n​td+1.\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{\alpha,d}}\mathbb{E}_{f}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\|\hat{s}_{t}^{G}-s_{t}\|^{2}p_{t}\lesssim\frac{(1+t)^{d-1}}{nt^{d+1}}.

In particular, the right-hand side is of order1/(n​td+1)1/(nt^{d+1})for0<t≤10<t\leq 1and of order1/(n​t2)1/(nt^{2})fort>1t>1.

## Proof.

LetKt​(x,y)=φt(d)​(x−y)γt​(x)K_{t}(x,y)=\frac{\varphi_{t}^{(d)}(x-y)}{\gamma_{t}(x)}.
By tensorization of the corresponding one-dimensional identities,∬Kt​(x,y)2​γt​(x)​φd​(y)​dx​dy=(1+tt)d.\iint K_{t}(x,y)^{2}\gamma_{t}(x)\varphi_{d}(y)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y=\left(\frac{1+t}{t}\right)^{d}.

Also,∇xKt​(x,y)=1t​(y−x1+t)​Kt​(x,y),\nabla_{x}K_{t}(x,y)=\frac{1}{t}\left(y-\frac{x}{1+t}\right)K_{t}(x,y),

and a direct Gaussian calculation yields∬‖∇xKt​(x,y)‖2​γt​(x)​φd​(y)​dx​dy=d​(1+t)d−1td+1.\iint\|\nabla_{x}K_{t}(x,y)\|^{2}\gamma_{t}(x)\varphi_{d}(y)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y=d\frac{(1+t)^{d-1}}{t^{d+1}}.(44)

Sincef≤C​φdf\leq C\varphi_{d}, these identities imply𝔼f​∫|R^t,0G−Rt|2​γt≲1n​(1+tt)d,𝔼f​∫‖R^t,1G−∇Rt‖2​γt≲(1+t)d−1n​td+1.\mathbb{E}_{f}\int|\hat{R}_{t,0}^{G}-R_{t}|^{2}\gamma_{t}\lesssim\frac{1}{n}\left(\frac{1+t}{t}\right)^{d},\qquad\mathbb{E}_{f}\int\|\hat{R}_{t,1}^{G}-\nabla R_{t}\|^{2}\gamma_{t}\lesssim\frac{(1+t)^{d-1}}{nt^{d+1}}.

By (37), the denominator-estimation contribution in
Lemma13is bounded by1(1+t)2​n​(1+tt)d≲(1+t)d−1n​td+1.\frac{1}{(1+t)^{2}n}\left(\frac{1+t}{t}\right)^{d}\lesssim\frac{(1+t)^{d-1}}{nt^{d+1}}.

The result follows.
∎

## Proof of the upper bound in
Theorem6.

The low-noise estimator (41) supplies the first branch
of (10). It remains to obtain the second branch
onhn2≤t≤1h_{n}^{2}\leq t\leq 1. Putσ=t,Λ=1∨log+⁡(n​tα+d/2)\sigma=\sqrt{t},\ \Lambda=1\vee\sqrt{\log_{+}(nt^{\alpha+d/2})}.
ThenN≥1N\geq 1. Fix a sufficiently small constantη>0\eta>0. Ifσ​Λ≥η\sigma\Lambda\geq\eta, the global plug-in estimator of
Lemma16gives1n​σ2​d+2≤η−d​Λdn​σd+2,\frac{1}{n\sigma^{2d+2}}\leq\eta^{-d}\frac{\Lambda^{d}}{n\sigma^{d+2}},

so it attains, up to a constant, the smaller of the intermediate and global
plug-in branches.

It remains to considerσ​Λ<η\sigma\Lambda<\eta. Choose a sufficiently large constantMM,
and then takeη≤(2​M)−1\eta\leq(2M)^{-1}. Generate independentZi∼N​(0,Id)Z_{i}\sim N(0,I_{d})and putYi=Xi+σ​ZiY_{i}=X_{i}+\sigma Z_{i}, so thatYi∼pt=Rt​γtY_{i}\sim p_{t}=R_{t}\gamma_{t}. On the ball{x:‖x‖≤M​Λ}\{x:\|x\|\leq M\Lambda\}, use the Gaussian plug-in estimatorsR^t,0G\hat{R}_{t,0}^{G}andR^t,1G\hat{R}_{t,1}^{G}. Outside this ball, apply
Lemma15toY1,…,YnY_{1},\ldots,Y_{n}. SinceM​Λ≤(2​σ)−1M\Lambda\leq(2\sigma)^{-1}, Lemma14gives𝔼f​∫‖x‖≤M​Λ‖R^t,1G−∇Rt‖2​γt≲Λdn​σd+2,𝔼f​∫‖x‖≤M​Λ|R^t,0G−Rt|2​γt≲Λdn​σd≤Λdn​σd+2.\mathbb{E}_{f}\int_{\|x\|\leq M\Lambda}\|\hat{R}_{t,1}^{G}-\nabla R_{t}\|^{2}\gamma_{t}\lesssim\frac{\Lambda^{d}}{n\sigma^{d+2}},\qquad\mathbb{E}_{f}\int_{\|x\|\leq M\Lambda}|\hat{R}_{t,0}^{G}-R_{t}|^{2}\gamma_{t}\lesssim\frac{\Lambda^{d}}{n\sigma^{d}}\leq\frac{\Lambda^{d}}{n\sigma^{d+2}}.

The representationRt=𝒜t​rR_{t}=\mathcal{A}_{t}r, together withDν​Rt=(1+t)−|ν|​𝒜t​(Dν​r)D^{\nu}R_{t}=(1+t)^{-|\nu|}\mathcal{A}_{t}(D^{\nu}r)for|ν|≤mα|\nu|\leq m_{\alpha}and the corresponding Hölder contraction, implies‖Rt‖Cα​(ℝd)≲1\|R_{t}\|_{C^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\lesssim 1uniformly over0<t≤10<t\leq 1.
Thus Lemma15applies in the complement.

Leta1=(d+2)/Da_{1}=(d+2)/Danda0=d/Da_{0}=d/D. Uniformly over0<t≤10<t\leq 1, Gaussian
tails imply, after increasingMMif necessary,∫‖x‖>M​Λγt​(x)aj​dx≲{N−aj,N≥e,1,1≤N<e,j∈{0,1}.\int_{\|x\|>M\Lambda}\gamma_{t}(x)^{a_{j}}\mathrm{d}x\lesssim\begin{cases}N^{-a_{j}},&N\geq e,\\
1,&1\leq N<e,\end{cases}\qquad j\in\{0,1\}.

Equations (42) and
(43) show that the two tail risks are
also bounded byΛd/(n​σd+2)\Lambda^{d}/(n\sigma^{d+2}). Combining the central and
tail estimators and applying Lemma13givessupf∈ℱα,d𝔼f​∫‖s^tim−st‖2​pt≲Λdn​σd+2.\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{\alpha,d}}\mathbb{E}_{f}\int\|\hat{s}_{t}^{\rm im}-s_{t}\|^{2}p_{t}\lesssim\frac{\Lambda^{d}}{n\sigma^{d+2}}.

The auxiliary Gaussian randomization can be removed by taking the conditional
expectation of the resulting score estimator givenX1,…,XnX_{1},\ldots,X_{n}, which does not increase squared risk.

Taking the best of the low-noise, intermediate-noise, and global plug-in
estimators gives (10) for0<t≤10<t\leq 1. Fort>1t>1, Lemma16gives1/(n​t2)1/(nt^{2}).
